Andrew Skurka on How to Prevent and Treat Blisters

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

This article is adapted from Andrew Skurka’s video for The Trek’s YouTube channel, part of a weekly series. It has been edited and condensed for clarity and readability.

Blisters are a fact of life for backpackers, and at their worst, they can totally shut down trips.

I know a thing or two about blisters. I used to be an avid thru-hiker. I’ve walked about 30,000 miles, including the Sea-to-Sea Route (C2C), the Great Western Loop, and the Alaska-Yukon Expedition. For the last 16 years, I’ve also been running a guided backpacking service, so I’ve been taking care of my feet and other people’s feet as well.

When it comes to blisters, an ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure. Here are five things you can do before leaving home.

Start testing your footwear on low-risk outings. By footwear, I mean both your shoes and your socks. They need to work together for your feet and the conditions in which you’ll be hiking.

Start with a short hike, build up to a full day, and then try a one-night trip. Once you’re confident that a particular combination works, take it on longer backpacking trips.

Once you find shoes and socks that work for you, buy extra pairs if you can. Six months or a year from now, the manufacturer may have changed the fit, fabrics, outsole, or padding.

In my case, I’ve used DeFeet Wooleator socks for 25 years. When I learned they were no longer being made, I bought eight new pairs. I believe those were the only size larges left on the entire internet. That should give me another two or three years before I need to find new socks.

I’ve also been a longtime user of the La Sportiva Bushido. It’s changed a little over time, but it works great for me. That’s one less thing to think about before a trip.

Go get a pedicure if you want, or grab your toenail clippers after a shower. Get rid of the extra length so your toenails don’t bang into the front of your shoes.

The places I most often see calluses are along the side of the big toe and around the rim of the heel. I also see people develop blisters underneath those calluses.

My approach is to carefully remove excess callused skin with a callus remover before a trip. I’m trying to keep that thick patch of skin from moving as one piece. Don’t do this the night before you leave; give your feet time to readjust.

If you know you always get blisters in a particular spot, preemptively tape that area. You’re better off protecting it at home than dealing with a blister once you’re on the trail.

Once you’re in the field, keep up the prevention routine. Get your feet warm and dry at night; I cover this in more detail in 6 Tips for Dry, Healthy Feet.

During the day, take shoeless breaks. If you’re stopping for longer than about 15 minutes, take off your shoes and socks and let some fresh air circulate around your feet.

Inspect your feet regularly, too. I’m often surprised by how many people don’t discover a blister until they get into their tent at night. Their feet already feel tender from hiking all day, so they haven’t noticed a particular problem developing.

Address hot spots as soon as you feel them. A hot spot might feel warm, irritated, or like a pebble in your shoe that stays in the same place.

It’s tempting to think, “Camp is only an hour away. Everyone’s hungry and tired, and I don’t want to interrupt the group. I’ll deal with it there.” But in that hour, a minor hot spot can become a blister.

Do yourself and everyone in your group a favor: stop and take care of it. Once you have a blister, you’re trying to manage something that’s already sore and may get worse.

My blister supplies are part of my broader first aid, foot care, and repair kit.

If you’re a solo thru-hiker, the small scissors on something like a Victorinox Classic can work. If you’re responsible for a group, you’ll want something more substantial. I carry children’s school scissors: not quite trauma shears, but easier to use than tiny multitool scissors.

The cornerstone of my foot care kit is nonstretch Leukotape.

If you don’t need a whole roll, save the backing paper from a sheet of address labels. Run strips of Leukotape along that backing, then cut them out. You can cut patches to size, round the corners, and peel off the backing without touching the adhesive with your fingers.

I avoid wrapping Leukotape around trekking poles or other objects the way people often carry duct tape. In my experience, rolling it onto itself compromises the adhesive. The label backing makes it much easier to handle.

Leukotape is extremely sticky. Once it’s on your feet, it comes off on its own schedule. Be careful removing it, and don’t put the adhesive directly over a blister: it can pull off the blister’s top layer or the fragile skin beneath it.

I carry a balm, such as Badger Balm, to help reduce friction. I also rub a little onto the outside of Leukotape after applying it. The tape’s adhesive can work through the fabric and stick to a sock; the balm helps prevent that.

I don’t use duct tape for foot care very often, but it’s slippery. I sometimes consider it for friction-related problems when I’m not concerned about moisture collecting beneath it.

Gauze covers the blister itself so the tape doesn’t stick directly to it. Tincture of benzoin towelettes help the tape stick when feet are wet, conditions are humid, or the tape is going on a particularly high-stress area.

I carry a needle for sewing and, when I decide to drain a blister, for that purpose as well. Cleaning the instrument beforehand is essential.

The knife has a more drastic use. If someone has a heel blister that won’t get relief, or pressure around the Achilles tendon, I sometimes cut the back of the shoe’s heel cup to reduce that pressure.

This permanently changes the shoe and reduces its support. I make a small cut, have the person walk around, and check for relief before cutting farther. There may be stiff plastic in the heel cup, so cutting it requires care.

I’m not a medical doctor. These are techniques I’ve learned through trial and error in the field, working with actual people and actual feet. Your mileage may vary.

These are the common blister locations I see and how I approach taping each one.

Blisters on the back of the heel are usually associated with friction against the shoe. Cutting the heel cup is an aggressive place to start, so I generally begin with tape.

I cut a strip of Leukotape to fit the hot spot or blister and round the corners so they’re less likely to peel up. A larger area may need two or three strips.

If conditions are wet or I’m concerned about the tape moving, I apply a benzoin towelette where the tape will stick. If there’s already a blister, I cover it with a small piece of gauze before applying the tape.

I use my scissors to help peel the tape from its backing without touching the adhesive. The gauze should cover the blister without sticking out from beneath the tape.

Blisters under a callus on the side of the big toe are common. I’m wary of trimming a callus in the field, so I usually leave it alone and focus on limiting its movement.

A small patch of tape is unlikely to stay put under that much pressure. I typically wrap the toe instead.

Even for my size 11.5 feet, a full-width strip is too wide, so I trim it narrower. I aim to put the overlapping seam on top of the toe, where it’s less likely to peel than underneath.

If the tape bunches around a curve, I make small cuts where it folds so I can press it flat without wrinkles.

I use the same approach as for the big toe: cut a strip long enough to wrap the affected toe, trim it to roughly half the roll’s width, and apply it with the tape lying flat.

These blisters are tough. The skin is thicker here, and there’s often a callus involved. It’s also a high-stress area, so a small piece of tape may peel off as you walk.

I use a larger piece that extends up the sides of the heel toward the ankle, where there’s less pressure working against it. Wherever the tape starts to fold, I cut it so I can press it flat against the skin.

After taping any of these areas, I rub a small dab of balm onto the outside of the tape to help keep it from sticking to my sock.

I’ve only seen this a few times, but a blister on the ball of the foot might be the worst one. I don’t have a great fix. The goal is to take pressure off the blister by creating a donut around it.

I don’t carry moleskin. If I needed to make a donut, one option I would consider is marking the blister’s location on the hiker’s insole, then cutting a corresponding hole in the insole. The idea is for the blister to sit inside that opening, with pressure distributed around it.

If you develop a blister here, think about shortening your days or your trip. It’s a painful spot and difficult to manage once a blister has formed.

I generally lean toward draining blisters because relieving the fluid pressure can make walking less painful. But opening a blister exposes the fragile skin underneath and introduces a risk of infection. As I mentioned earlier, I’m not a medical professional.

When I decide to drain one, I clean the instrument first and consider where fluid is being pushed as the person steps. I want the opening positioned so it can continue to drain.

Afterward, I wash the area with soap and water and leave the skin over the blister in place. Don’t peel that layer off; let it come off on its own.

If the tape comes off easily, I generally remove it and let the foot dry overnight.

If the tape is fresh and removing it would take skin with it, I leave it alone. I still try to get the foot dry, using dedicated sleeping socks or sleeping without socks, depending on the conditions.

Increasing redness around a blister and pain that gets worse each day are warning signs I watch for. If you suspect an infection, get off the trail and seek medical care. Staying on the trail and hoping it improves is not a good option.

Andrew Skurka has backpacked, skied, and packrafted approximately 30,000 miles, including the Sea-to-Sea Route, Great Western Loop, and Alaska-Yukon Expedition. He also runs a guided backpacking program, where much of his foot care experience comes from.

In the video, Andrew says his 2027 guided-trip schedule will be released in November 2026, with registration opening in December. Learn more at andrewskurka.com/guided-trips.

RIP to LIV, the Golf League No One Ever Really Cared About

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The most remarkable thing about the era of LIV Golf, a five-year run characterized by near-parodic blunders and missteps, is that leadership and its public-facing talent don’t appear to have learned any lessons from their many failures.

From the beginning, the upstart league, established in 2021 as a rival to the dominant PGA Tour, was plagued by its own ineptitude. It’s only fitting—after LIV’s newfound lifeline, private investment firm BC Partners, reneged on a $300 million investment—that the brand’s figureheads are now celebrating the end by taking a victory lap. Tone-deaf doesn’t begin to describe it.

LIV CEO Scott O’Neil—who took over for original boss and Major winner Greg Norman in January of 2025 and was faced with finding new cash flow after Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund announced in April that it would no longer finance the operation—clearly exists in a different reality than our own. “Together, our team delivered world-class individual and team competitions across five continents, built a global business, secured pathways to The Open and U.S. Open… and built a league capable of attracting both elite and rising talent from around the world,” he said in a statement late last week. It’s unclear if the farewell tour will continue, though, an even newer report suggests that BC is looking to purchase a bankruptcy loan from LIV’s current owners. If this Hail Mary works, LIV’s future would look much different than the one O’Neil has promised.

Another way to look at it? One of LIV’s biggest accomplishments was that the league, despite its intentions to diversify the game and bring fun back into a sport that had come to personify privilege and exclusivity, inadvertently consolidated the PGA Tour’s power as a monopolistic behemoth now primed to solidify its status as the only league in town. They helped usher in a new era of leadership and investment for the Tour, including the introduction of CEO Brian Rolapp, who is by all accounts the most well-liked and accomplished boss it’s had in quite some time.

LIV also managed to tarnish the reputations of its biggest stars. Jon Rahm, who was vehemently opposed to the dog and pony show when it was announced, only to sheepishly take a bag of blood money so big his great grandchildren will never have to work a day in their lives, is now one of the game’s great “What If?” stories. Prior to the US Open at Brookline Country Club in June 2022, the defending champion was asked about his thoughts on the upstart league. “Money is great,” he said. But Rahm and his wife thought about it further. “Will our lifestyle change if I got $400 million? No, it will not change one bit.” Just over a year later, he defected to LIV.

Then there’s Phil Mickelson, who was poised to become a fixture of the American Ryder Cup team and widely considered a shoo-in to hold a cushy job as an analyst for golf’s biggest broadcasting events. Upon joining the league, he admitted in a conversation with Alan Shipnuck that Saudi Arabia was a terrible bedfellow. (Shipnuck and Mickelson have disagreed over whether the comments were on or off the record.) “They killed Khashoggi and have a horrible record on human rights. They execute people over there for being gay,” he explained. But there was a caveat: LIV was simply a “once-in-a-lifetime opportunity to reshape how the PGA Tour operates.” Miraculously, he got that part right.

Prize money on the PGA Tour is greater than ever, the Tour’s main events have recalibrated, and its season-ending championship series has been completely reimagined. Mickelson won’t be allowed within 100 miles of it.

The league’s biggest attraction, Bryson DeChambeau, is theoretically the worst-situated of the game’s many turncoats, though he’s probably more excited about growing his YouTube channel than he is about playing golf anyway. No one’s put their reputation on the line for this gaggle of buffoons quite like DeChambeau, who spoke proudly about how LIV was “growing the game” and “bringing team golf to the world.” This past weekend, he shared a statement on the dissolution of LIV. “I still believe in team golf,” he wrote. “It may look different in the future, but I still believe it’s essential to growing the game.” A loyal foot soldier, even from the grave.

It was always about the money. Nothing more, nothing less. This was a cash grab of epic proportions, and golfers not good enough to earn their keep on the PGA Tour took a life raft and briefly found themselves in possession of luxury superyachts. Then, low tide left them holding bags of cash and not much else. Was it worth it? For most of these golfers, probably. They were past their primes when LIV began; they’re still past the primes now, and just a helluva lot richer.

The league positioned itself as a disruptor, and disrupted so poorly that they’ve turned the PGA Tour into the strongest version of itself we’ve seen since Tiger Woods was at his peak. All that bloviating about the good of LIV Golf from its loudest talking heads is a bunch of nonsense.

It’s hard to tell if these statements are one last line of defense or more genuine delusion from LIV’s last advocates. They came in with the idea of sticking it to the PGA Tour, which at the time was facing bleak circumstances, bogged down in nonsense before redirecting its energy towards batting this gnat into extinction. Ultimately, LIV helped the PGA Tour save itself.

Now it’s gone for good, and five billion dollars later, we’re not gonna remember a damn thing about it. Not the players, not the leadership, and surely not all the ways they supposedly grew the game while merely growing their own bank accounts.

Delta Air Lines, United Airlines & JetBlue Now Sell The Business Class Seat Without The Business Class Deal

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

You can now buy a business class seat without buying the full package traditionally associated with business class. Delta Air Lines, United Airlines, and JetBlue Airways have introduced lower-priced premium fares that preserve the physical seat while separating benefits once bundled into the ticket. A passenger may still receive a lie-flat seat and premium meals, but could lose advance seat selection, lounge entry, generous baggage allowances, or the ability to change plans without a fee.

The shift reflects a broader airline strategy: sell the same onboard seat at several price points, then charge according to how much flexibility and ground service a traveler wants. Delta launched Basic fares across Delta One, Premium Select, and First in July 2026. United announced Base Polaris in April, while JetBlue introduced Mint Base and is preparing its BlueFirst domestic first-class product for December. Their rules differ, and BlueFirst is a recliner product rather than a true lie-flat business class. Still, all three carriers are moving toward a model in which cabin and fare bundle are separate decisions.

For decades, airlines generally sold business class as a package. The fare bought the seat, but also included services such as advance seat selection, lounge access, multiple checked bags, flexible changes, and higher mileage earnings. Customers could expect the most expensive cabin to include a broad set of privileges. The new structure divides that package into tiers. A base or basic fare offers a lower entry price, while standard, classic, or flex options restore extras for a higher amount. Two passengers can occupy adjacent lie-flat seats while receiving different ground benefits and ticket conditions.

The model follows unbundling in economy, where basic fares often exclude advance seat selection, checked luggage, or flexibility. Applying it to premium cabins gives airlines another way to compete on the initial price displayed in search results without discounting every component of the experience. For travelers, the headline fare is less informative than it once was. A low business-class price may suit a short trip with fixed plans and little luggage, but prove less compelling for an international journey involving connections or schedule uncertainty. Travelers must compare the fare conditions rather than rely on the cabin name.

Delta began selling expanded premium Basic options on July 8, 2026, covering Delta First, Premium Select, and Delta One, which the airline calls Basic Business. Delta First Basic began flying on select domestic and Latin American routes in July, while Premium Select Basic and Basic Business became available for purchase for travel beginning in September. Basic Business retains the Delta One onboard experience, including a lie-flat seat, premium dining, and cabin amenities. Restrictions apply mainly to booking and airport benefits. Seats are assigned after check-in, changes and cancellations are subject to fees, and the fare is not refundable to the original payment method after the risk-free cancellation period. Customers are also ineligible for complimentary upgrades and same-day confirmed or standby changes, including those with Medallion status.

General SkyMiles members earn two miles per eligible dollar on Basic Business, compared with five on Delta One Classic and seven on Delta One Extra. Basic fares also include one fewer checked bag than the comparable Classic fare where complimentary baggage applies. Restrictions also vary between Delta’s three premium cabins. See Delta's fare rules here.

Lounge access has a transition period. Basic Business tickets retain Delta Sky Club and Delta One Lounge access, as well as Delta One check-in, through January 18, 2027. For travel beginning January 19, those benefits are no longer included. Customers may still enter a Sky Club through a separate qualifying membership or card benefit, but Delta One Lounge access will be reserved for Delta One Classic or Extra.

Delta Air Lines will launch Basic Business and First Class fares in 2026, unbundling premium travel as fare segmentation expands across cabins.

United announced its premium fare tiers on April 3, 2026, introducing Base, Standard, and Flexible options for Polaris and Premium Plus on long-haul international flights, transcontinental services, and selected longer Hawaii routes. The rollout extends the tiered approach already used in Economy. Polaris Base retains the lie-flat business-class seat, but removes several benefits associated with the standard product. Advance seat selection costs extra, the fare includes one checked bag rather than two, and changes or refunds are restricted. United also excludes Base customers from its dedicated Polaris Lounges. United Club access remains available.

Standard restores complimentary seat selection, two checked bags, and the ability to make changes, while Flexible adds a fully refundable ticket. The tiers separate the onboard product from the ground experience and ticket flexibility. Travelers who mainly want a flat bed on an overnight flight may find Base sufficient, particularly if they do not need lounge access or expect to change their itinerary. Those connecting internationally with several bags may find the higher fare more practical.

United’s structure illustrates why “business class” no longer describes a single set of entitlements. Polaris Base and Polaris Flexible passengers share the cabin, but not the same airport access or ability to recover a ticket’s value if plans change. The value depends on the cost of adding each feature separately. The table below from NerdWallet breaks down the comparison well:

JetBlue’s Mint Base is a lower-priced version of its lie-flat business-class product. The fare includes the Mint seat, premium dining, early boarding, priority security, and one checked bag. Standard Mint and Mint Flex include two checked bags. Mint Base does not include complimentary advance seat selection or BlueHouse lounge access at New York’s JFK and Boston Logan (BOS) airports.

Seat selection on Mint Base begins 24 hours before departure. Changes and cancellations also carry fees; cancellations produce a travel credit minus the applicable charge. Standard Mint provides advance seat selection and a travel credit for eligible cancellations, while Mint Flex adds a refund to the original form of payment and more generous same-day flexibility. JetBlue lists one TrueBlue point per dollar for Mint Base, against three per dollar for Mint and Mint Flex, before applicable booking bonuses.

JetBlue is extending the same fare architecture to BlueFirst, its new domestic first-class product. BlueFirst is not Mint and does not have a lie-flat seat. It uses wide recliners in a two-by-two layout, with additional legroom, complimentary food and drinks, and priority airport services. The first revenue service is scheduled for December 8, 2026, between Boston and Fort Lauderdale, with additional routes planned. BlueFirst Base includes one checked bag, while standard and Flex versions include two. Seat selection is complimentary within 24 hours of departure on Base, whereas advance selection is included in the higher tiers. Changes and cancellations are subject to fees on Base; Flex adds refunds to the original payment method and fee-free same-day switches or standby. JetBlue is extending fare segmentation across its premium portfolio.

Delta One and United Polaris are leading business class products, but which one is the better choice? Is network coverage better than luxury?

The three airlines have adopted a similar commercial idea, but their restrictions differ enough that comparing fares by the word “Base” can mislead. Delta Basic Business reduces mileage earnings, removes advance seat selection, restricts same-day changes, and eventually excludes lounge access. United Polaris Base preserves United Club entry but removes Polaris Lounge access, charges for seat selection, and reduces the baggage allowance. JetBlue Mint Base removes BlueHouse access and advance seat selection, while keeping priority security and one checked bag.

A passenger who values a specific seat may need to add the selection fee. Someone traveling with two checked bags should account for the extra bag charge, while a traveler with uncertain plans should compare potential change or cancellation fees against the price difference for a more flexible fare. Delta also says certain eligible SkyMiles American Express cardholders retain baggage benefits, and JetBlue Mosaic members and other eligible cardholders continue to receive applicable benefits.

The strategy changes how premium cabins compete. Rather than lowering every business-class ticket, airlines can advertise a cheaper entry point while protecting higher-yield fares from customers who need flexibility, lounge access, or additional baggage. This may make premium seats accessible to more price-sensitive travelers, but it also risks disappointing customers who interpret the cabin label as a promise of the traditional package. Booking disclosures must clearly explain those differences.

Delta, United, and JetBlue are reshaping premium travel around a separation between the seat and the services attached to it. The lie-flat bed or wide recliner remains the central product, while lounge access, baggage, seat selection, and flexibility become fare-level choices. For passengers, value depends on itinerary, loyalty benefits, and the likelihood that plans will change.

The next competitive test will be whether airlines make these differences clear enough for customers to compare total value before paying. Booking platforms may need clearer comparisons of lounge eligibility, refund rules, seat assignment, and baggage allowances. Airlines that make the trade-offs transparent can sell a lower-priced premium experience without undermining trust. Those that emphasize the cabin while obscuring restrictions risk turning an attractive fare into an expensive surprise.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

They didn’t “introduce lower-priced premium fares.” They took the regularly priced premium seat and stripped it of all the perks (sans the seat) and then made a new higher fare for the fully-premium seat, thus increasing prices for all. Just another way for Delta and United to do what they do best: gouge their customers for even more money (along with “personalized” AI-generated fares).

I just sailed the Nile on a Viking ship. It's a great way to tour Egypt

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Our Viking guide, Karim Zohny, is leading us through one of the best-preserved ancient temples in all of Egypt, the Temple of Hathor, explaining the famously colorful reliefs that line its massive sandstone pillars, walls and ceilings.

Located along the Nile near the Egyptian city of Qena, it is, as Zohny notes, unusual in its motifs, which include intricate celestial maps and giant images of the Egyptian queen Cleopatra and her son Caesarion.

That our small group of two dozen passengers from the Viking river ship Viking Osiris is even here is also relatively unusual.

Most vessels that sail the Nile don’t come this far north. Instead, they stick to the 130-mile stretch of river that runs between the more southerly cities of Luxor and Aswan.

It's just one of several things that make Viking's 11-night Egypt tours, in my opinion at least (after years of traveling in Egypt), among the best choices for exploring the country's splendors.

Pairing a seven-night Nile sailing with four nights of land-based travel, Viking’s “Pharaohs and Pyramids” trips offer some of the most comprehensive, in-depth touring that you will find in Egypt. They also offer a Nile river ship experience that has few rivals, thanks to Viking’s recent rollout of an all-new series of Nile vessels that is setting a new bar for the region.

In addition, as I have seen on more than one Viking trip in Egypt, the trips offer a particularly seamless and mostly all-inclusive experience, with just about every element — from lodging and meals to expert-led guided tours and special access to iconic sites — included in the fare.

Aimed at the same sort of history-loving travelers who get excited about visiting the ancient ruins of Greece and Italy, Viking’s Egypt trips — like many tours of the country — focus heavily on what some consider the greatest civilization of all time: ancient Egypt.

Indeed, almost from the moment we land in Cairo to begin the tour, which begins with a three-night stay at the upscale Fairmont Nile City Hotel, we are thrust more than 4,500 years back in time to one of the oldest and greatest eras of ancient Egypt: The Old Kingdom.

Also known as the Age of the Pyramids, this was the period that saw the construction of monumental structures such as the 4,700-year-old Step Pyramid of Djoser, which we visit on our first full day of touring.

Located at the edge of the desert just outside Cairo, in an area known as Saqqara, it was, as Zohny explains, not only the first pyramid built in Egypt but also by far the largest stone structure built by humans up to that time.

In that respect, its construction was one of the great milestones of history.

It's a history we experience viscerally as we frog-walk down a low-sloping tunnel into another nearby pyramid built for the pharaoh Teti. After nearly a hundred feet, we emerge into a three-room tomb covered in ancient hieroglyphics, among the oldest funerary texts ever found in an Egyptian tomb.

The next day, Zohny, an expert “Egyptologist” who will be our guide for the entire trip, brings us to the even bigger (and more famous) Pyramids of Giza and the nearby Great Sphinx, which also are just outside of Cairo.

Built about a century after the Step Pyramid of Djoser, the Giza pyramids mark the peak of pyramid building in Egypt and are truly a sight to behold.

In between visiting these massive structures, we are treated to a full morning of exploring the new Grand Egyptian Museum, where we marvel at the gilded treasures recovered from the pharaoh Tutankhamun's tomb just a century ago. We also visit Cairo's alabaster-clad Mosque of Muhammad Ali.

It is a whirlwind two days that prepare us for the equally stunning sites from the more recent New Kingdom, Ptolemaic and Roman periods of ancient Egypt that we will see while sailing the Nile on Viking Osiris.

But first, we must get to the ship. Like most of the vessels that sail the Nile, Viking Osiris is based in Luxor, hundreds of miles south of Cairo — the center of ancient Egypt, which began around 4,100 years ago (when it was called Thebes).

After checking out of the Fairmont, we fly as a group to Luxor on a charter flight.

To sail the Nile on Viking Osiris or one of its many sister vessels (at last count, there were eight on the river) is to be on something akin to a greatest hits tour of nearly 2,000 years of iconic Egyptian structures.

Luxor, Qena, Kom Ombo, Edfu. On the ship's weeklong itineraries, these Nile river destinations famous for ancient sites come one after the other, each offering something spectacular that you probably remember from your second-grade history class.

In Luxor, it is the massive Temple of Karnak, with its colossal sandstone columns arranged in neat rows (if you’ve seen the James Bond vs. Jaws chase scene in the 1977 film “The Spy Who Loved Me,” you know what I’m talking about).

In Qena, it is the impressive Temple of Hathor, which is part of an even bigger archeological site known as the Dendera Temple Complex.

In Kom Ombo, it is the equally impressive temple of the same name, built during the Greco-Roman period and featuring reliefs of the Roman emperors Tiberius and Domitian.

Then there’s Edfu, home to the long-buried-in-sand Temple of Horus. Lost to time until a French archeologist began digging it out 166 years ago, it is almost completely intact, with its hypostyle halls and inner sanctuary looking much as they did 2,000 years ago.

Some of these destinations that the ship visits are gateways to multiple world-class sites. In addition to the Temple of Karnak (where Viking ships have an exclusive dock location), Luxor is our base for an outing to the nearby pharaonic burial grounds, the Valley of the Kings, a highlight of any Egypt tour.

In our case, the visit includes the opportunity to descend into five of the more than 60 tombs found in the valley so far — two more than is typical for many Egypt tours.

Viking has paid extra to get us access to perhaps the most famous tomb of them all, that of the pharaoh Tutankhamun (King Tut, as we called him when I was a boy), who remains inside the tomb in mummified form, as well as the spectacular (and normally expensive to visit) tomb of Seti I.

During our stay in Luxor, we also wandered through the Temple of Luxor and the terraced Mortuary Temple of Hatshepsut — the latter a masterpiece of ancient architecture.

Some passengers also soar skyward in a hot air balloon (one of the few extra-charge excursions during the trip) for views of the Mortuary Temple, Valley of the Kings and other area sites from the air.

While exploring the remnants of ancient Egypt is a big part of the trip, it’s not the only focus. As we sail the Nile, we also get a taste of everyday life on the river.

Some of my favorite moments of the voyage come as we steam through the many rural stretches of the Nile between Luxor and Aswan, where the riverbanks often are lined with date palms, reeds, wild grasses and, in some areas, lush agricultural crops.

It is a scene evoking the Nile of ancient times.

At times, I spot farmers working in small fields of bananas or sugarcane, or tending to cows grazing along the waterfront.

We regularly pass fishermen in small boats slapping the water with oars to scare the fish into nets — a traditional technique.

It is, notably, a narrow band of abundance. In some places, the fertile area fed by the life-giving waters of the Nile stretches just a few hundred feet inland, in other places maybe a mile or two, before the landscape abruptly transforms into harsh desert that stretches for hundreds of miles.

It’s one of the things that makes Egypt so fascinating. It is a country that essentially unfolds along one very long, very narrow ribbon of green in a sea of desert.

Or, as the Greek historian Herodotus famously wrote, Egypt is the gift of the Nile.

You can read about this in books, of course. But it's something you only truly understand when you see it up close from the deck of a passing vessel.

One of the big allurements of Viking sailings on the Nile is their laid-back pace.

Many Egypt tour itineraries include only three or four nights of cruising between Luxor and Aswan to see the major sites, or even fewer, resulting in a frenetic touring schedule. There is a lot to pack into just a few days.

Viking is one of just a small number of Egypt tour companies that slow the Nile-cruising portion of the trip by spreading it over a seven-night span. This allows for not only a more in-depth experience but also a more relaxed one — the latter of which has particular appeal to the mostly older travelers who are Viking's core market.

Many of Viking's customers are retirees who have the time for longer trips and also prefer to "explore the world in comfort," as Viking likes to say.

On several days of the sailing, notably, we only tour ancient sites for a few hours in the morning and spend the afternoon relaxing on board as the vessel sails the river.

During this time, Zohny and two other onboard Egyptologists lecture on topics such as medicine in ancient Egypt and Egyptian hieroglyphics. There are lessons in Egyptian cooking and basic Arabic. There's afternoon tea.

But there’s also plenty of time to just kick back on the sun deck.

The extra days also allow time for visits to ancient sites that most tours don’t include and for experiencing more on land than just ancient sites.

In addition to stopping at lesser-visited Qena to see the Temple of Hathor, for instance, we spend a morning docked in the often overlooked Nile town of Esna to see not just its lovely if small Roman-era Temple of Khnum but to shop in one of its small markets for local textiles.

For many of us, the shopping time allows us to haggle for Egyptian galabeyas — a traditional, loose-fitting robe worn by men and women across Egypt — for another major moment of the cruise, that night’s Taste of Egypt & Galabeya Evening. Passengers are encouraged to wear local dress, as the crew does, too, for a special dinner of all-local cuisine, and local music and entertainment.

In Aswan, which is as far south as river ships on these itineraries can go, we spend two days experiencing a wide variety of activities that range from touring the dedicated-to-Isis Temple of Philae and sailing in a traditional wind-powered felucca to visiting one of the local villages that are home to Nubians — a distinct ethnic group that has been living in the region for thousands of years.

We also visit the mighty 56-year-old Aswan High Dam, a marvel of construction that blocks river ships from going farther south on the Nile.

As noted above, Viking Osiris is part of a new series of Viking vessels on the Nile that, in my opinion, are the best-designed on the river.

Upscale and intimate, with just 41 cabins and suites apiece, these vessels feature the same elegant, clean-lined Scandinavian-influenced décor that can be found on Viking ships around the world and lots of high-end touches.

Public areas on each vessel include a stylish restaurant overseen by a European chef, a casual deck-top eatery with indoor and outdoor seating, and a cozy lounge and bar — all offering commanding views.

A key element of the design is the wide expanses of clear glass that serve as the outward-facing walls of nearly every public room. This essentially turns the entire ship into a window onto the stunning, colorful scenery that is scrolling by.

In essence, the light-greenish water of the Nile, the rich green papyrus and tall bamboo-like grasses along its banks, and the tan desert cliffs in the distance become the "art" and focal points of the ship's spaces.

That the glass is clear is notable for the Nile, where many vessels are wrapped in dark or metallic glass to reflect the often-intense heat of the sun — glass that, alas, often makes these ships feel closed off to the splendors of the river. Viking went to great lengths to keep the view clear by sourcing a more expensive type of glass that blocks heat without appearing tinted from the inside — a seemingly small detail that makes all the difference.

Other public spaces on each ship include a swanky sun deck with rows of plush lounge chairs for sunning and lovely shaded seating areas. At the very back of the top deck is a narrow pool.

Even the smallest cabins on the ships are generous in size, measuring 232 square feet. And they are filled with luxury elements such as high-end linens and pillows on the beds, heated bathroom floors and roomy wardrobes with wooden hangers.

My cabin — one of eight suites measuring 409 square feet or more — was among the most luxurious that I have seen on a Nile ship. Modern and residential in feel, it featured a large living room with a seating area and desk for working, an adjacent bedroom with a walk-in closet and bathroom, and an outdoor balcony with seating for two. The latter is something you don't always see on Nile ships.

Still, perhaps the most upscale touch of the vessel is the service level that it offers. A crew of 48 people is on hand to serve at most 82 passengers (the ship's maximum capacity with every cabin full — on my sailing, there were just 72), so there's never a lack of staff to cater to your every whim.

I saw this high service level firsthand quite a few times on board, whether it be from the waiter in the restaurant who, unprompted, prepared my traveling companion a special soothing tea with ginger, lime and honey after she mentioned she was feeling a little queasy, to the bartender in the lounge who not only remembered my favorite cocktail but started mixing it before I even had to ask.

In addition, Zohny, our Egyptologist, was the most knowledgeable and engaging Egypt guide that I have encountered during 25 years of visiting the country.

As is typical for higher-end tours of Egypt, Viking's trips in the region aren't inexpensive.

Sold as a combined land-and-cruise tour package, the 11-night "Pharaohs and Pyramids" journeys start at $6,999 per person, based on double occupancy (about $14,000 for a couple sharing a room). And such starting fares are specifically for packages that include an entry-level "standard" cabin without a balcony during the cruise portion of the trip.

For a package that includes a cabin with a balcony during the cruise portion of the trip, you'll find starting rates of around $7,499 per person (about $15,000 per couple). Packages that include a stay in a larger suite cost closer to $25,000 per couple or more, depending on the departure date.

In general, you'll also pay more at all cabin levels for departures from late December through early April, when cooler temperatures in Egypt make touring more pleasant.

That said, such fares are highly all-inclusive. Not only are your ship and hotel rooms included in the price, but most of your meals (along with wine, beer and soft drinks with lunch and dinner while on the ship). You also get extensive guided tours to all the main sites every day of your trip. The latter is a significant value, given the cost and logistical headaches of booking tours in Egypt on your own.

Indeed, a notable part of what you're paying for on a Viking trip to Egypt is access to extremely knowledgeable Egypt experts (known as Egyptologists) who travel with you and explain the many ancient temples, tombs and other historic sites you're seeing.

In addition, the fares include the interior flights in Egypt that will get you between Cairo and Luxor for the cruise portion of the trip, as well as airport transfers when you book international flights through Viking.

Some higher-level cabins also come with complimentary laundry service and a premium beverage package. Little things like shipboard WiFi are also included for all.

Viking's 11-night "Pharaohs and Pyramids" tours offer one of the most comprehensive and seamless ways to explore Egypt. The trips pair a well-paced, seven-night Nile sailing aboard an exceptionally designed new ship with a multi-night land stay in Cairo, together allowing visits to many of Egypt's most iconic attractions, including the Pyramids of Giza and the Valley of the Kings.

While fares are on the higher side among Egypt tours, the extensive all-inclusive nature of the trips — accommodations, most meals, extensive touring with an expert Egyptologist and internal flights are among the inclusions — means they offer a lot of value for the money. And perhaps most importantly, they make touring Egypt easy.

Ford Drops Hint About New Fathom EV Battery Life

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The Ford Motor Company has been playing its forthcoming Fathom electric pickup truck close to the vest, releasing few details ahead of volume production except for the MSRP of $28,350. Still, some clues have dropped. The latest news involves a newly released real-life data analysis of five-year battery capacity for the Ford Mustang Mach-E. The findings raise expectations for the next generation of Ford EVs, namely, the Fathom, which is the first vehicle to roll down the tree-like assembly line of the company’s new “Universal EV” platform.

On one level, the comparison between the Mach-E and the Fathom EV battery is apples-to-oranges. The 2021 Mach-E deployed [correction: NCM (nickel-cobalt-manganese) batteries with Standard Range at 68 kWh usable capacity as launched, and Extended Range at 88 kWh usable capacity as launched]. Ford is equipping the Fathom with an LFP (lithium-iron-phosphate) EV battery. LFP is a relatively new, more economical formula than Li-ion. All else being equal the tradeoff is lower range, though some Ford watchers point out that drivers can order their Fathom with an optional extended-range battery.

The point is that the 2021 Mach-E is old news. The new news is the forthcoming Fathom, which Ford has been regularly teasing as the first product of its secret, not-so-secret “skunkworks” team for the past five years or so. The addition of a cost-cutting, internal inverter for bidirectional charging is the topic of the most recent news drop, indicating that Ford expects Fathom drivers to use their EV battery for stationary applications as well as drive time.

Drivers who routinely use the bidirectional feature will likely put their Fathom through more charging cycles than a typical EV, and prospective buyers will need some assurance that their zero emission pickup will be up to the task.

And, that’s where the 2021 Mach-E data comes in. Ford has just released the 5-year numbers for retained battery capacity over time, and they look pretty good. By releasing the data now, when the Fathom is nearing volume production, Ford has raised expectations that the new electric pickup truck will deliver similar, if not better, longevity.

Correction: The Mach-E data doesn’t just look pretty good, it looks very good. Ford deployed its telematics platform to assess more than 36,000 Mustang Mach E’s in the “class of 2021,” driven in the US and Canada over an average ownership period of 5.2 years as of August 2026, during which time drivers clocked a collective 2 billion miles.

The 2021 Mach-E was sold with both standard and extended range battery packs, and the data combines both variations.

Relative to the Fathom’s bidirectional charging feature, the key finding is that high-mileage vehicles scored well. “More than 2,000 owners have crossed 100,000 miles on their original battery pack while maintaining >91% average health,” Ford explains. In the 2021 cohort, 30 drivers also ran past 200,000 miles while retaining an average capacity of about 88%.

Ford, which graciously shared an embargoed press statement with CleanTechnica over the weekend, also emphasizes that 95% of the 2021 Mach-E’s landed between 87% and 97% health, indicating that a relatively small number of outliers brought the average down to 92% overall.

The Mach-E data will surprise nobody who has been paying attention to the latest news about EV battery lifespan. As it turns out, EV batteries last far longer than first anticipated. That’s significant enough when an EV battery is deployed for mobility purposes. Capacity retention takes on extra importance when the same battery is used to manage electricity use in homes and other buildings.

Ford provided some insights last month, when it posted a long form article describing the Fathom’s built-in inverter. An inverter is a device that converts the DC current from an EV battery into the AC current used in homes. A typical bidirectional charging system requires the property owner to install a wall-mounted inverter, adding another layer of expense. Ford has absorbed the cost of the inverter into the Fathom itself, a decision aimed at encouraging drivers to make use of bidirectional charging at home. The company has also been working with utilities to help motivate drivers to take advantage of the feature.

With bidirectional charging, EVs realize their full potential as rolling, zero emission power generating stations. Drivers can use bidirectional charging on an emergency basis, to keep the lights on at home in case of a grid power outage. They can also take advantage of utility incentives, which reward EV owners for deploying their stored ectricity during periods of peak demand or other stresses on the grid, benefiting all ratepayers in the service territory.

Some US utilities already offer discounted or even free rates for EV owners who charge up their battery during off-peak hours. Try that with your gasmobile!

Stay tuned to this channel for further details about the Fathom and its battery. In the meantime, some Ford dealers have been passing along information from the automaker’s HQ. Here’s a representative example from the DC-area dealer Koons Ford:

“Battery options beyond the standard-range configuration have not been announced yet. Ford has confirmed a long-range battery option is planned and that its upcharge will be significantly lower than what Lightning buyers experienced, but specific range figures and pricing tiers are not yet public. This page will be updated as Ford confirms additional specifications.”

Okay, so much for that. Tonkin Hillsboro Ford in Oregon is hardly more forthcoming:

“Ford Fathom pre-orders will begin in early 2027, with a starting MSRP of $28,350 for the standard-range battery. While some specifications are still to come, there’s already plenty to know about this new Ford electric truck.”

“Ford has not yet released projected EPA-estimated driving range figures for the Fathom,” Tonkin elaborates. “The vehicle is currently being introduced with a standard-range battery, but an official EPA range estimate isn’t available yet.”

“This is one area where shoppers will need to wait for additional information,” the dealer adds.

Hmmm…let’s turn to the automotive press, where EV Charging Stations was somewhat more forthcoming. “The 2027 Ford Fathom electric pickup truck will not exceed 300 miles of range in its base form, but it likely will with the optional larger battery,” EVCS reports.

“The Ford Fathom has a 400-volt LFP battery system and 48-volt auxiliary electrical architecture. The truck features a cell-to-pack battery system (no modules), with prismatic battery cells being structural elements of the vehicle,” EVCS elaborates, further speculating that the extended-range battery could involve a higher-performing LFP formula or a switchover to the familiar NMC (nickel-manganese-cobalt) concoction.

EVCS also calculates that the standard EV battery will yield a range of under 220 miles, with the extended range version coming in at 300 miles, as Ford has indicated.

Meanwhile, the Detroit Free Press has noticed that a fully loaded Fathom with all the bells and whistles still comes in around the $40,000 mark, well below the $54,000 average cost of new EVs.

We won’t have long to wait for the full details to come out. Last week, Torque News was among those reporting that the Fathom is well into the final validation stages leading to full volume production.

With sales expected to begin late next year, the Fathom will face some competition from other EV makers that are aiming at the electric pickup truck market. One pickup stakeholder that Ford won’t have to worry about, though is Tesla, which is planning to halt production of the Cybertruck next year.

Photo: Ford is drawing attention to the long lifespan of the 2021 Mustang Mach-E EV battery, setting high expectations for the forthcoming Fathom electric pickup truck (courtesy of Ford via CleanTechnica archive).

Ex–PlayStation Exec Says True Innovation Would Be Making The PS6 Cheaper: ‘We Have Enough Power In The Machine’

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

PlayStation 6 is coming and it’s not coming cheaply. Sony is making “solid progress in preparing for the next-generation platform,” the company said last week, but its next-gen device is also reportedly likely to cost over $1,000. ExâPlayStation executive Shawn Layden would prefer the PS6 innovate on price rather than power.

“I think we have enough power in the machine,” the former chairman of SIE Worldwide Studios told The Trophy Room in a recent interview. “The innovation needs to come not from making PS6 for $1,200âit’s to make another PS5 for $300. Now, that would be true innovation.”

Layden has been a critic of the console war arms race in recent years, arguing that improvements have become increasingly hard to spot with the naked eye while sitting in anything less than pristine viewing conditions. The price tags for these improvements have also skyrocketed. Not only has the price of the PS5 actually gone up during its life cycle, the budgets for triple-A studios have exploded even as sales stay flat or go down, leading to a crisis among big publishers.

“I mean, the the jump from PS1 to PS2 was incredible, right?” he said. “And the jump from compact disc to DVD, different storage medium, higher density, and then from two to three when you got to 1080p and you can really do 60 frames per second at uh, you know, at a high frame rate. And then PS4, which was like, [it] completely worked and all that was done and and you still got it for $399. And then PS5, we’re talking about ray tracing and teraflops. And the talk about PS6 is even more ray tracing and more teraflops. These are the differences only dogs can hear.”

These trends are also coming to a head at a time when AI hyperscalers are consuming RAM at an unprecedented clip, spiking the prices for other hardware manufacturers at an unsustainable pace. Some companies predict the prices will only continue to go up through 2028, meaning the next generation of consoles could be the least affordable and the least impressive relative to the sorts of performance leaps fans have been trained to expect from a new batch of hardware.

Then you look at the Switch 2 and Steam and realize that millions of gamers are content just to play great games, regardless of whether they are graphical showcases or not. Lots of us just spent the weekend playing the Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake. I had plenty of thoughts strolling down memory lane but “I wish this looked better” definitely wasn’t one of them.

Jennifer Aniston on ‘The Rachel’ Revival, the Bob She Regrets, and LolaVie’s New Hairspray

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Jennifer Aniston may just be hair’s ultimate cultural icon. Her Friends character Rachel Green inspired one of the most coveted hairstyles of the ’90s: “The Rachel,” a voluminous, layered cut that sent countless women to salons in search of hair that looks as effortlessly chic as Aniston’s did on-screen. The irony? She remembers the style as anything but effortless. “I remember how much work that original haircut was!” Aniston tells ELLE exclusively.

Today, hair trends have shifted toward styles that feel more relaxed, lived-in, and less polished—including the modern version of “The Rachel” that features longer and softer layers. There’s also a greater emphasis on hair that looks and feels healthy, with movement and texture taking precedence over a stiffly styled finish. Even Aniston prefers the “undone” approach. “I don’t love anything that makes your hair stiff or crunchy,” she says. That philosophy is at the heart of the latest addition to her hair-care brand, LolaVie: the Weightless Medium Hold Hairspray. “We wanted to create a product that gives you hold without sacrificing the feel of your hair,” she adds.

Powered by adaptive peptides and a trio of tropical fruit-derived actives, the superfine spray sets your style in place while leaving behind touchable softness and a healthy-looking shine. The formula even makes second-day styling a breeze, with 450°F heat protection and 72-hour frizz protection.

In an interview with ELLE ahead of the product’s launch today, Aniston reveals her hair-care routine, her bob era, why she envies Sandra Bullock, and her favorite rom-coms. Read the full conversation below.

I try to keep it pretty simple. I always start with a good shampoo and conditioner, and then choose products depending on what my hair needs that day. If I’m blow-drying, I go for heat protection and a little smoothing. If I’m going for a more natural style, it’s all about hydration and movement.

We wanted to create something that gives you hold without sacrificing the feel of your hair. The Weightless Medium Hold Hairspray helps keep your style in place while still allowing your hair to move and feel soft and touchable.

I’ve always been curious about advances in science and medicine, especially when it comes to taking care of yourself, but I’ve learned it’s important to be discerning about where you get your information. I always do my research and have trusted sources and experts that I turn to. Just because something is trending doesn’t mean it’s right for you—or anybody, for that matter.

There’s so much information and so many new treatments available, which is wonderful, but it can also feel overwhelming. I hope the future is about getting back to the basics: sleep, movement, nutrition, managing stress, and taking care of yourself consistently. Technology can be amazing, but it’s not a replacement for the fundamentals.

I love that people are still inspired by it. The modern versions feel a little more relaxed and lived-in, which I love.

Many times throughout the years. Hair is such a fun way to experiment with how you look and feel, and sometimes you just want a change. I remember a couple of times thinking, A bob would be fun, so I took the leap...and then immediately started to let it grow back out. What can I say? I’ve found what works for me.

Sandy has the thickest head of hair on a woman over 40 that I’ve ever seen in my entire life! I have such hair envy! She is also one of my biggest LolaVie supporters. She’s ordered every product available from day one and continues to do so. I know she loves me, but God knows she doesn’t have to do that.

There are so many good ones! When Harry Met Sally, of course, is the quintessential rom-com. Rob Reiner and Nora Ephron—nobody did it better. The Philadelphia Story is also a classic.

It’s bittersweet. I’m proud of the journey we’ve taken Alex on and very grateful to have been part of telling these stories with such an extraordinary cast and crew. Our writers did an incredible job wrapping up not only Alex’s journey but the other characters we’ve come to love. You’ll just have to wait and see!

This interview has been condensed and edited for clarity.

Ancient DNA From 700 Skeletons Just Rewrote the Origin Story of the Languages We Speak Today

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Two Nature studies trace Germanic and Celtic tongues through buried bones, and the research results are surprising.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

One of the most fascinating aspects of humanity is the roughly 7,000 languages we’ve devised to express ourselves. And while these languages may seem pretty different—as anyone who’s crammed for a foreign language exam can attest—linguists have known for centuries that a large majority of European languages, including English—as well as many tongues outside of the continent—are derived from a single root language known as “Proto-Indo European.”

By the late 1800s, linguists suspected this proto-language began with horse-herding peoples north of the Black and Caspian seas, because its vocabulary centers on pastoral life. Genetic evidence has also pinpointed the Yamnaya culture as a key influence on the language’s migration because this group moved from modern Ukraine and Russia into eastern and central Europe some 5,000 years ago. From there, however, how other languages developed, branched off, and migrated into their present day configurations remains a fascinating puzzle.

But a pair of twin studies, both published in the journal Nature, attempt to track some of these language migrations. The researchers analyzed the DNA of thousands of prehistoric humans, including 700 newly sequenced individuals, in hopes of understanding how today’s language maps came to be.

“Ancient DNA cannot reveal the language or languages a particular individual spoke,” three of the study’s authors wrote in an article for The Conversation. “But by comparing prehistoric population movements with archaeological and linguistic evidence, we can increasingly test competing theories about how languages spread.”

The two articles use similar techniques to explore Germanic languages (such as German, English, Norwegian, Dutch, and Swedish) and the more elusive Celtic languages, whose roughly two million remaining speakers live primarily in Ireland, Wales, Brittany, and the Isle of Man. While the Germanic languages study uncovered fascinating new details about their migration, including that their ancient ancestral tongue might’ve reached Scandinavia 800 years earlier than we thought, the study focused on the Celtic languages appears to settle a longstanding debate about where they originated.

There have long been two competing theories about the geographic starting point of the Celtic languages. The first holds that proto-Celtic arose along Western Europe’s Atlantic coast. The second holds that it emerged in the continent’s interior, in the east, among the Bronze age Urnfield culture, and later migrated to its modern home in the northwest. The genetic analysis revealed the answer. An ancient people genetically associated with the Urnfield culture migrated from east to west roughly 3,200 to 2,800 years ago—exactly when many believe the proto-Celtic language first formed. Although relatively few Celtic-language speakers remain today, their populations once stretched across a large swath of the continent, before rival groups pressed in from both sides and drove them out.

“They had their time, but the Scandinavians start to expand to the south, and then the Romans move up and delete the Celtic languages,” University of Gothenburg archaeologist Kristian Kristiansen, a co-author of the study, told Science.

However, the team cannot fully rule out the western theory. Evidence of a migration from southwestern Europe into Britain roughly 3,800 years ago suggests the language may still have originated along the Atlantic coast. An archaeogeneticist who spoke with Science also noted that the skeletal samples offer stronger evidence for the Germanic languages than for the Celtic ones, and that surveying more Celtic burial sites could help refine the data. In short, many questions remain.

So when you’re puzzling over that next Duolingo practice session, keep in mind that deciphering the evolution of the language you speak is even more deviously difficult.

Darren lives in Portland, has a cat, and writes/edits about sci-fi and how our world works. You can find his previous stuff at Gizmodo and Paste if you look hard enough.

This 61-Year-Old Is in the Shape of His Life—and Credits This Exercise with Transforming His Chest

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Ricky Dietrich says the incline cable pullover has been a game changer.

A stronger chest isn't built through endless bench pressing and cable flys alone. Sometimes, adding a less obvious movement to your routine can provide a completely different stimulus.

At 61, Ricky Dietrich claims he's in the best shape of his life, and one exercise he credits with helping build his chest is the incline cable pullover.

“This is one of the only exercises I owe my lower chest growth to,” he says. “It keeps constant tension through the entire rep, lets me push close to failure safely, and has been one of the biggest contributors to building a fuller lower chest.”

While Dietrich describes it as a lower-chest exercise, pullovers aren't quite that simple. Depending on your setup and technique, both your chest and lats can contribute to the movement, with small adjustments changing where you feel the exercise most.

The incline cable pullover is a variation of the classic dumbbell pullover, a movement famously championed by Arnold Schwarzenegger and his Golden Era contemporaries.

Dietrich's version swaps the dumbbell for a cable machine and performs the movement on an incline bench. The cable keeps tension on the working muscles throughout more of the range of motion, rather than allowing the resistance to drop away at certain points.

Technique matters, particularly if you want to bias the chest rather than allowing your lats to dominate.

Dietrich recommends beginning with your arms overhead, keeping them relatively close together and maintaining a proud chest as you pull the cable forwards. Rather than continuing until your hands reach your hips, stop while your arms are still roughly 45 degrees from your torso, squeezing your pecs hard.

Allowing your elbows to flare and driving them further towards your sides can shift more of the work towards your lats. Using a lighter load should also make it easier to control the movement and concentrate on the muscles you're trying to target.

Think of the incline cable pullover as an accessory rather than a replacement for your presses and flys. But if your chest training has become stale, it could provide a useful new angle.

Ryan is a Senior Writer at Men’s Health UK with a passion for storytelling, health and fitness. During his two-and-a-half years there he wrote news stories for the website and features for the magazine, while he also interviewed names such as Les Ferdinand, Ally McCoist, Jamie Redknapp and Antonio Rudiger, among many others. His standout memory, though, came when getting the opportunity to speak to then-Plymouth Argyle manager Steven Schumacher as the club won League One in 2023.

Having grown up a keen footballer and playing for his boyhood side until the age of 16, Ryan got the opportunity to represent Northern Ireland national futsal team eight times, scoring three goals against England, Scotland and Gibraltar. Now past his peak, Ryan prefers to mix weightlifting with running – he achieved a marathon PB of 3:31:49 at Manchester in April 2025, but credits the heat for failing to get below the coveted 3:30 mark…

Exclusive: FormAssembly overhauls its platform to expand into AI-powered process automation

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

FormAssembly Inc., a provider of online data collection and workflow automation software, today announced a complete platform overhaul that expands its capabilities beyond building forms to automating complex business processes using artificial intelligence.

The new platform, which the company said took seven months to develop, combines form creation, workflow automation, integrations and compliance management in a single no-code environment.

It also introduces conversational AI agents that can collect information through natural language interactions and support for the Model Context Protocol, enabling external AI agents to interact with FormAssembly’s software.

The launch marks a significant expansion for the 20-year-old company, which has traditionally focused on helping organizations collect structured information securely and integrate it into business applications. “We’re about how people share information with organizations and what happens after that,” said founder and Chief Executive Cedric Savarese.

The new platform builds on the AI assistant FormAssembly introduced last October, which enabled users to generate forms and configure workflows using natural language instructions. The latest release extends those capabilities to entire business processes, including approvals, document generation, electronic signatures and payments.

For example, a municipal government could describe an application process for obtaining a permit to hold an event in a public park. The platform’s assistant, called Fai, could then generate the necessary forms, approval workflows and documentation requirements.

“You can explain what you need in plain English to your agent, and the agent will build the entire process in a couple of minutes,” Savarese said.

FormAssembly claims the AI-assisted builder can reduce development time from days or weeks to minutes. Savarese said the company based those estimates on recreating existing customer workflows using the new platform.

A second major addition is conversational intake agents that replace traditional forms with interactive voice or text conversations. The agents use predefined data structures to guide conversations, answer questions and convert unstructured responses into information suitable for downstream systems.

The agents can also personalize interactions using previously collected information, although Savarese emphasized that access to that information is restricted by default.

“The default for us is privacy,” he said. “You only decide what information needs to be collected and what information is relevant for that particular task for the AI agent.”

FormAssembly uses frontier AI models, primarily from Anthropic PBC, rather than developing its own. Savarese said the company has zero-retention agreements with Anthropic and OpenAI Group PBC to protect customer information.

The revamped platform also adds governance capabilities that evaluate workflows against predefined compliance policies before publication. Organizations can use built-in rules or define their own requirements based on internal policies and applicable regulations. Potential violations are flagged for human review rather than automatically blocked.

MCP support and application programming interfaces enable customers to create and manage FormAssembly workflows through external systems without accessing the software’s traditional interface.

Savarese said this approach could protect established software companies from the risk that AI agents will make conventional software-as-a-service applications obsolete.

“People will use AI agents, but they use AI agents to drive software, not to replace it,” he said.

The company isn’t positioning itself as a comprehensive competitor to business process management vendors such as ServiceNow Inc. Instead, it intends to concentrate on processes that begin with collecting information from people.

Existing customers won’t be forced to migrate immediately. Savarese said migrations aren’t planned until January, and pricing for existing customers remains uncertain. More sophisticated automation capabilities could carry additional charges depending on customers’ current subscriptions.

FormAssembly, founded in 2006, raised its first outside funding of $10 million in 2019 after 13 years of bootstrapping. The company says it has thousands of customers, particularly in regulated industries such as healthcare, financial services, education and government.

Nvidia RTX PRO 6000 user’s cables almost melted, even when limited to 500W

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

12V-2x6 strikes again, but thankfully it didn't take out a GPU this time.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Reddit user u/Doomslayer606 posted about frayed braided sleeving on both 12V-2x6 cables for their two RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Workstation GPUs the other day, asking whether it’s normal cable degradation or a sign of overheating. The GPUs were running at 500W, below the maximum 600W power draw, but commenters are confident it’s overheated cables, as the poster reports stiff, cracked, and easily peeling sleeves on only some of the wires.

u/Doomslayer606 posted on r/PcBuild with the title “Did I just save my RTX PRO 6000 GPUs from melting?” with photos of the 12V-2x6 cable’s braided sleeving frayed. The poster asked in the description whether this was normal degradation, a manufacturing issue, or overheating. The poster reported that the damaged braided sleeving felt stiff and peeled off easily, which is a strong indication of heat damage, and the comments were sure that it was.

Luckily, the poster’s GPUs were safe, but the 12-2x6 connectors are notorious for melting due to high power draw. One comment asked if they kept the stock power limit, to which the poster responded that they lowered both GPUs to 500W — another user responded asking why the poster wouldn’t use 300W, but the poster said it would reduce performance by up to 50% and they need to keep up with their deadlines.

Theoretically, the cable should be able to handle 500W, as each positive pin would only handle 6.94 A, which isn’t enough current to significantly heat up 16 AWG wire, but one of the main causes of cable overheating is uneven current distribution across the pins. Since only two of the pins melted on both cables, it’s likely those received high-current spikes often, leading to heat buildup.

Users have suggested aftermarket cables like the Corsair ThermalProtect 12V-2x6 cable that detects overheating cables, and the Thermal Grizzly WireView Pro II or Aqua Computing AMPINEL, which have active current balancing to prevent any wire from taking too much current alone. In a recent story, someone even created a power distributor, distributing the 12 pins into two 8-pin cables with a custom-designed PCB, which lowered the cable temperatures significantly — while another modder had a cursed solution, soldering thick cables directly to the RTX 5090’s power pins.

In an ideal world, consumers shouldn’t need another product or mod their GPUs to stop them destroying themselves. GPUs have only gotten more powerful, but instead of using more or thicker wires, we’re getting fewer wires of the same thickness. Understandably, having multiple 8-pin connectors gets messy, but surely there has to be a better solution. Maybe GPUs have outgrown the Mini-Fit Jr. connectors and need to have their own special connector with thicker pins and thicker wires.

Meteorite that landed in Cape Cod was a 'lone wolf' from the outer solar system, scientists find

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.
