ECA Power completes community solar project in town seeking revitalization

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Employee-owned solar and energy storage developer ECA Power, Luminace, ComEd, construction partner Miller Bros., and local community leaders celebrated the energization of the 7.4-MWDC Rockford Community Energy Initiative (RCEI), a community solar project in Cherry Valley, Illinois.

“The Illinois General Assembly has spent the past several years passing legislation to encourage and incentivize renewable energy projects like this one,” said State Sen. Steve Stadelman. “It’s always exciting to work on legislation and then see that work translate into real development on the ground.”

“Northern Illinois is becoming a national leader in community solar, and projects like this are helping bring the benefits of clean energy to more families and businesses,” said Gil Quiniones, president and CEO of ComEd. “We’re proud to work with ECA Power and Luminace to connect projects like this to the grid, and build a power system that can support continued clean energy growth across our region.”

RCEI was built in a state-designated R3 area, bringing new investment to a community targeted for revitalization. As part of its community benefit agreement with the Village of Cherry Valley, ECA Power contributed $150,000 to support the buildout, operation and maintenance of an EV charging station.

“At ECA Power, we believe the success of a project should be measured not only by the energy it produces, but by the lasting value it creates for the surrounding community,” said Todd Fryatt, founder and CEO of ECA Power. “The Rockford Community Energy Initiative reflects that commitment by expanding access to affordable, locally generated energy while bringing meaningful investment to the region. From supporting local revitalization to our partnership with the Village of Cherry Valley, we’re proud to deliver a project that will benefit this community for years to come.”

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

Home listings pile up in October, but sellers won't slash prices

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Homebuyers continue to retreat, but sellers are still digging in, refusing to cut prices or give up. As a result, September's frozen housing market is now October's frozen and increasingly bloated market.

Active inventory in October hit 183,500 homes, up 7.4% year-over-year, according to Realtor.com. That is the fastest annual growth in eight months. Active listings have been rising each month since February. This is despite the fact that new listings are actually falling, down 7.5% from October 2025.

The market has seen several years of extremely lean supply, driven by the homebuying frenzy during the first two years of the pandemic, when mortgage rates hit more than a dozen record lows. Now supply nationally is just 6.6% below typical pre-pandemic levels, the narrowest gap since the pandemic.

"The market is cooling on both sides, but in different ways. Buyers are stepping back as higher mortgage rates cut into their buying power, while sellers are listing less often without a broad increase in delistings or price cuts," said Danielle Hale, chief economist at Realtor.com. "We're seeing a pause in new supply, but owners who already have homes on the market appear to be largely unphased."

Just over 20% of sellers cut prices in October, about the same as both September and October of last year. There was also no meaningful increase in delistings by sellers.

Mortgage rates came down a little bit in October month-to-month, but they are still over a full percentage point higher than a year ago. That continued to weigh on sales. Newly pending sales, representing signed contracts, not closings, dropped 16.8% in October year-over-year, according to Zillow. That was the biggest annual drop since August 2023 and slowest October since 2018.

While rates fell slightly, home prices continued to gain. Zillow estimated the monthly mortgage payment on the typical U.S. home was $2,004 in October, assuming a 20% down payment and excluding taxes and insurance. That is an increase of 12.8% from last year and 4.8% higher than in September, the biggest one-month jump in four years.

"November is shaping up to have an even larger contraction, but the silver lining is that next month is likely going to be the worst of it," said Mischa Fisher, Zillow's chief economist, in a release. "I expect the pace of the slowdown to ease into the new year. There are deals to be had for buyers able to brave the affordability headwinds in the coming months, and the continued inventory buildup means there is a lot of upside for sales into next year if we get rate relief."

Analysts see no broader ‘contagion’ from $6.3B Binance hack

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Binance’s pledge to cover all user losses helped contain the fallout from the $6.3 billion hack, analysts said.

Binance’s pledge to cover user losses from its $6.3 billion hack helped contain the fallout across crypto markets, analysts told Cointelegraph.

“There doesn’t seem to be any contagion-type effect from the hack, given that Binance is able to cover the losses using its balance sheet,” Martin Lee, market insights lead at DWF Labs, told Cointelegraph. He said investors turned their focus to the implications of the US midterm elections.

Binance’s daily net outflows fell to $458 million in the 24 hours leading to Monday’s publication time, down from $6.1 billion on Nov. 2 after withdrawals resumed, according to DefiLlama.

Attackers stole about $6.3 billion from Binance hot wallets in the Oct. 29 breach, making it the largest crypto hack to date. Bitcoin (BTC) fell as much as 5.7% over the first five days after the hack, a decline CryptoQuant called “modest” in its Wednesday report .

Bitcoin fell roughly 13.6% after the $1.4 billion Bybit hack in 2025 and about 9.9% after the Coincheck hack in 2018. At least 14 of the past 38 hacks CryptoQuant reviewed were followed by steeper Bitcoin declines.

Bitcoin’s drawdown after the Binance hack, compared to previous exchange hacks. Source: CryptoQuant

Major exchange hacks have previously triggered “short-lived drawdowns that markets later absorbed” as long-term holder demand returned, Lacie Zhang, research lead at Bitget Wallet, told Cointelegraph, adding that the Binance hack does not represent a “structural trend reversal” for crypto markets.

Related: Binance hackers had 8-week intrusion window before $6.3B exploit: SEAL

“Much of the hack’s impact now looks priced in,” with Bitcoin currently trading at an “attractive level,” Bitfinex analysts told Cointelegraph.

“Confirmation of a market bottom now depends on the Consumer Price Index (CPI) reading due on 10 November, which will shape the Federal Reserve’s next rate decision. Higher bond yields would put pressure on risk assets.”

Market participants priced in an 80% chance that the Federal Reserve will raise rates at its Dec. 9 meeting, up from 69% a month earlier, according to the CMEGroup’s FedWatch tool.

Bitcoin fell 2.5% over 24 hours to $74,580 as of 1:21 pm UTC on Monday, according to CoinMarketCap.

Student journalists are reporting more local news for real audiences. Their professors are exhausted.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

A new study finds that journalism school faculty — charged with putting more student work in front of non-student readers — often serve as a hidden infrastructure for local news. Burnout is a risk.

If there’s one big takeaway from the past two decades of local news struggle — the often perilous, occasionally brilliant transition from dominant newspapers to online media — perhaps it’s this: Passion alone isn’t enough.

A newspaper newsroom filled with committed editors and brilliant reporters still stares into the same headwinds as its more middling peers. The devotion of those leading a nonprofit outlet can mean little if revenue can’t keep the doors open. And even when the money is less important — when journalism is somewhat protected from the demands of the market — passion is still a finite resource, and it’s not always renewable.

That truism came to mind when I was reading a recent article by Sima Bhowmik of the University of Vermont and Andrea Lorenz of Kent State. It looks at one specific corner of the contemporary news ecosystem: journalism schools that use their students to fill voids in local coverage.

J-schools, after all, have faculties full of experience and students who need it. A few decades ago, an early-career journalist might have spent a few years covering city council meetings and school board budgets before moving up the chain to a bigger outlet. That path barely exists these days, and journalism programs know their students need the chance to do real work. What if these reporters-in-training could handle some of the local stories that the city’s daily no longer can?

RELATED ARTICLEA “win-win” partnership brings a surge of reporting firepower to hyperlocal news outlets around BostonSarah ScireNovember 13, 2025RELATED ARTICLELen Downie: The teaching hospital model works, but it will require drastic changeLeonard Downie Jr.September 12, 2012If you happen to live near a college with a journalism program, you’ve probably seen this idea — often called the “teaching hospital” model — in action. Here in the Boston suburbs, many of the stories I read about my town are written by Boston University students. According to UVM’s Center for Community News, where Bhowmik is a post-doc research fellow, there were 282 such efforts as of 2025, producing local news inside 205 colleges and universities. Those numbers were up from 227 programs in 172 colleges the year before — a boom fueled by a surge in philanthropic funding for these partnerships.

Bhowmik and Lorentz’s paper — published in Journalism & Mass Communication Educator — is the result of interviews with 118 faculty and staff at 101 of those colleges, and their major takeaway is one that will be familiar to many in local news: They’re exhausted.

Participants repeatedly used phrases such as “at capacity,” “don’t have the bandwidth,” “not enough hours of the day,” and “it’s just a lot” to describe the workload. Several described long workdays and exhaustion, with one participant saying the amount of editing “just about kills me every semester.” At many institutions, faculty workload was intensified by staffing shortages, retirements, hiring freezes, and budget cuts. This left faculty with little time to provide the one-on-one coaching necessary to elevate student stories to publishable quality… “What’s happening in the academic ecosystem is so phenomenal, thanks to these really passionate founders on the faculty,” Bhowmik told me. “But the extra work they’re putting it isn’t always getting acknowledged within the system. A professor might be editing 10 or 20 stories a week, by students without much experience, on top of a full teaching load. There’s a lot of overwork and some dissatisfaction, even though they know how important the work is.” (See above: Passion alone isn’t enough.) Here’s how one faculty member described their situation: Think about it: Like, how many reporters does the average assigning editor at a mid-sized newspaper supervise? Half a dozen? So try, like, 20 of them, and each of them is writing for a different outlet maybe, which has its own set of deadlines and editor. And they’re all at varying skill levels, and broadly, they’re all pretty new. Their copy requires a lot of work, their reporting often requires a lot of handholding. Another talked about the responsibility of getting student work up to the level a news outlet expects. You can’t just give a meh student story a C- and be done with it if a local editor is expecting publishable copy. It takes a lot of work to get student work up to the quality of professionally published work. And a lot of students start out with really good ideas, do some good reporting by the time they’re done in the semester, it needs work, and that tends to fall on the faculty person. And that’s a lot of work outside of uncompensated work…But bridging that gap between what a student produces at the end of a semester and what can actually be published is still a big gap that needs to be addressed. And aside from the editing work, faculty described the administrative burden of being the connective tissue joining two distinct worlds. Negotiating pitches, coordinating deadlines, dealing with revisions, shepherding copy flows — all of it with students who are juggling other courses, work-study jobs, and the life of college student — can be “difficult to sustain without administrative support, dedicated staff, or funding for editors,” they write. Just to save a few dozen proud Mizzou alums from emailing me: Yes, the “teaching hospital” model is not a new idea. The idea of giving journalism students real-world experience in public-facing outlets has been central to the Missouri Method for more than a century, and other major journalism schools have long done versions of this work. But Bhowmik notes that many of these new efforts are in smaller colleges with smaller journalism programs — places without the in-house infrastructure of a Columbia Missourian or KOMU, where media partnerships involve more ongoing negotiation. That can leave more of the pressure of sustainability on one or two overworked faculty members. As Bhowmik and Lorenz put it: …faculty labor is not incidental to the model; it is the hidden infrastructure that allows student work to move from classroom assignment to public-facing journalism. We argue that passion may help launch these programs, but passion alone is not a sustainable staffing model. If universities treat these initiatives as ordinary courses while expecting faculty to function simultaneously as instructors, editors, partnership managers, and newsroom supervisors, the model remains vulnerable to burnout and institutional instability. None of this, of course, is to say that these academic-publisher partnerships aren’t performing valuable roles in hundreds of communities. These programs have generated thousands of stories that otherwise might never have reached the public. But the sort of exhaustion Bhowmik and Lorenz describe reminds me a lot of what we saw in the first wave of local nonprofit news outlets in the late 2000s and early 2010s. Many of those were founded by idealistic journalists — often refugees from the local daily — who saw the opportunity to do something new and valuable for their communities. They put in long hours editing stories, selling ads, pitching foundations, and building audiences. And within a few years, a lot of them were burnt out, and their outlets suffered for it. In the decade-plus since, funders have gotten smarter about building institutional capacity — knowing that passion can only fuel so much. The world Bhowmik and Lorenz describe inside these journalism programs makes me think that department chairs and deans would be wise to consider a similar pivot to make these academic-publisher partnerships sustainable for the long term.

“What’s happening in the academic ecosystem is so phenomenal, thanks to these really passionate founders on the faculty,” Bhowmik told me. “But the extra work they’re putting it isn’t always getting acknowledged within the system. A professor might be editing 10 or 20 stories a week, by students without much experience, on top of a full teaching load. There’s a lot of overwork and some dissatisfaction, even though they know how important the work is.”

Here’s how one faculty member described their situation:

Think about it: Like, how many reporters does the average assigning editor at a mid-sized newspaper supervise? Half a dozen? So try, like, 20 of them, and each of them is writing for a different outlet maybe, which has its own set of deadlines and editor. And they’re all at varying skill levels, and broadly, they’re all pretty new. Their copy requires a lot of work, their reporting often requires a lot of handholding. Another talked about the responsibility of getting student work up to the level a news outlet expects. You can’t just give a meh student story a C- and be done with it if a local editor is expecting publishable copy. It takes a lot of work to get student work up to the quality of professionally published work. And a lot of students start out with really good ideas, do some good reporting by the time they’re done in the semester, it needs work, and that tends to fall on the faculty person. And that’s a lot of work outside of uncompensated work…But bridging that gap between what a student produces at the end of a semester and what can actually be published is still a big gap that needs to be addressed. And aside from the editing work, faculty described the administrative burden of being the connective tissue joining two distinct worlds. Negotiating pitches, coordinating deadlines, dealing with revisions, shepherding copy flows — all of it with students who are juggling other courses, work-study jobs, and the life of college student — can be “difficult to sustain without administrative support, dedicated staff, or funding for editors,” they write. The idea of giving journalism students real-world experience in public-facing outlets has been central to the Missouri Method for more than a century, and other major journalism schools have long done versions of this work. But Bhowmik notes that many of these new efforts are in smaller colleges with smaller journalism programs — places without the in-house infrastructure of a Columbia Missourian or KOMU, where media partnerships involve more ongoing negotiation. The idea of giving journalism students real-world experience in public-facing outlets has been central to the Missouri Method for more than a century, and other major journalism schools have long done versions of this work. But Bhowmik notes that many of these new efforts are in smaller colleges with smaller journalism programs — places without the in-house infrastructure of a Columbia Missourian or KOMU, where media partnerships involve more ongoing negotiation. That can leave more of the pressure of sustainability on one or two overworked faculty members. As Bhowmik and Lorenz put it: …faculty labor is not incidental to the model; it is the hidden infrastructure that allows student work to move from classroom assignment to public-facing journalism. We argue that passion may help launch these programs, but passion alone is not a sustainable staffing model. If universities treat these initiatives as ordinary courses while expecting faculty to function simultaneously as instructors, editors, partnership managers, and newsroom supervisors, the model remains vulnerable to burnout and institutional instability. None of this, of course, is to say that these academic-publisher partnerships aren’t performing valuable roles in hundreds of communities. These programs have generated thousands of stories that otherwise might never have reached the public. But the sort of exhaustion Bhowmik and Lorenz describe reminds me a lot of what we saw in the first wave of local nonprofit news outlets in the late 2000s and early 2010s. Many of those were founded by idealistic journalists — often refugees from the local daily — who saw the opportunity to do something new and valuable for their communities. They put in long hours editing stories, selling ads, pitching foundations, and building audiences. And within a few years, a lot of them were burnt out, and their outlets suffered for it. In the decade-plus since, funders have gotten smarter about building institutional capacity — knowing that passion can only fuel so much. The world Bhowmik and Lorenz describe inside these journalism programs makes me think that department chairs and deans would be wise to consider a similar pivot to make these academic-publisher partnerships sustainable for the long term.

And aside from the editing work, faculty described the administrative burden of being the connective tissue joining two distinct worlds. Negotiating pitches, coordinating deadlines, dealing with revisions, shepherding copy flows — all of it with students who are juggling other courses, work-study jobs, and the life of college student — can be “difficult to sustain without administrative support, dedicated staff, or funding for editors,” they write.

Just to save a few dozen proud Mizzou alums from emailing me: Yes, the “teaching hospital” model is not a new idea. The idea of giving journalism students real-world experience in public-facing outlets has been central to the Missouri Method for more than a century, and other major journalism schools have long done versions of this work. But Bhowmik notes that many of these new efforts are in smaller colleges with smaller journalism programs — places without the in-house infrastructure of a Columbia Missourian or KOMU, where media partnerships involve more ongoing negotiation. That can leave more of the pressure of sustainability on one or two overworked faculty members. As Bhowmik and Lorenz put it:

…faculty labor is not incidental to the model; it is the hidden infrastructure that allows student work to move from classroom assignment to public-facing journalism. We argue that passion may help launch these programs, but passion alone is not a sustainable staffing model. If universities treat these initiatives as ordinary courses while expecting faculty to function simultaneously as instructors, editors, partnership managers, and newsroom supervisors, the model remains vulnerable to burnout and institutional instability. None of this, of course, is to say that these academic-publisher partnerships aren’t performing valuable roles in hundreds of communities. These programs have generated thousands of stories that otherwise might never have reached the public. But the sort of exhaustion Bhowmik and Lorenz describe reminds me a lot of what we saw in the first wave of local nonprofit news outlets in the late 2000s and early 2010s. Many of those were founded by idealistic journalists — often refugees from the local daily — who saw the opportunity to do something new and valuable for their communities. They put in long hours editing stories, selling ads, pitching foundations, and building audiences. And within a few years, a lot of them were burnt out, and their outlets suffered for it. The world Bhowmik and Lorenz describe inside these journalism programs makes me think that department chairs and deans would be wise to consider a similar pivot to make these academic-publisher partnerships sustainable for the long term.

Chiesa ‘can return soon’ to Italy squad, but requires Liverpool opportunities first

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Roberto Mancini says Federico Chiesa ‘can return soon’ to the Italy national team squad, but adds that he requires fitness and playing opportunities at club level with Liverpool first.

Chiesa has not played for Italy in over two years, since the EURO 2024 round of 16 defeat against Switzerland.

He was named in the Italy national team squad for the first time since then by Gennaro Gattuso ahead of the World Cup play-off campaign earlier this year, but withdrew and left Coverciano on the first day of the camp.

Chiesa was not included in the youthful Italy squad led by Silvio Baldini for friendlies against Luxembourg and Greece in June, nor was he included in Mancini’s first squad after his reinstatement as head coach back in September.

Mancini, speaking during a press conference at Coverciano on Monday, left the door open for Chiesa’s return to the Italy national team squad first, but made it clear that he must regain full fitness at club level first.

“I spoke to Federico many times, also recently,” said Mancini. “He’s not playing right now and must regain fitness, but he is 29, still in his physical maturity; I think he can return soon.”

Chiesa has again been suffering from physical and fitness issues in the early stages of the 2026-27 season. A back injury ruled him out of the first five Premier League matches of the season, but since then, he has only appeared once and for just two minutes at the end of a 3-0 defeat against Manchester City.

He has been left out of the Liverpool squad for three of the last four matches in a row and was not included on the club’s Champions League squad list for the 2026-27 league phase.

Josh King says Jude Bellingham has taken him 'under his wing' in England camp

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

King earned a first senior England call-up on Friday, along with Bellingham’s brother Jobe

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Josh King says Jude Bellingham has taken him “under his wing” in the England camp as the Fulham star hopes to earn a first cap for his country this international break.

The 19-year-old earned a maiden call-up to the England squad on Friday, with Thomas Tuchel hailing the “exciting” player having only just missed out on selection for the first camp of the season across September and October.

It is not King’s first time with the England group, however, with the midfielder picked as part of a five-man development squad for the pre-World Cup training camp in the summer.

It was there that King says Bellingham took on something of a mentorship role for him, crediting the England talisman for helping him integrate.

"There were a lot of people I learned from [in the pre-World Cup camp in Miami],” King said. “Jude was a big person for me. He took me under his wing and tried to make me feel welcome.

"There were a lot of players like that. Harry Kane as well, he was one person who really made me feel welcome and tried to help me through my time there.

"But it's a credit to what England have going here, everyone is a really good person as well as good player on the pitch."

Speaking further on his experience before the World Cup, he said: "It was an amazing opportunity for me. It was nice to meet the lads and get a taste of what it was like to play for England.

“I took a lot of learning from that opportunity. It was a massive part of my journey and it gave me the confidence to start this season well.

"[Tuchel] said to keep going, play well, rack up the minutes. As long as you're trying to perform as well as you can at club level, the rest comes."

King was an injury doubt after suffering groin pain in Fulham’s 1-1 draw with Newcastle on the weekend but his withdrawal was “precautionary”.

"I couldn't turn down the call,” he asserted. “I'm here. I'm all good fitness wise."

King will now hope to make his England debut as Tuchel’s side play their final two Nations League group matches, first hosting Croatia on Thursday before travelling to Madrid to play world champions Spain on Sunday.

Tuchel cited his versatility when explaining why he picked him for the November camp, with King - often a number 10 - able to operate across the midfield as well as up front.

But King isn’t fussy where he plays and is happy to feature wherever Tuchel wants him.

"I'm happy to play anywhere for the manager,” he added. “I have to add other parts to my game, in wider positions you might have to cover defensively or make runs in behind.

"I'm capable of playing lots of different positions, which is probably one of the reasons why I'm here."

Jose Mourinho Named Best Player 'He's Ever Seen on a Football Pitch'

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Rob is senior football editor at GiveMeSport, with over 15 years experience covering sport in various capacities since graduating from the University of Gloucestershire in 2008 with a degree in journalism. A childhood obsession for all things football developed into a career writing about the beautiful game, initially producing content for clients including Luton Town Football Club and the PGMOL. His first big interviews were with Premier League referees Howard Webb and Michael Oliver in 2009. He then spent four years producing written content for a betting website, mainly focusing on football, and was there to cover various games and tournaments including the 2010 World Cup and Euro 2012. Rob joined GiveMeSport in 2013 (initially as a sub-editor before becoming a senior writer and then senior editor in 2020) and has covered countless football matches and other sporting events, interviewed various high-profile figures including Rio Ferdinand, Peter Crouch and Theo Walcott, and written well over 10,000 articles during that time. Rob has played a key role in GiveMeSport's coverage of the World Cups in 2014, 2018 and 2022, plus Euro 2016, Euro 2020 and Euro 2024, along with many Champions League finals. While football is Rob's area of expertise, he has also covered a wide range of other sports during his time at GiveMeSport including boxing, tennis and darts, as well as the 2016, 2020 and 2024 Olympic Games.

Jose Mourinho once named the best player ‘he’s ever seen on a football pitch’ - but the current Real Madrid boss snubbed both Cristiano Ronaldo and Lionel Messi in the process.

Mourinho has made no secret of his admiration for both Ronaldo and Messi over the years.

The revered Portuguese tactician worked with his compatriot Ronaldo at Real Madrid between 2010 and 2013, although they didn’t always see eye-to-eye at the Bernabeu.

Indeed, Luka Modric revealed that Mourinho even made Ronaldo cry in the dressing room on one occasion.

That said, Mourinho will always be grateful for the incredible contribution that Cristiano made under his management during his first spell at Real Madrid.

As for Messi, Mourinho admitted the 2022 World Cup winner is the one player he would loved to have managed. He also described the eight-time Ballon d’Or winner as the “God of football”. Praise doesn’t come any higher.

Despite that, there’s one legend who Mourinho said is the “best football player” he’s ever seen on a pitch.

And were it not for injuries, this player would almost certainly have been part of the debate over the greatest football players of all time.

That player is, of course, Ronaldo Nazario - the Brazilian, and the original, Ronaldo. In 2023, Mourinho told LiveScore, via The Sun, of R9:

"The best football player that I have ever seen on a pitch.

“I think injuries killed a career that could have been even more incredible. But that talent this guy had at 19 years old was something incredible.”

Mourinho agreed that R9 lacked the same longevity as Ronaldo and Messi but suggested his natural ability was off the scale.

"Not with a career like Cristiano or Messi, 15 years at the top - every day,” Mourinho said. “But a natural talent, incredible."

It’s remarkable that Ronaldo Nazario managed to come back from two career-threatening knee injuries and was still lethal in and around the penalty area during the latter years of his career.

But Mourinho witnessed Ronaldo up close during the Brazilian’s breathtaking 1996-97 season at Barcelona, where he scored 47 goals in 49 games.

Blessed with extraordinary speed, close control, trickery, and agility, Ronaldo was like nothing football had seen before when he burst onto the scene in the 1990s.

While he still ended his career with his status as an all-time great secured, there will always be a question of ‘what if?’ over the Brazilian.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Scotland have been handed a major boost as they prepare to return to Nations League action after it was confirmed that Switzerland captain Granit Xhaka has withdrawn from the squad ahead of the team’s clash in St Gillen next week.

Xhaka, 34, has been forced to pull out of the Switzerland squad after picking up a calf injury in Sunderland’s 1-0 win over Chelsea in the English Premier League on Sunday, with the Swiss FA confirming he will play no part in their upcoming fixtures against Slovenia and Scotland this month.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Forced off just three minutes into the Stadium of Light clash, Sunderland head coach Regis Le Bris confirmed he had been struggling with a problem before the game, his 50th for the Black Cats. “It looks like a calf injury,” said the Frenchman. “But we don't know exactly the timeframe at the minute. It was just the last sprint of the warm-up; that's often the case [with these injuries].

The news means that Xhaka, capped an astonishing 152 times by his country, has now been absent from international duty for the last 120 days. He also withdrew from last month’s reverse fixture at Hampden Park amid a pending investigation into claims that he forged a Covid certificate.

The Sunderland star was accused of obtaining a forged vaccination certificate for a doctor based in Lucerne in order to bypass travel restrictions. He later apologised and confirmed his actions were a result of “fear and uncertainty” and a “deep mistrust” in the vaccine, and received a suspended fine of £136,265 (150,000 Swiss francs) from the Lucerne public prosecutor's office. He voluntarily missed all four of Switzerland’s Nations League fixtures.

However, with the matter resolved, he was reinstated to Murat Yakin’s squad for their final two Nations League fixtures, including their clash with Scotland at the AFG Arena on Monday, November 16.

Needing just a point from their game against Slovenia in Maribor this Friday to confirm their status as Group B1 winners, it was hoped that their legendary captain could return to the national fold for the first time since their 3-1 extra-time defeat to Argentina in the World Cup during the summer.

However, on the eve of his side returning for their initial training camp, Xhaka’s absence has been confirmed by national team following a scan at the Academy of Light after he limped out of Sunderland’s win on Sunday afternoon. Revealing the news via a short statement, the Swiss FA said: “Granit Xhaka has to withdraw from the national team gathering due to a calf injury.”

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Just 12 minutes into the game, George Coleman broke into the box and was brought down, earning Uckfield a penalty. Rhys Taylor stepped up and confidently tucked it away to make it 1-0.

Garroch wasn't finished there! Finding himself around the goalkeeper again, this time from a wider angle, he managed to dig out a cross to the oncoming Oscar Morris, who powered his header home to make it three.

In the second half, the boys continued to control the game. Just shy of the 75th minute, Alex Saunders took a short corner to Taylor, who dropped his shoulder to beat his defender before whipping the ball into the bottom corner for 4-0.

Five minutes later, Taylor was involved again, playing a lovely ball down the left to Morris, who took on two defenders, worked his way into the box and fired into the near-post corner to make it 5-0.

In the closing moments, Tyler Grant picked up a loose ball just beyond the halfway line before driving towards the box. He cut the ball back to Fraser Argyle, who finished brilliantly with the outside of his foot to complete the scoring at 6-0.

It was a fantastic team performance, featuring six goals and another clean sheet.

Uckfield are fourth and visit fifth-placed Reigate Priory twos on Saturday.

There’s a certain perspective on this weekend’s defeat now, the dust having settled on the Ewood Park 2-1 loss and the furious away fans now returned back to their day jobs. In the cold light of day this would seem to be a drama, not a crisis – West Ham are ninth in the division, sure, but still just six points off second spot.

Defeats have been bad, but it is not yet disaster time, according to talkSPORT’s Jason Cundy

It shows the hugely competitive nature of this league, true, but suggests that with Kretinsky’s swift action to replace Nuno with former Hammer Scott Parker, West Ham have acted in time to save themselves.

That is certainly the view of Jason Cundy, talk SPORT pundit and former Chelsea and Tottenham regular who is quite happy to stick the boot in to the Hammers. Unnervingly, in this instance he’s helped bring a dash of stability to Hammers’ fans’ woes. Yahoo Sport’s article confirms – Cundy is still a believer:

“Maybe it’s just me being deluded but I still think West Ham will be there or thereabouts come the end of the season. It’s not a disaster this.”

Yes there’s a drama at east London, as most neutral’s favourites for automatic promotion continue to stumble. But six points can easily be recovered over the hectic schedule in the run up to Christmas. Maybe a reminder to us all to take a breath, check the table again at the end of the year – and let Scott Parker do his thing in the meantime.

Birmingham City set to welcome back James Beadle for Stoke City clash, but doubts remain over six-goal star

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Birmingham City have gone into the November international break top of the Championship. BCFC have been really impressive to start the year, and have continued winning games, even with some injuries to key players.

Chris Davies’ side have gone the their last two games without goalkeeper James Beadle and top-scorer August Priske. However, as reported by Birmingham Live, Davies has provided an update on the pairs fitness going into the internationals.

“James (Beadle) definitely will be (back),” Davies confirmed but he couldn’t be so sure on Priske. “August (Priske) did everything to get ready but he couldn’t,” the Blues manager said.

Both Beadle and Priske could return for Birmingham City’s trip to Stoke City on 21 November, but the striker’s availability would be the bigger boost.

Beadle is the established first-choice goalkeeper after missing the wins over Millwall and Swansea with a knock. Davies has confirmed he will be available for Stoke, although Ryan Allsop has impressed in his absence.

Priske’s return is less certain, but potentially more significant. The forward provides their main focal point in attack. Davies is hopeful the international break will give him enough time to recover, but has stopped short of guaranteeing his availability.

While BCFC have continued scoring without him, having their main goal threat back for such an important away fixture would be a considerable boost.

Priske has been a key player for Birmingham City this season and has really come into his own after a slow start last term. However, his injury has not slowed up the Blues as many might’ve thought.

Davies has adjusted his side well and in Priske’s absence, BCFC have still been an attacking threat. Max Bird has come into the side as part of the front four with great success.

Jay Stansfield has also found his scoring touch with three goals in his last two games. The Blues have adjusted very well to Priske’s absence, and while they were prefer to have their top-scorer in action, the club have shown they are still strong enough without him.

Anthropic skips six-year waitlist with massive $3.4 billion gas turbine generator deal

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Anthropic takes over Caterpillar generator orders as AI companies increasingly secure their own electricity supplies

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Anthropic, the AI company behind Claude, has agreed to spend $3.4 billion securing 1.7 gigawatts (GW) of gas-fired generators for its AI data centres, taking over equipment orders originally placed with Caterpillar by Texas-based supplier OnePWR Solutions. According to Reuters, the agreement was reached in late September, and also covers the engineering and construction work needed to install the equipment. Anthropic is not buying OnePWR itself. Instead, it is taking over the supplier's existing generator commitments, which were placed roughly two years ago but required additional financing. By taking over the existing orders, Anthropic avoids having to start from scratch and wait for Caterpillar to find another larger order into its production schedule. That timing matters too, as larger orders for large gas turbines and generators are piling up, and manufacturers are struggling to keep up with demand, especially from AI companies.

Notably, global turbine orders have reportedly exceeded 100GW, while manufacturing capacity stands at an estimated 60GW to 70GW. Average delivery times in the U.S. have reached six years, with some prospective orders extending into 2030 or 2031.

The latest purchase puts Anthropic increasingly in familiar territory. Elon Musk’s AI has already turned to gas-powered generation to bring its large computing facilities online without waiting for conventional grid connections. Recently, xAI installed eight converted General Electric jet turbines at a data center in Mississippi. Each unit can generate up to 50MW, giving the installation a potential combined output of 400MW. The deployment has also attracted environmental scrutiny, with the Southern Environmental Law Center challenging the site's emissions permits.

The environmental implications of relying on gas-fired generators have also attracted criticism. “This is pretty grim—these kinds of generators are terrible for the environment.” Shakeel Hashim, founder of ReadTransformer, tweeted, commenting on Anthropic's purchase. His reaction points to an issue Anthropic will have to contend with as it expands its power infrastructure: securing electricity quickly does not remove the environmental costs of generating it.

Musk's companies have been moving further into the equipment business, too. In August, it emerged that the company was bringing turbine-blade and vine manufacturing in-house, with Musk identifying components production as a major constraint on generator deliveries, with some turbine blades reportedly taking between 60 and 90 weeks to be manufactured. Earlier, in July, SpaceX also acquired APR Energy, a company with a fleet of trailer -mounted gas and diesel generators capable of producing more than 1GW.

The approach is not exclusive to Musk's businesses. OpenAI has followed a similar route for its first Stargate data center in Abilene, Texas. In July, we reported that Crusoe, the company developing the facility, had acquired 29GE Vernova LM2500XPRESS gas turbines, each rated at 34MW. Together, the 29 turbines could generate up to 986 MW. Anthropic is taking a different path, though, by stepping into OnePWR’s existing Caterpillar orders, securing generators that were already in use, rather than buying new equipment.

That said, the power shortage is forcing AI companies to look beyond new generator orders. There has been an upsurge in demand for used and refurbished turbines, including retired jet engines converted to generate electricity. But even those alternatives have limitations, including maintenance requirements, remaining service life and suitability for continuous operations. For Anthropic, however, taking over OnePWR’s Caterpillar orders could provide a way around some of those hurdles. The agreement also provides installation capabilities, meaning the company is securing more than the generators themselves.

Delightful Tidying Up Indie Gets A Surprise Switch 2 Edition Today, Complete With 120fps

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Admirers of 2022's delightful little 'tidy 'em up', A Little To The Left, will want to pay attention to this one. Developer Max Inferno and publisher Secret Mode have announced the game's Switch 2 Edition, and it's available today!

Launching to celebrate the game's fourth anniversary, A Little to the Left - Nintendo Switch 2 Edition boasts a smooth 120fps, Mouse Mode controls, faster load times, a visual boost, and USB mouse and keyboard support.

The best part is that the upgrade path is completely free for all those who own the base game on Switch 1. The NS2E store page isn't live at the time of writing, but considering that the original only set us back £12.99 / $14.99, we can't imagine this new version will be particularly bank-breaking.

Max Inferno also has a patch inbound for all versions of the game, which targets a few known issues in the Daily Tidy mode and Seeing Stars DLC puzzles.

The fiddly controls were one of our biggest hangups with the original release on Switch 1, which we otherwise said "provides an experience that is a weird mingling of the semi-passive and viscerally intrusive" in our 8/10 review. Let's hope that everything plays as it should in this new version.

What hardware does your PC need to run a local AI model?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Less than you might think, but a 16GB PC will only take you so far.

Download Ollama or LM Studio, pick a model, and within a matter of minutes, you can be up and running with a local chatbot. There's no subscription fee, it works without an Internet connection, and none of your data leaves your PC. But there's a catch: What you can run, and how quickly it responds, depends entirely on how powerful your machine is.

Your everyday PC with 16GB of RAM will be able to run relatively small models with around seven to eight billion parameters. Meta's Llama 3.1 8B, or Alibaba's Qwen3 8B or a graphics card with 8GB of VRAM will run them decently fast. But those models are a long way from today's frontier models behind ChatGPT or Claude. If you want to run something closer, you'll need at least 32GB of memory and a graphics card with 16GB or more VRAM which, at today's prices, doesn't come cheap.

The memory a model needs scales almost linearly with its size, which is measured in billions of parameters (B). Most people run compressed ("quantized") versions of local models that store each parameter in about four bits, cutting a model to roughly a quarter of its original size. In Ollama's library, that puts Llama 3.1 8B at 4.9GB, Microsoft's Phi-4 at 9.1GB, Google's Gemma 3 27B at 17GB, and Meta's Llama 3.3 70B at 43GB. Ollama's general rule of thumb has been that you need 8GB of RAM for a 7B model, 16GB for 13B, and 32GB for 33B.

OpenAI's gpt-oss-20b has 21 billion parameters but only uses 3.6 billion of them for each token via a design known as "mixture of experts." That makes it fast, but the model still has to fit in available memory. OpenAI says it runs "with just 16 GB of memory," but at 14GB in Ollama, that's almost all of a 16GB PC.

Each time a model generates a word, or more accurately a token, it has to read through all of its parameters. As such, speed depends far more on memory bandwidth than raw processing power. Nvidia's engineers describe that stage as "memory-bound." A graphics card's VRAM is several times faster than the RAM in the rest of your PC, which is why a model that fits on your GPU runs a lot faster than one that spills over into system memory.

As a rule of thumb, anything above five to 10 tokens per second keeps pace with how fast most people read. In LMSYS testing, Nvidia's RTX 5090 was found to generate text from OpenAI's gpt-oss-20b at 205 tokens per second. llama.cpp's benchmarks, meanwhile, put Apple's base M4 chip at about 24 tokens per second on a 7B model. Ollama measured Nvidia's DGX Spark, the $3,999 AI developer PC Nvidia started selling last year, at 38 tokens per second on Llama 3.1 8B, but just 4.42 on the 70B version.

I personally ran Mistral 7B on a 2024 Asus Zenbook Duo with 32GB of RAM and an Intel AI Boost NPU. Through Ollama, Mistral pushed CPU use to 52 percent and memory use to around 17GB. That's manageable on its own but would bog the laptop down if anything else demanding was going on. Running through Intel's OpenVINO toolkit instead, the same model kept the NPU at 98 percent while the CPU sat at three percent, and the laptop stayed fast and responsive while it wrote 2,500 words (of nonsense) in about three minutes.

In Artificial Analysis' April 2026 rankings, Qwen3.5 27B from China's Alibaba scored 42 on its intelligence index, level with OpenAI's GPT-5 at its medium setting, while Google's Gemma 4 31B scored 39. The leading frontier cloud models sat at 57, though, and Qwen3.5 27B needs between 17GB and 20GB of memory, which is obviously beyond what a 16GB PC has to give.

But even the most powerful PCs will barely scratch the surface of what frontier intelligence needs. Moonshot's Kimi K2.6, a 1-trillion-parameter model that was among the best open-weight models when it launched in April 2026, needs around 600GB of combined RAM and VRAM to run at 4-bit, according to Unsloth, which publishes compressed versions of open models. Its successor, Kimi K3, scored 57 on Artificial Analysis' intelligence index in July 2026, behind only Anthropic's Claude Fable 5 and OpenAI's GPT-5.6 Sol. At 2.8 trillion parameters, though, its weights alone come to roughly 1.4TB, which TECHi estimates would barely fit on an eight-GPU data center server.

So, for the odd private summary or some offline coding help, a 16GB PC is fine. For something you'd actually use instead of a cloud chatbot, you'll want 32GB of RAM or a 16GB graphics card. A 32GB kit of DDR5 alone costs $434, according to Tom's Hardware's RAM price index, or almost two years' worth of a $20-a-month ChatGPT Plus or Claude Pro subscription. Anything close to the frontier, though, is a job for a data center.

Musk says Tesla will refund some Cybertruck deposits, defends decision to end production

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Tesla CEO Elon Musk is defending the Cybertruck and the company’s decision to stop producing the polarizing steel-clad truck, claiming that the order rate far exceeds its production.

Tesla will shut down production of the Cybertruck in about eight months Musk said in an X post on Monday. He also said the company will try to make as many of the vehicles as possible before then, though Tesla will “have to return deposits for a lot of people.”

Musk argued the Cybertruck was not unprofitable, and that Tesla was ending production because the “opportunity cost of the complexity of Cybertruck is vastly outweighed by the value of incremental Cybercab production.”

In other words, Musk is arguing that Tesla can make more money by using the factory space and resources dedicated to the Cybertruck for building Cybercab robotaxis. Musk said last week that Tesla plans to use the factory space to build more two-seater Cybercabs.

The first Cybertrucks were delivered to customers in late 2023, when it seemed like it could be a breakthrough hit for the company. Reservations for the vehicle had ballooned to 1 million, according to Musk, who also predicted that the company would sell around 250,000 of the trucks annually.

But those reservations never translated into actual sales. Tesla doesn’t break out sales of the Cybertrucks, but industry experts estimate a total of about 71,000 Cybertrucks have been sold since the first deliveries began in November 2023.

Many of those sales came from inside Musk’s ecosystem of other businesses. For instance, SpaceX (now known as SpaceXSI), which he also leads and is the largest shareholder of, purchased $131 million worth of Cybertrucks from Tesla in 2025, according to the company’s S-1 registration statement ahead of its IPO. Another Musk company, xAI, also bought $430 million worth of Tesla Megapacks for its data centers, before it merged with SpaceX.

But sales of the Cybertruck, which lagged for months, could see a final bump now that it’s going out of production. Still, even a scarcity boost is unlikely to push the truck’s demand to the lofty projections from several years ago.

What is the Ironman World Record? It’s Complicated

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Who holds the Ironman world record? Depending on who you ask, it's Magnus Ditlev and Anne Haug, who own the fastest iron-distance time. (Photo: Ironman, Challenge Family, FinisherPix)

After a dramatic DNF in the final miles of the run at the 2026 Ironman World Championship, Magnus Ditlev (DNK) raced at Ironman Florida just four weeks later to post the fastest Ironman finish time ever with a 7:19:17.

That time upended the previous best iron-distance set by Sam Laidlow just a few months prior at Challenge Roth, where the Frenchman finished in 7:21:04 on the course where the fastest women’s race ever is still held by Germany’s Anne Haug from 2024

And yet, despite all these amazing feats, there are no world records in full-distance racing.

Confused? It’s understandable. Pour yourself a cup of coffee and get ready to learn why Ironman (and iron-distance) world records technically don’t exist.

In the world of running, 26.2-mile marathons must adhere to rigorous certification standards set by World Athletics that include restrictions on things like point-to-point courses, elevation drop, and (recent) International measurement, but triathlon operates without such official oversight. Course configurations are notoriously inconsistent, often shifting from one season to the next, and triathletes don’t even think twice about point-to-point courses or elevation drops between start and finish.

Certainly course distances are confirmed in high-level World Triathlon races like the Olympics, but there is no course certification for long-course or for regular age-group races. Often, you’ll hear athletes remark that their GPS watch clocked an Ironman course as short or long – rarely does a race hit exactly 2.4 miles of swimming, 112 miles of cycling, and 26.2 miles of running. Those are ballpark figures that races would love to hit if they can, but permitting issues might mean the host city can only close 110 miles of roads for the bike course – this occurred at Ironman Texas in 2018, when “Ironman record times” were clocked by Melissa Hauschildt and Matt Hanson.

Beyond the logistics, an athlete’s clock is at the mercy of Mother Nature; factors like local climate, hilly terrain, or even a current-assisted swim can skew the data (as was the case in 2021, when Blummenfelt floated downcurrent to a 39:41 swim en route to a then “Ironman record” of 7:21:12 in Cozumel).

Because of that, Ironman confirmed in 2026 that they do not keep track of “official” world records for that reason, with a spokesperson telling Triathlete, “Due to variance of courses (even from year-to-year the same race could have course alterations), combined with the fact that unlike marathons, triathlon does not have official course certification for records, we use world best, course best, race best, swim/bike/run course best, etc.”

The same holds true for Ironman 70.3 races and non-branded half-iron triathlons; no course certification, no “official” record.

For what it’s worth, the Guinness World Records recognizes the “fastest time to complete an Ironman triathlon,” but not fastest iron-distance time (which we’ll explain in the next section). This may be more a function of marketing, as Ironman has established its own “partner” page on the Guinness Book of World Records website. Guinness does recognize some other triathlon records, including “fastest lumberjack triathlon,” which raises many other questions we’ll save for a different article.

Then there’s the branding gymnastics of Ironman™ versus an iron-distance race like Challenge Roth.

You see, even though Challenge Roth is a 140.6-mile triathlon, it’s not an Ironman. Ironman is a distinct brand of 140.6-mile races, though many use the name to designate the distance (similar to how most Americans say “pass me a Kleenex” for tissue, or “I need a Band-Aid,” instead of requesting an adhesive bandage).

And though Challenge Roth has been around for decades, the course and race have evolved. In 2010, the Challenge Roth run course was adjusted to be more spectator-friendly, and the bike course has been altered several times for safety, most recently in 2024. In 2026, Roth expanded the pro bike drafting zone from 12 to 20 meters, creating entirely different race dynamics altogether.

Ultimately, establishing a definitive “record” is difficult even for a single event, given how much can change from one year to the next. And though iron-distance “records” are often broken at Challenge Roth, they’re technically not the site of “the fastest iron-distance times in history.”

In 2022, Kristian Blummenfelt and Kat Matthews covered the 140.6-mile distance in what would have been record-setting times of 6:44:25 and 7:31:54 – except that they did it in a unique setup involving pacers, drafting, and extra equipment as part of the Sub7/Sub8 Project. The event allowed for advanced technological and athletic optimizations, featuring specialized gear like prototype bikes and pace teams on the swim and bike to provide slipstreams for Blummenfelt and Matthews to draft off of — all things you’d never be allowed to do in a legitimate race

This format drew considerable criticism and debate on social media, with the community questioning whether the Sub7/Sub8 times should be viewed as legitimate records. Many referenced Jan Frodeno’s 7:27:53 time for a similar iron-distance experiment known as Tri Battle Royle, which was held on a highly modified course featuring special ramps and a perfectly mapped swim line to remove tricky turns.

Because these events were so heavily optimized, no one regards these times as actual world records — just like the much-hyped 2017 Breaking2 Nike marketing event for the marathon record. Instead, they’re chalked up to fascinating experiments for fans to geek out over. (And for that, we’re thankful.)

So now that we’ve determined there is no official world record for Ironman, iron-distance triathlon, or otherwise, we can establish there are fastest-known times, with all the caveats above.

Coinbase receives approval to become a chartered trust bank

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The new status lets the company handle payments and custody services.

Coinbase has received approval from the US Office of the Comptroller of Currency to become a nationally chartered trust bank, the crypto company announced. The designation is more limited than a full bank charter, so Coinbase won't be able to handle regular deposits or grant loans. However, the new status will allow it to conduct payments and custody services, and expand its stablecoin business.

"This is an important step forward for Coinbase and the broader crypto industry," the company said in a press release. "Coinbase has always pursued clear, consistent and best-in-class regulatory oversight that will move the financial industry, and American leadership in digital assets forward."

Other digital asset companies have sought to become trust charters, including Block and Wise, but only Circle Internet Group, Ripple and now Coinbase have been successful. Coinbase says it's the world's largest crypto custodian, and recently partnered with Citigroup to do stablecoin-to-currency conversions.

The company's past is far from squeaky-clean, though. In 2022 and 2023, the SEC sued Coinbase over the illegal sales of unregistered securities and alleged violations of securities laws, respectively. In 2023, the company settled with the state of New York for $100 million over violations related to money transmitting and other irregularities. Last year, the company was hacked and the perpetrators demanded $20 million to keep the information private.

World of Hyatt Free Night Awards: How To Earn, Redeem, Gift, & Maximize Them

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

In addition to being able to redeem World of Hyatt points for hotel stays, program members also have the opportunity to earn free night certificates, which can be redeemed for stays at hotels.

In this post, I want to talk about everything you need to know about World of Hyatt free night award certificates — how do you earn them, how can you redeem them, and what are the restrictions associated with them?

There are a variety of ways to earn World of Hyatt award free night certificates:

For some context on the potential value of these certificates:

Note that while World of Hyatt is pretty strict when it comes to the expiration date of free night awards, members can sometimes receive points in exchange for expired certificates. This isn’t a published policy, but rather is an unwritten courtesy that’s sometimes extended to members, so it’s not something you can count on, but it’s worth asking about. This generally only works for awards issued through Milestone Rewards perks, and not those issued through credit cards.

If you want to see your free night award certificates, just log into your World of Hyatt account. Once in there, click on the “Awards” section. There you’ll see how many awards you have, what kind of awards you have, and when they expire (you have to stay by the expiration date, and not just book by then).

You can generally expect that your free night awards will be deposited in your World of Hyatt account within a few weeks of completing eligible activity, though often even faster than that.

The process of redeeming your Hyatt free night awards is pretty easy, and can be done online. There are two ways you can approach this:

As you start to redeem World of Hyatt free night award certificates, there are a few things to note:

With that in mind, I think the easiest way to redeem is to just start searching on hyatt.com. Search availability for the city or hotel you want, and go through the process as if you’re trying to make a points booking, by clicking the “Use Points” button.

You’ll see the hotel results on the next page. You’ll want to select a hotel that shows the points cost as being within the range of the certificate you have (in other words, for a Category 1-4 free night certificate you’d want it to cost no more than 25,000 points per night).

For example, take the Hyatt Regency JFK Airport at Resorts World, which represents a good use of a free night certificate compared to the cash rate.

When you click through and view rates, you’ll be able to select whether you want to redeem your free night award certificate or points.

For what it’s worth, for a random one-night stay on a Friday at this hotel that I pulled up, the cheapest paid rate would be over $500 per night, including taxes and fees.

It’s pretty amazing that a free night certificate offered by the $95 annual fee World of Hyatt Credit Card can be used here.

There are a couple of situations in which you may want to book by phone:

Much like an award night booked using points, stays booked with free night award certificates are eligible for all World of Hyatt elite benefits. If you’re a Globalist member you’ll receive a suite upgrade subject to availability, complimentary breakfast, lounge access, etc. The only restriction is that you can’t redeem a suite upgrade award, club access award, or Guest of Honor award, for stays booked with free night awards (because you can’t stack certificates).

Furthermore, Hyatt stays booked with free night awards are eligible to earn elite qualifying nights, so they can help you jump to the next elite tier, or help you reach the next Milestone Rewards perk.

It’s possible to transfer your World of Hyatt free night awards to others. This is super easy to do. When you’re in the “Awards” section of your World of Hyatt account, just take a look at your free night awards.

To gift an award to another World of Hyatt member, just click the three dots next to the award you want to gift, click the “Gift Your Award” button, and then you’ll be able to transfer your award. You’ll just need the World of Hyatt number and last name of the World of Hyatt member who you’re gifting the award to.

The award will then instantly post to their account, and they can redeem it however they’d like, with the same expiration date.

I figure it makes sense to briefly share my strategy when it comes to redeeming Hyatt free night award certificates. To keep things simple, let me talk about Category 1-4 free night certificates.

While they can be redeemed at properties costing up to 25,000 points per night (in peak season), I wouldn’t value them that high, given the expiration date of the certificates (meanwhile points never expire in conjunction with activity).

Does it only make sense to redeem a Category 1-4 free night certificate at a Category 4 hotel, or under what circumstances could it make sense to redeem at a hotel that requires fewer points? My general approach is as follows:

For example, while I’ve typically used Category 1-4 free night certificates at Category 4 properties, I’ve also a couple of times redeemed them at Category 3 properties, when I wasn’t 100% sure I’d be able to use them otherwise. I think that’s ultimately still a great deal, especially if it’s a property where the cash rate would be high.

Everyone should consider their own situation, though. For example, some people consistently plan all of their travel six or more months in advance, and in that case you wouldn’t want to wait as long as I do to try to maximize certificates.

There are several ways to earn World of Hyatt free night certificates, whether it’s through the World of Hyatt Credit Card, or through the Milestone Rewards program. There’s lots of value to be had with these certificates, and I’ve consistently been able to maximize their value pretty well. Now you can even transfer them to others.

Hopefully the above answers any questions you may have about Hyatt free night award certificates, though if I missed anything, please let me know.

Memory shortage spurs smartphone makers to melt used memory off old devices, then re-solder it onto new products

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The smartphone industry is expected to see a spike in reballed memory adoption. According to a report by global market intelligence and research firm TrendForce, the current penetration rate is estimated at around 3–4%, with reballed memory primarily used in entry-level and budget smartphone models. However, this figure is expected to rise to 7–9% in 2027.

Reballed memory is recycled memory salvaged from older or discarded devices. The term reball comes from the process itself, in which the solder balls on a used memory chip are first melted so the chip can be removed from the old device, then re-soldered to a new device, thus allowing the component to be reused rather than discarded. You can see the process in action in the video below.

This offers manufacturers a cheaper alternative to sourcing new memory chips, potentially helping them keep production costs under control. Currently, reballed 4GB LPDDR4X memory modules make up the majority of such components on the market, and they are used in 4G smartphones priced below CNY 1,299 (approximately $200). This tactic is becoming increasingly important for smartphone makers, as customers are particularly sensitive to price increases in the budget segment. This leaves limited room to raise retail prices without affecting demand. Using reballed memory can help offset rising bill of materials (BOM) costs while allowing brands to keep entry-level devices affordable.

TrendForce expects DRAM prices to rise even further in the first quarter of 2027, which may put additional pressure on smartphone manufacturers. As a result, the use of recycled memory and other lower-specification components could gradually expand beyond the entry-level segment and into more premium smartphones. What is currently a cost-saving measure for budget devices could eventually become a broader strategy for manufacturers looking to manage expenses amid a prolonged period of high memory prices.The rising cost of memory is also pushing PC enthusiasts and hardware hobbyists to explore unconventional ways to reuse existing DRAM chips. Recently, an electronics hobbyist converted a relatively inexpensive $120 DDR5 SO-DIMM kit into a desktop memory kit valued at around $1,000. The project involved salvaging an SK hynix 2x 16GB DDR5-5600 SODIMM kit purchased from Facebook Marketplace and reballing the chips onto custom desktop DIMM PCBs, with the resulting modules reportedly capable of reaching DDR5-7800 speeds.

Converting laptop memory into desktop DIMMs is technically demanding and requires considerable expertise and specialized equipment. Similarly, reballing memory for use in smartphones is far from straightforward, particularly given the tiny solder connections in modern BGA (Ball Grid Array) packages. Nevertheless, both developments highlight how manufacturers, repair specialists, and hardware enthusiasts are exploring alternatives to reuse existing memory to offset rising costs and limited availability.

American Airlines Makes Hotel Vouchers Easier To Get, But Warns Of Fewer Hotel Choices

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

American Airlines is updating how it provides hotel rooms, food credits, and transportation to passengers stranded by flight disruptions. Getting help without standing in another long line is welcome. But an internal memo also warns that employees may have fewer hotel choices to offer, which leaves me wondering whether a faster voucher will necessarily mean a better night.

View From The Wing highlights a November employee memo outlining changes to American’s Hotel, Meal and Transportation system, known internally as HMT.

American was already promoting automatic disruption vouchers in 2025. The question now is whether the latest changes make that assistance more reliable and easier to use.

I recently wrote about my American Airlines flight from Chicago to Los Angeles that nearly became an overnight stay.

A 7:10 p.m. departure slipped repeatedly until the posted departure time reached 11:55 p.m. The aircraft was there, but we had no pilots. Eventually, pilots arrived from Dallas and received a chorus of cheers from passengers who were thrilled we might actually get home.

During the wait, an Admirals Club agent offered me a hotel room if I wanted to rebook for the next day. The offer came with a caveat:

I appreciated having an option, but I wanted to get to Los Angeles. By that point, an airport hotel would have meant a short night followed by another trip through security and another flight.

But it is a reason why I appreciate this new rollout. Every minute spent waiting for an agent to arrange accommodation is a minute you cannot spend sleeping. If American can deliver a usable hotel offer to your phone promptly, that is a practical improvement.

“Frontline teams may see fewer hotel choices displayed than they are accustomed to seeing.”

Yes, a shorter list of suitable properties could work perfectly well and it is too early to say whether the remaining hotel options will be worse. But I’m not all that optimistic, here.

What happens when the available hotel is far from the airport, has no functioning shuttle at that hour, or cannot accommodate a family in an appropriate room.

We are not talking about putting every stranded passenger in a luxury hotel. But I do expect a clean room and a workable way to get there and back. A voucher is useful only if the accommodation behind it is suitable.

My agent’s Hilton comment was amusing, but it also captured the uncertainty. When you accept an airline-arranged room late at night, you may have little idea what you are getting.

Uber Central allows businesses to request rides for customers who do not have the Uber app. Riders receive trip details and a tracking link by text.

That could be particularly helpful for someone who rarely uses rideshare services or is already overwhelmed by a disrupted journey.

According to the reported instructions, however, hotel shuttles remain the first option, followed by Uber vouchers. Agent-arranged rides are intended for exceptions when those options do not meet the passenger’s needs. Broad availability of the new arrangement has not yet been established.

I hope agents have enough discretion to solve the actual problem. A hotel shuttle listed in a database is no help if it stopped running an hour ago…as a practical aside, before you leave the airport terminal call the hotel to make sure that the shuttle is running.

Under American’s published customer service plan, when a disruption is caused by the airline, assistance available upon request includes:

The plan also promises reimbursement of reasonable hotel costs if American cannot provide an approved hotel voucher (with an almost impossible caveat that such authorization must be obtained in writing). For disruptions beyond its control, such as weather, American generally leaves passengers responsible for their own accommodation and meals.

If you qualify for assistance and no automatic offer arrives, ask an agent. And if you get the food/beverage voucher, realize that the $12 won’t go very far (unless you are in Denver).

American Airlines deserves credit for making disruption assistance easier to deliver. Passengers who have already lost hours to a delay should not have to lose another hour obtaining a hotel voucher.

But fewer hotel choices could limit the usefulness of that improvement. I will judge the system by whether it gets stranded passengers into suitable rooms promptly, with transportation that actually works.

On my recent Chicago flight, I was thankful the pilots arrived and I never needed the hotel. Next time, I may not be so fortunate…(or maybe next time I will fly Delta or United).

Mancini has spoken with Tresoldi over Italy call-up and says ‘I think there’s a chance’

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Roberto Mancini has confirmed that he has spoken with Club Brugge striker Nicolo Tresoldi about his international future and says that there is ‘a chance’ the 22-year-old decides to declare for Italy at senior level.

Tresoldi is currently a regular with the Germany U21 side and it is expected that he will continue playing with the German youth side until the end of the U21 European Championship coming up at the end of the 2026-27 season.

Tresoldi was born in Cagliari to an Italian father and a mother of Italian and Argentine descent, but moved to Germany as a teenager, which means that he has three options to pick from at senior international level.

The youngster boasts impressive goalscoring numbers in Belgium: He has averaged exactly a goal every other game during his time with Club Brugge (37 from 74 appearances across all competitions) and has 14 goals from 17 appearances in 2026-27 already, including five goals from four Champions League outings.

There has been a lot of hope among Azzurri supporters that Tresoldi, a childhood Milan supporter, will decide to represent Italy at senior international level.

Mancini spoke about the talented 22-year-old during his press conference at Coverciano on Monday.

The CT confirmed that both he and Claudio Ranieri have spoken to Tresoldi regarding his international future and says that ‘we hope he makes the right choice’.

“I spoke to Tresoldi, Ranieri too. But I believe that he must feel that he wants to play for the national team,” said Mancini on Monday.

“His father is Italian; he won the Euros with the U21. I think there’s a chance. He knows he can be an important player for the future; we hope he makes the right choice.”

Ken Jennings Knows Exactly Who Should Replace Him On Jeopardy (And I Don’t Hate It)

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.
