A Survivor 51 Castaway Is Not Pleased With Jeff Probst And Called Him Out On Social

People Are Worried Kimmel Will Get Canceled. Meanwhile, The Tonight Show’s Out Here Doubling Down

Michael Che Is Always Asked About The Best And Worst SNL Hosts, And I Appreciate His Thoughtful Take

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Ken Jennings isn't going anywhere as Jeopardy!'s host, but he has two famous names he's considered should he need a substitute for a day. Fans may not be surprised to hear that both of these TV personalities have been working hard amid the 2026 TV schedule. These two stars sound like entries on a wishlist, but now that Jennings has put it out there, I'd love to see both of them host the quiz series.

Jennings finally accepted the full-time hosting gig in 2023 in the aftermath of Alex Trebek's passing and various inquiries from curious fans. During a taping, Jennings was asked who'd be a fitting replacement for him in the event he has to miss shows. The first name he floated was none other than longtime Survivor host Jeff Probst. As Jennings astutely mentioned, before Probst was watching contestants battle it out in Fiji, he had a very different gig:

Well, you know who has a lot of Jeopardy! hosting experience is Jeff Probst, who hosted Rock & Roll Jeopardy! back before he was Jeff Probst. I mean, he now hosts three seasons of Survivor a year, I think, so the guy is probably busy.

Probst actually hosts two seasons of Survivor a year, both of which are filmed back-to-back in Fiji in a little under two months. As the host told Variety back in 2024, however, hosting the series is a year-round job. When he's not on the island, he's helping work on post-production, overseeing the casting process for future seasons and hosting a podcast. All this to say, he may be just as busy asJennings suggested, even if he doesn't have a third season to film.

One could argue that in the event that Probst was unavailable, Pop Culture Jeopardy! host Colin Jost could serve as a substitute. Unfortunately, for Jost, though, Jennings named-dropped the host of another hit reality TV series instead.

If Jeff Probst is too tied up in Survivor, Ken Jennings also floated RuPaul as a potential fill-in. The Drag Race host may not be any less busy than Probst, but that didn't stop the former Jeopardy! champ from name-dropping him as well:

Also, RuPaul, I think, would be pretty good.

RuPaul hasn't hosted Jeopardy or a spinoff, but all that Drag Race experience and his time mentoring many successful drag winners has to count for something. I think he'd be a great choice for a one-off, provided he has the time to commit.

It's interesting to hear Ken Jennings not mention Mayim Bialik, as she co-hosted with him for years before he become the sole host. Perhaps he's privy to some info that she's not willing to return to Jeopardy, or he just thought that would be a boring answer to give to the audience. We can only speculate.

For now, it's a non-issue. Ken Jennings is solidly in the role as host, despite pushing off full-time hosting duties for years. It's kind of hilarious, considering everyone, including Alex Trebek, assumed he'd end up in the role, and that he ultimately embraced his fate years later.

Jeopardy! airs on weekdays in syndication, so check their local listings for when and where to watch it. We'll also keep an eye out to see who may substitute for Ken Jennings should he need a day off in the near future, and if Jeff or Ru end up getting tapped for it.

Atletico Madrid willing to wait for Alvarez to hit form

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Diego Simeone is willing to be patient with Julian Alvarez, who he feels is vital to getting Atletico Madrid to where they want to be.

Alvarez has netted just twice in all competitions this season, while providing three assists.

His latest goal involvement came on Sunday, as he teed up Giuliano Simeone to open the scoring against Barcelona. The Blaugrana, though, went on to win 2-1.

Barca pushed hard to sign Alvarez this past summer, with Atletico refusing to budge and even threatening legal action against the reigning LaLiga champions.

And though Alvarez is yet to hit his peak form, Simeone is willing to wait.

"He's an extraordinary player, we love him dearly, we need him, we need the best Julian, and we're going to work with him," the Atletico boss told Movistar+.

"Of the team's five good scoring opportunities, he was involved in three or four. He just lacked the goal that would have made him stand out, but it will come."

In his post-match press conference, Simeone added of Alvarez: "We all wish he had scored, but we'll wait for him; we need him because if we want to get where we want to go, we need him, otherwise we won't get there."

Asked if Alvarez was suffering from a lack of confidence, Simeone replied: "I'm not in his body, he has our trust."

— Atlético de Madrid (@atletienglish) November 9, 2026

Alvarez led Atletico for shots (four), touches in the opposition box (seven) and successful dribbles (three), while also creating two chances.

Atletico's defeat snapped a seven-game winning run in LaLiga. They sit third, two points behind second-place Real Madrid and five adrift of leaders Barca.

"I'm left with an outstanding performance from the players, nothing to complain about," Simeone told reporters.

"We put the opposing players in a position where they weren't comfortable. We lacked the clinical finishing we've shown on other occasions, and they're a very confident opponent who capitalised on their chances.

"I'm proud of the lads, I loved it, and I'm very happy with everyone's performance."

AMD confirms GPU driver bug is wearing out your SSD – how to stop it

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

AMD has admitted a bug within its External Events Utility is wearing out SSDs, and promises a fix.

Under normal circumstances, an SSD might go all day without needing to access or write files. But for some AMD Radeon users, anything but normal circumstances are causing drives to continuously write and re-write data. The External Events Utility (EEU) that AMD installs alongside its CPU and GPU drivers is reportedly the culprit.

Also: Nvidia RTX 50 GPUs hit with more price hikes

Fortunately, there are a few workarounds available to avert disaster while AMD works on a more permanent solution.

Reports of the issue first surfaced in July on AMD’s official PC user forum, with one user stating that the External Events Client Module and EEU were constantly writing information to their SSD after installing a Radeon 9060XT graphics card. Subsequent comments on the thread this month confirm that the issue has persisted despite several bug fixes and patch updates, with two users reporting hard numbers for data usage ranging from 0.3 MB/s to as much as 1.5 MB/s.

Also: The best laptops: Expert tested and reviewed

This week, Reddit user darklinkpower posted that the AMD Adrenaline Edition desktop application also had the EEU driver bug, using up to 1% of their drive health per month for around 16 months. That might not sound like much, but it can cause catastrophic problems if left unchecked.

AMD seems to be aware of the issue; a company official who goes by AMD_Vik on Reddit commented that it will “hopefully [be] fixed in the next [update].” While there’s little indication of how quickly a permanent, official patch will be pushed to users, there are a few ways you can protect yourself from the bug and prevent your storage drive from collapsing.

This seems to be the most consistent fix for most AMD users reporting issues, but disabling the EEU can cause its own problems, such as disabling AMD FreeSync support or introducing display glitches if you hot-swap screens while your PC is powered on.

The AMD Adrenalin Edition desktop app lets you control how your PC components communicate with one another, and it might be another facet of a complex problem. By allowing your CPU to access your graphics card’s memory, you can temporarily boost performance, but the EEU bug seems to let it and your GPU run rampant, eating up every bit of memory they can find. Changing a few settings within the app may help:

Also: I tried local AI on HP’s Omnibook Ultra

This is a more niche solution that carries its own risk of introducing communication issues between your CPU and GPU, so I only recommend trying this route if disabling the EEU itself doesn’t resolve the issue. AMD’s official fix may be in the works, but without a firm timeline for release, users are left twisting in the wind trying to stop their PCs from hemorrhaging storage capacity.

Dirty Martini Gnocchi

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

When my fellow food editor Brooke Caison tried this recipe, she said, “This gnocchi skillet makes me want to drink.” So why can’t you do both? A dirty martini happens to be the perfect template to turn into a savory recipe, as we’ve riffed in many other ways: dirty martini dip, dirty martini deviled eggs, dirty martini pasta… shall I go on? This dirty martini gnocchi is my latest attempt to transform this iconic drink into dinner, and I have to admit—it’s a success.

The dirty martini:To make this recipe, you only need the vermouth you’d actually put in a dirty martini, Castelvetrano olives in brine (that’s the dirty part of the recipe), pimento peppers, garlic, lemon, Parmesan, and, of course, the gnocchi. You may also be asking: Why not use pimento-stuffed olives instead of having to buy two different ingredients? Well, Castelvetrano olives are superior in texture and flavor, so it was a no-brainer to include them. But this pasta truly wouldn’t be complete without some pimento peppers. Trust me—the extra step makes a lot of difference.

The gnocchi:Personally, I like using Colavita or DeCecco’s store-bought gnocchi. Once you cook some garlic, add the vermouth and broth, then cook the gnocchi until it swells up and gets a little tender. You don’t want to overcook it, which is why I recommend adjusting the amount of broth as you go. If you’re wondering when your sauce is done, check by seeing if you can coat the back of a spoon with it.

Storage:If you don’t end up eating the entire skillet as I would, then you can store the leftovers in an airtight container in the refrigerator for up to 3 days.

Two Brothers Avenged Their Father, Ate His Killer’s Heart, and Found the Lost Gospels. Or Did They?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The Nag Hammadi texts upended what we knew about early Christianity. But their origin story is a bit murky.

The texts now known as the Nag Hammadi Library sent shockwaves through the scholarly world when they first surfaced on Cairo’s black market for antiquities in 1946.

Up until that moment, the beliefs and practices of the early Christian sect known as the Gnostics survived only through the writings of their rivals, the orthodox sects that would to some extent evolve into the Christian faiths we know today. Those condemnations of the Gnostics pilloried the “heretics” with accusations of “wife-swapping, cannibalism, and profane Eucharists in which they consumed bodily fluids,” as National Geographic put it. But suddenly, after nearly two millennia, the rediscovered texts finally allowed the Gnostics to speak for themselves.

Composed in Coptic in the fourth century, the twelve papyrus codices contained fantastical stories that strayed wildly from orthodox Christian doctrine. As National Geographic recounted, the texts told of “elaborate myths about creation in which a lesser malevolent deity split off from the true transcendent deity.” They also taught of 74 angels, each responsible for a different part of the human body. In the Gospel of Thomas, we learned the “secret sayings” attributed to Jesus Christ, and in the Gospel of Philip, disciples lamented how frequently the Messiah lavished affection, including kisses, on Mary Magdalene.

The lost gospels were an extraordinary find, and decades later, when the man who found them was finally tracked down, his account of the discovery proved just as extraordinary. The French scholar Jean Doresse spent the late 1940s combing the Nag Hammadi region for clues about who had unearthed the codices and how. But it wasn’t until the mid-1970s that the American scholar James M. Robinson put a name to the local farmer who, Doresse had been told, dug up the texts. That man was Muhammad ‘Ali al-Samman, and the story he told Robinson involved a startling discovery and a bloody family feud.

As Robinson recounted it, at least, al-Samman’s tale would have been at home in the very kind of religious texts he’d found. Albeit, less the “Jesus in Jerusalem” kind, and more like Nephi confronting Laban.

The story goes that Muhammad ‘Ali al-Samman and his brother (accompanied by others, depending on which version of the story you hear) had ventured out to the Jabal al-Tárif archaeological site in the cliffs along the Nile, north of Nag Hammadi. The men were in search of sebakh, the decomposed remnants of mudbrick, to use as fertilizer.

During their dig, Muhammad ‘Ali uncovered a large earthenware jar. Far from taking on the role of eager amateur archeologist, however, the modest farmer reportedly found himself caught in the throws of superstition. He was resistant to disturb the vessel, lest it contain a jinn, which might bring some evil upon him. Only when Muhammad ‘Ali considered the gold and treasure which might await him inside did he smash the jar and discover the thirteen bound papyrus books within.

From there, the saga takes a dramatic turn. Al-Samman brought the books back to his home in al-Qasr, supposedly placing them on a pile of straw near the family’s oven. He could not immediately take them anywhere to be analyzed or appraised, as he had more pressing matters to attend to: avenging his father’s murder.

Not long after the texts were discovered, al-Samman’s mother passed along word to Muhammad ‘Ali and his brothers that his father’s nemesis, Ahmed Isma’il, was nearby and vulnerable. Muhammad ‘Ali and his siblings supposedly sought out the man they blamed for their father’s death, and took revenge by hacking off his limbs and devouring his heart “as the ultimate act of blood revenge.”

Knowing the police would come looking for him, Muhammad ‘Ali handed the papyri over to a Coptic Orthodox priest named al-Qummus Basiliyus Abd al-Masih for safe-keeping. Already, in a twist befitting a tale of Scheherazade, some of the texts had been lost when al-Samman’s mother ‘Umm-Ahmad burned them as fuel for her oven. While in al-Qummus’ care, the surviving papyri came to the attention of a local academic, who was unable to secure their purchase. So the lost Gospels travelled to the black markets of Cairo, where they were ultimately intercepted by Egyptian authorities, who brought them to the Coptic Museum in Cairo.

It was a remarkable tale, one that James M. Robinson would relish retelling over the years, beginning with his 1977 work The Nag Hammadi Library in English. Scholar Elaine Pagels would also recount the tale of bloody revenge and burned papyri in her deeply influential assessment of the texts, 1979’s The Gnostic Gospels. In a field of scholarship where so many discoveries are made during the shady, not to mention narratively rote, practice of “graverobbing,” here was something far more engrossing.

Which is why the “official story” merits a reasonable amount of scrutiny.

In 2013, Mark Goodacre of Duke University published a study in the Journal for the Study of the New Testament titled, “How Reliable is the Story of the Nag Hammadi Discovery?” Goodacre didn’t call into question the veracity of the documents themselves; they’d been authenticated and dated by scholars to around the fourth century. Instead, Goodacre questions Robinson's account of the discovery. Knowing exactly how the Nag Hammadi Library was found, he argues, is crucial to understanding the texts themselves: where they came from and how they survived to the present day.

Goodacre noted that inconsistencies appear even within Robinson’s recounting of the story over the years. Depending on when Robinson was retelling it, the group that first ventured out in search of sebakh was variously “two-person (1977), seven-person (1979) and eight-person (1981).” The size of the jar they found also grew over the years, with Robinson first estimating it at 60 cm, only for Pagels to later report it as “almost a meter high.” As such, Goodacre notes, “the jar has grown from just under 2 ft to well over 3 ft.” In later tellings, Pagels would increase the size even more. In a 1987 TV special called The Gnostics, Pagels is shown telling a classroom that the jar was actually six feet tall.

The same 1987 documentary shows another problem Goodacre points out: al-Samman’s own account doesn’t match Robinson’s. The filmmakers interviewed al-Samman himself, a decade after Robinson first published his story, and the version he gave on camera was very different. Speaking through an interpreter, al-Samman said he had been alone when he found the jar. “I smashed the pot on my own and inside I found these old books,” he says “Then I brought the others over to see.”

One could argue that al-Samman’s memory had simply faded in the decade since he spoke with Robinson, which would explain the discrepancy. But that argument cuts both ways. Robinson interviewed al-Samman some 30 years after the discovery, so the version Robinson recorded may have been just as distorted by time.

Ultimately, Goodacre observed, there’s ample reason to be skeptical of the story. There’s little to back it up, and plenty of red flags that present themselves at even the slightest probing. Yet, Robinson’s story continues to be perpetuated by scholars even now (with Goodacre taking time to note the blatant plagiarism in some retellings, including the phrase “the ultimate act of blood revenge”).

Even in the field of theological study, where scholars are meant to turn a skeptical eye to the religious texts of the past, it seems we humans can’t resist adding a bit of flair to our origin stories.

Michale Natale is a News Editor for the Hearst Enthusiast Group. As a writer and researcher, he has produced written and audio-visual content for more than fifteen years, spanning historical periods from the dawn of early man to the Golden Age of Hollywood. His stories for the Enthusiast Group have involved coordinating with organizations like the National Parks Service and the Secret Service, and travelling to notable historical sites and archaeological digs, from excavations of America’ earliest colonies to the former homes of Edgar Allan Poe.

Outlook.com bounces legit emails as Microsoft's authentication crackdown goes sideways

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Missing DMARC or wobbly SPF means your mail may be treated as suspicious

A validation error has stopped Outlook.com from accepting legitimate emails.

The problem appears widespread and, according to reports from affected users, can result in non-delivery reports with error codes related to SPF (Sender Policy Framework) validation failures.

Other users have reported errors highlighting a lack of DMARC (Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance) information.

The troubles began at the end of last week and appear to be the result of Microsoft tightening the rules (or at least attempting to do so) for accepting emails. The company's consumer email services are affected, and it appears that if a domain doesn't have working SPF or if it has a missing DMARC record, then Outlook.com and its ilk will treat it as suspicious and not accept emails.

The rules are designed to deal with spam, bulk senders, and the like. In 2025, Microsoft announced new rules designed to strengthen email authentication for domains sending more than 5,000 emails per day. In addition to DMARC and SPF compliance, DKIM (DomainKeys Identified Mail) was also required.

Although this seemed like a good idea to ward off spammers, small senders now appear to be affected. According to complainants in the Outlook forum, a single email with just one line might now be rejected. Furthermore, Microsoft has not issued any advisory to customers explaining how the rules have changed.

Although some customers insist their DMARC, SPF, and DKIM settings are compliant, many more have reported that once they set them to Microsoft's preferences, email began flowing once more.

SPF must pass for the sending domain (the domain's DNS record should accurately list authorized IP addresses/hosts). DKIM must pass to validate email integrity and authenticity. DMARC must have at least p=none and align with either SPF or DKIM (preferably both).

The Register asked the Windows giant for more information, but we have yet to receive a response.

To make matters worse, the glitch also appears to be intermittent. A resend attempt might succeed following a rejection. To compound the situation, the rejection error message may also be vague about what caused the bounce in the first place.

A moderator in Microsoft's support forum acknowledged the bug and said it was "classified as a service degradation."

"Microsoft is actively investigating the underlying cause of this email delivery issue, and engineering teams are actively analyzing service telemetry and customer-provided data to identify the source of the issue."

Still. It could be worse. Earlier today, several users in Europe, the Middle East, and Africa were affected by another Microsoft whoopsie – a "recent service change" that caused some connections to be abruptly terminated. Outlook on the web, as well as the Purview and Defender portals were all affected.

Microsoft reverted the change in the Exchange Online front-end infrastructure, and all was well.

It has yet to say what else, if anything, might have changed in its email services to cause legitimate messages to bounce back. ®

Bangladesh Factories Grind to a Halt Amid Wage Protests. What’s Next?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The Mirpur area of Bangladesh’s capital, Dhaka, is usually a hive of activity. One of the country’s five major manufacturing hubs, it is home to more than 300 garment factories, making clothes for global brands and retailers including Walmart, H&M, Zara, Levi’s, Marks & Spencer and Primark. But on Thursday, the last working day of the week in Bangladesh, the area was eerily quiet.

The calm comes after a stormy week, which saw workers take to the streets to protest the national minimum wage, demanding the formation of a government-led Minimum Wage Board and a substantial raise from BDT 12,500 (around $100) to BDT 30,000 (around $240) per month. On November 2, more than 600 of the country’s 4,000 garment factories were forced to suspend operations for the day due to protests. By Tuesday, one worker had been killed in Gazipur, another major industrial hub, as law enforcement agencies moved to contain the unrest, which had spread across major garment manufacturing centers, disrupting factory operations and traffic. Police say more than 100 people were arrested. And by Wednesday, there was a slight reprieve: protests temporarily subsided as the government announced a Minimum Wage Board, and declared that a revised minimum wage structure for garment workers will take effect in December, with higher payments reflected in January payslips.

While the Minimum Wage Board is unlikely to meet protestors’ BDT 30,000 demands ($240), its formation is seen as a major step forward. Wage structure for garment workers hasn’t changed since 2023. “We can say our first demand has been met, as a wage board has been formed and they said that the new wage will take effect in December,” says Sajal Sardar, a sewing machine operator at a knitwear factory in Mirpur. Sardar is one of around four million garment workers in Bangladesh, more than half of whom are women. Despite serving as a cornerstone of the national economy, these workers continue to live in difficult conditions.

Minimum wage protests in Bangladesh – and the response from police – have caused significant disruption in key garment making districts.

A decade ago, 23-year-old Sardar started working in factories as a child. During that time, he has been unable to build up any savings, as the minimum wage is so scant. He belongs to a family of seven, in which he and his brother are the only earners, bringing home a combined monthly income of around BDT 30,000. He personally earns BDT 14,200 ($115) of that — or BDT 17,000-18,000 ($136-144) with overtime. At times, the family is forced to borrow money to meet unexpected expenses, such as medical costs and younger siblings’ tuition fees.

Like many of the workers involved, Sardar sees protesting as a last resort, prompted by years of unmet government promises and rising living costs. “We don’t want to take to the streets,” he says, “but there is no affordable healthcare for us. Landlords keep raising house rents every January. The prices of staple food items and cooking gas have gone up several times during this period, and our wages have remained the same.”

The latest round of protests follow intense clashes throughout 2023, after which the former Bangladeshi dictator Sheikh Hasina was ousted in a student-led popular uprising. An interim government led by Nobel laureate Muhammad Yunus took office in 2024, and was replaced by the current government following elections in February 2026. When Yunus was installed, the new government introduced a legal requirement to form a wage board that would review minimum wages every three years, replacing the previous five-year interval. But progress has been slower than expected. “The government should have initiated the process by forming the wage board six months before the ending of the three-year tenure,” says Sardar.

Experts say the aftershocks of the protests will be felt across the garment industry for years to come.

Unions support the decision to determine a new minimum wage in December, says Khairul Mamun Mintu, a union leader at the Bangladesh Garment Workers Trade Union Center. But if the proposed restructure fails to meet protestors’ demands, the fight will continue. “We have placed our demands. Now, stakeholders will decide what would be a reasonable wage for all. “If that also doesn’t work, we will have to take to the streets again.”

It’s been a difficult few years for Bangladesh’s economy, casting considerable doubt over whether the proposed minimum monthly wage of BDT 30,000 ($240) is achievable. Although the country’s export earnings rose 6% to $17.04 billion in the third quarter, the trade deficit widened by 31% year-on-year to $7.48 billion, as the war in the Middle East sent its fuel import bill soaring. The new Minimum Wage Board will need to balance these considerations with workers’ demands.

Projections by independent institutions also point to mounting economic challenges. The Asian Development Bank (ADB) has lowered its growth forecast for Bangladesh for the current year to 4%, while projecting inflation at 9% (experts warn that the actual rate could be as high as 20%). Meanwhile, the World Bank has also sharply downgraded Bangladesh’s growth forecast for the 2026-27 full year to 3.4%, down from 4.6% in June and 6.1% in January.

This downward spiral has hit fashion manufacturing hard. The new government, led by Tarique Rahman of the Bangladesh Nationalist Party (BNP), which assumed office following the February 12 election, has raised fuel prices twice, in April and September, resulting in a cumulative increase of more than 35%. And electricity prices at the retail level also rose by more than 16% in June. Meanwhile, retail Liquefied Petroleum Gas (LPG) prices have increased in two consecutive hikes over the past two months, climbing from BDT 1,625 ($13) to BDT 2,060 ($16) per 12 kilogram cylinder. In March, the same cylinder cost BDT 1,340 ($10).

All of this means fashion manufacturers are grappling with a tight squeeze on prices, even before potential wage increases come into play. If brands aren’t willing to pay more to accommodate these cost hikes, it’s likely that workers will bear the brunt.

The latest wave of protests has caused substantial losses for factory owners, who manufacture clothes for global fashion brands at relatively low prices. ABM Shamsuddin, a factory owner who represents manufacturers on the newly formed six-member wage board, said the unrest has caused shipment losses worth BDT 850 million (around $68.3 million).

International buyers tend to pursue three options in these circumstances, he says. Ideally, they would accept delayed shipments without imposing penalties, but more likely they will demand that products be delivered at a 50% discount, or ask factories to send the shipments by air rather than sea shipping. Either choice would be a significant financial hit for the factory owner. Instead, he hopes global brands acknowledge that the unrest was beyond factory owners’ control. “They can see what is happening here.”

The Clean Clothes Campaign (CCC), a global network of labor unions and non-governmental organizations, is urging global fashion brands sourcing from Bangladesh to support garment workers’ demands. Ideally, brands would also publicly commit to continue sourcing from Bangladesh and ring-fence labor costs, or offer commercial terms that would enable suppliers to pay the new minimum wage, reassuring the government and employers that higher wages would not drive production to other countries. “It is crucial that brands act immediately and speak out in support of workers’ demands rather than waiting until the wage negotiations are over,” the organization’s living wage coordinator Anne Bienias said in a statement.

According to Mahmud Hasan Khan, president of the Bangladesh Garment Manufacturers and Exporters Association (BGMEA), the apex body representing garment manufacturers, protests tend to erupt whenever the time comes to revise workers’ wages. Such persistent unrest has undermined international buyers’ confidence in Bangladeshi manufacturers, but angst is misdirected toward protestors and factories. “They (the buyers) can convey their concerns to the government in writing,” says Khan. “Both their interests and ours are at stake.”

The bigger issue is the lack of collective bargaining mechanisms inside factories, adds Kalpona Akter, president of the Bangladesh Garment and Industrial Workers Federation (BGIWF). “If workers could negotiate directly with factory owners inside the factories, they wouldn’t have to take to the streets,” she says. Often, by the time the government has reviewed wages and made incremental changes, progress is obsolete, she adds, pointing to the interim government’s previous attempt to raise the annual wage increment for garment workers from 5% to 9%. “Since the basic salary is quite low, the increment does not make much difference. Commodity prices rise at a pace that wages cannot keep up with.”

Union leaders say that while manufacturers and the government are responsible for ensuring workers’ welfare, international buyers also have a share of the responsibility. A report published in May by the CCC and Public Eye found that global fashion brands were paying almost the same prices per item as they did 20 years ago, with some prices even 30% lower in inflation-adjusted terms. This, the report said, “undermines suppliers’ economic resilience and depresses wages”, adding that buyers still source a standard cotton T-shirt for around $2-3 per piece, with some paying as little as $1.

This race to the bottom is compounded by the country’s low labor costs, says Akter: “[Brands] enjoy the bigger piece of the pie here.”

Moving forward, Akter says manufacturers should first increase workers’ wages and then negotiate with buyers to ensure that the additional costs are reflected in purchasing prices. “Otherwise, buyers will stand firm on their position, manufacturers will keep bleeding, and workers will keep protesting on the streets.”

The USDA Thinks I Can Live on $65 a Week. Can I?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Welcome to The Receipt, a series documenting how Bon Appétit readers eat and what they spend doing it. Each food diary follows one reader’s week of expenses related to groceries, restaurant meals, coffee runs, and every bite in between. In this time of rising food costs, The Receipt reveals how folks—from different cities, with different incomes, on different schedules—are figuring out their food budgets.

In today’s special edition of The Receipt, we're unmasking the diarist and following James Beard Award–winning writer Paul Greenberg—a self-described cheapskate—on his quest to live on the USDA's Thrifty Food Plan.

My friends sometimes mistake me for a survivor of the Great Depression and not a Gen X-er raised in a household free from deprivation. I don't just bargain hunt. I bargain scour as if it were 1931.

This habit stays with me no matter if I’ve got a book on the bestseller list or if the stock market has just tanked. As a lifelong fisherman, I love angling for opportunities lolling in the currents of American commerce. I can’t stand the idea of buying a $5 gallon of milk when I know that the same milk from the same cows is lurking at a supermarket across the street for under three bucks.

With the inflationary surge of recent years, my friends have at last started to take a curiosity in the methods of my madness. According to the US Bureau of Labor Statistics, the price of sirloin steak is up 21% since January 2024. Romaine lettuce? 27%. Even a gosh darn cup of coffee will cost you over 50% more in 2026 than it did when the Kansas City Chiefs last won the Super Bowl. So when Bon Appétit asked my cheapskate self if I could get through a week of groceries for below what the USDA stipulates as its “thrifty food plan,” I smiled the knowing smile of an old-timer. Bon Appétit and USDA, I accept your challenge.

The USDA established the Thrifty Food Plan in 1975 and updated it in 2021 in an attempt to model a healthy diet that hits all the food groups. The TFP, as we’ll call it here forward (to economize on words in these lean times), proposes different shopping lists for different genders and age groups. I took those 2021 numbers and adjusted them for 2026 inflation. As a man in his 50s, I was given a total allotment of $64.90 per week, and recently, I took to the weird yet familiar corners all over New York City at which I procure my groceries. Using my usual shopping list, I mostly beat those numbers across the board—without, as US Agriculture Secretary Brooke Rollins recently suggested, resorting to a nutritionally suspect “$3 a meal for a piece of chicken, a piece of broccoli, you know, a corn tortilla and one other thing.”

However, the experiment raised a deeper question: Should I have to? But first, here’s how I did it.

Out of habit or laziness, many of us have a favorite grocery store. In an era of rapacious capitalism that has pushed so many Americans toward insolvency, there can be no favorites. Rather, it pays to seek out places where merciless competition drives major chains toward a common bargain basement. In Manhattan, where I live, I call these places “golden corners”—spots where Trader Joe’s, Target, and the rising star of discount grocery chains, Lidl, cluster together around a single intersection.

I’m able to easily deduce which place offers the lowest prices and run between them in a single shopping outing. While I’ll occasionally venture elsewhere for my meat and dairy (see: Chinatowns, featured later in this article), I find a good cutthroat corner serves me best when looking for animal protein.

Note: There is no beef on this list. For climate change reasons, I’ve never been a fan of the world’s most carbon-intensive protein, and to my mind, the $3-cheaper-per-pound ground turkey works just fine in a Bolognese. Also note that neither the TFP nor I put any cheese on our lists either. I’m not sure why TFP refrained, but after beef, cheese is one of the most climate-impactful animal proteins on the planet. The environmentalist in me also says cut the cheese.

Professional economizers often encourage us to choose vegetables and fruits with longer shelf lives that can bang around in our fridges until we’re ready to eat them. I’ve found picking the cheapest fruits and vegetables when they’re available is ultimately a better deal. For this, I turn to a staple of American urban street life: the fruit cart.

Fruit cart vendors, common in New York, Los Angeles, Miami, and Chicago, buy produce wholesale and offer deep discounts, especially when shelf life is short. My go-to cart guy in Lower Manhattan nearly always has a $1 special (cauliflower, cucumbers, asparagus, berries of all kinds) and the relationship we’ve built means he steers me toward what’s good.

What if the nearly overripe berries go off? To this I say: There is no berry too off that can’t be made into jam (which saves you in the miscellaneous category at this article’s end). As for salad greens, while the mainstream shopping world wrings its hands over cyclosporiasis, I’ve found that joining a community garden circumvents the dreaded disease while also purging the scourge of inflation from your wallet. My community garden costs me about $50 a year in membership fees, seeds, and fertilizer, plus about an hour of my time a week in tending. The buy-in amounts to roughly 50¢ per salad.

Whatever fruit carts and my garden can’t supply, I usually find at great prices in Manhattan’s Chinatown, likely because vendors have connections to a handful of small farms and wholesalers that operate outside of most mainstream supermarkets. According to the California Migration Museum, there are roughly 50 Chinatowns across America in nearly every major American city, which is to say that a lot of the country should have access to one.

Note: Even with bargain basement shopping, I only barely hit the USDA fruit mark. This was because nearly half of the USDA’s fruit budget is juice, which, to my mind, is a bit like calling ketchup a vegetable.

The TFP drives a hard bargain on grains, expecting the home shopper to plunk down $3.76 for an entire week’s worth of “refined-grain staple grains," which include rice, pasta, bread, tortillas, and the like. That’s tough when a loaf of Wonder Bread at Walmart costs you $2.47. Add a single 1-lb. box of pasta and it could send you over budget.

There’s a bit more give in the "whole-grain staple grains" category, which allows $6.94. But even a whole wheat loaf from Walmart (priced at $2.97) eats up just over half of my weekly allotment.

The only way I was able to snag the bread, rice, and pasta in my weekly shop and stay under budget was by leaning on another Asian country and by working hard on my home turf.

Patel Brothers, a national chain of Indian grocery stores, sells brown and white rice as well as beans of all flavors and provenances for prices that range from 80¢ to $2 per pound. As for bread, Target’s house brand of whole wheat flour is $2.69 for a 5-lb. bag. About a pound and a quarter of it makes a loaf. Weekly sandwich bread therefore costs out at 67¢ per loaf.

Last and actually also least: pasta. Nobody but nobody beats Trader Joe’s buck a pound.

Shopper beware—the last-minute, forgotten miscellaneous item is often the thing that stings you most at the register. Salt, sugar, cooking oil, and coffee purchased last minute, at even a cheap store, can be double what you’d pay if you are keeping careful track of who's charging what at your local golden corner. Keeping a passive list in your head of where the best buys on miscellany live helps these kinds of rookie mistakes from happening.

Additionally, as above, a little home elbow grease can be surprisingly effective in the condiment aisle. Fruit cart fruit made into jam and an immersion-blended home mayonnaise (a commitment of two minutes max) cost you pennies. And then there’s online. I have yet to find a better deal on coffee than on Amazon.

So I did it. I filled my fridge, stocked one shelf of my pantry, and even had a little left to treat myself to breakfast after this exhausting pan-city forage. Here’s what the final numbers looked like:

Does this mean the USDA’s stingy allowance is appropriate for these ever more expensive times? That anyone can be a successful cost-cutter like me?

Um, no. My kids are grown. I’m a freelance writer with weird hours. I live in a vast, quirky city with odd corners that can cost out cheaper than parts of the developing world. A busy working mom in a food desert who can’t commit to a community garden would find the ordeal a heavy lift.

Thank you for joining me on this ride through my own personal Great Depression. But as for you, USDA, I think it’s time you upped your budget. Stop cutting things like food stamps and healthcare. Perhaps the newest iteration of Congress will be more inclined to make this happen than its predecessor. We are, after all, among the wealthiest sovereign nations in the world.

C’mon, America! Don’t be such a cheapskate.

The $2.85B Binance hackers lost shows why the UK needs stablecoin freeze rules

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The Binance hack proved stablecoin freezes work. Now the UK should make clear freeze rules a condition of listing coins.

Opinion by Lissele Pratt, co-founder, Capitalixe

Last weekend, the Federal Bureau of Investigation claimed that North Korea was behind the $6.3 billion Binance hack, which analysts are calling the largest heist on record.

While I’ve read a lot about the scale of this heist, far less has been said about what went right.

Because while it is true that the perpetrators stole $6.3 billion, they haven’t actually been able to hold on to a significant proportion of those takings. $2.85 billion of the funds has been frozen.

The reason for this comes down to what the stolen money was comprised of. Of that $2.85 billion, 94% was held in stablecoins.

While this hack was terrible news all around, it did show that centralized control, oftentimes criticized by crypto purists, might actually be one of the strongest tools we have against crypto crime.

Because, unlike other assets, stablecoins can be frozen by their issuers, giving victims a real chance to stop stolen money reaching the wrong hands.

It does, however, raise an uncomfortable question. If private companies have the power to freeze billions of dollars in assets, who decides when they should be allowed to use it?

The United Kingdom now has the opportunity to do just that.

To get an idea of just how much the freeze mattered, you just have to look at what the hackers did within the first hour of their heist.

Related: Binance, Polymarket seek Polygon hard fork to recover $6.8M in stolen crypto

Clearly, they understood that there was a very limited window to move the stablecoins before anyone could stop them. So that’s exactly what they did.

What was frozen from the Binance hack. Source: Elliptic

Less than an hour after the hack began, they had already sold $325 million worth of stolen USDT (Tether’s stablecoin) for Ether. The reasoning behind this was to swap an asset that could be frozen with one that could not.

Luckily, stablecoin issuers were quick to act, with Tether’s first major freeze coming just 37 minutes after the stolen funds reached the hackers’ main wallet, and Circle following three minutes later, blocking the movement of USDC.

Without Tether and Circle intervening, far more of the funds could have ended up beyond their reach.

The freeze, to its credit, worked for the most part because a handful of companies decided, in real time, to use it.

The problem is that there still isn’t an agreed standard that outlines when a freeze is justified or how quickly the response needs to be, and we’re beginning to see the consequences of not having these answers.

For instance, Circle is facing a proposed class action from Drift Protocol investors who allege it failed to freeze around $230 million in stolen USDC after the April hack. Their argument is that Circle could have stopped the money from moving, but didn’t act in time.

Meanwhile, Tether is being sued in New York by two businessmen who claim it froze millions of their assets months before a seizure warrant existed. It’s important to mention that Tether has responded to this case, calling these allegations baseless.

Working in payments and banking, it’s not uncommon for me to see traditional banks freeze money and accounts. Due to this, you may argue that if a bank can do this frequently, why shouldn’t a stablecoin issuer be able to?

Stablecoin freeze timeline. Source: CertiK

My argument isn’t that they shouldn’t. But banks don’t get to decide they want to freeze assets out of nowhere. There’s usually a legal trigger behind their decision, as well as a way to challenge it.

Stablecoin issuers now hold the same power while having to act faster and across borders. So it’s about time they’re given some structure.

There is, however, a caveat with regulating stablecoin issuers. Tether and Circle, two of the largest issuers, aren’t based in the UK, so British regulators can’t simply tell them what to do.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

What Britain can control, though, is which stablecoins UK customers are allowed to buy, and they’ve already begun doing so.

In June, the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) published its cryptoassets regime. Under it, UK trading platforms must carry out due diligence and publish a disclosure document before they list a coin, and that includes stablecoins issued overseas. It comes into force on 25 October 2027.

The Bank of England is finalizing its Code of Practice for systemic stablecoin issuers by the end of this year. It covers stablecoins used widely enough for payments to matter to UK financial stability.

Neither framework establishes the clear obligations around freezing stolen funds that I believe are necessary. But both are still taking shape, giving the UK the perfect opportunity to address this gap now, rather than trying to retrofit new requirements later.

So here’s a solution that I believe would work. Before a stablecoin can be sold on a UK platform, its issuer should have to agree to two conditions.

The first being that it is contactable and ready to act. Tether and Circle did exactly that last week, but the victim was the world’s biggest exchange. A small UK platform should get the same response.

I work with a number of smaller crypto businesses that don’t have Binance’s profile or a direct line to Tether. Under the current system, they’re the ones most likely to be left waiting.

The second is that there needs to be a clear timetable for what happens next. If an issuer freezes funds, it should notify the National Crime Agency that same day. The freeze should then expire if there hasn’t been a court order put out to keep the wallet frozen.

Neither of these are extremely controversial takes. They’d give law enforcement a chance to intervene when money is stolen and also protect people from having their assets frozen indefinitely, without legal grounds.

The Binance hack has shown us that stablecoins can give victims of crypto crime a chance to stop stolen money before it’s gone for good. That shouldn’t be a privilege reserved only for the world’s biggest exchanges.

How To Preload GTA 6 In Full On Xbox Series X|S (Even If You Haven't Bought It)

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

All 200GB+ goes live in just a few hours' time

The official preload for GTA 6 is literally just a few hours away at the time of writing, and it means everyone will be able to install all 200GB+ of the game on Xbox Series X and Xbox Series S ahead of launch on November 19th.

Here are the official preload / preinstall times for GTA 6 on Xbox Series X|S, as we recently highlighted on Pure Xbox:

News GTA 6 Download Size & Preload Times Officially Revealed For Xbox Series X|S

So, there are two ways to preload GTA 6 on Xbox Series X|S depending on whether you have or haven't bought it yet. If you've pre-ordered the game, payment should have now been taken out of your account, and you'll be able to preload it by going to the Microsoft Store app on your Xbox console and searching for Grand Theft Auto VI.

The page you're looking for is the one in the image at the top of this article, although instead of a "pre-order" button, you should see the ability to install the game if you've bought it already. This will install a 300MB placeholder file until 12am GMT on November 10th, after which time the full 200GB download will become available as an update.

If you run into any issues with this, it's worth doing a restart of your console after the full preload goes live.

"On XBOX Series X|S, pre-loading begins globally on Tuesday, November 10 at 12 a.m. GMT. Code-in-box versions for both consoles should be available from retailers beginning November 12. The pre-load file will require up to 212 GB depending on your device, so make sure you've got ample space to begin pre-loading as soon as possible."

If you haven't bought GTA 6 yet but still want to preload it on Xbox Series X|S, you can do so by opening up the Xbox mobile app, going to the "Store" page (represented by a bag icon) and searching for Grand Theft Auto VI. You're specifically looking for "Grand Theft Auto VI" - not the Bundle version, but the standalone version.

In our case, it showed up as the fourth option down - you should see a variant of the following page when you open it:

Again, hitting the install button prior to 12am GMT on November 10th will install a small placeholder file, and then the full 200GB+ download will become available when the full game preload goes live.

That means everyone (except Xbox One owners, of course) has the ability to download GTA 6 on their Xbox consoles later today, so be sure to clear some room ahead of time - it's going to take up a heck of a lot of storage space!

JBL's Latest Portable Party Speaker Is $150 Off in This Early Black Friday Deal

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

Woot has dropped the price of the JBL PartyBox 330 to $479.95 as an early Black Friday deal, saving you $150 and bringing it to its lowest price to date, according to price trackers. For comparison, the same speaker currently costs $629.95 at Amazon, although that purchase includes a 90-day free trial of Amazon Music. Woot, meanwhile, offers free shipping for Prime members ($6 otherwise), and covers the new unit with a 90-day limited warranty.

Beyond the discount, the PartyBox 330 has plenty to offer if you're looking for a speaker that can handle everything from backyard barbecues to larger gatherings—sitting between the smaller Club 120 and the larger PartyBox 520 in JBL's lineup, it delivers deep, punchy bass and enough volume to entertain a crowd, while its roughly 20-hour battery life means you can keep the music going all day without having to find an outlet. It's also rated IPX4 for water resistance, so a little rain or an accidental splash shouldn't be a problem.

That protection has limits, though—the 330 isn’t dustproof or particularly impact-resistant, so you’ll still want to be careful where you set it down. Audio-wise, sound quality also changes depending on where you're standing, with music becoming noticeably more muffled from the sides and behind. Fortunately, JBL's graphic equalizer and sound presets give you some control over the tuning. Its two microphone inputs and a guitar input also make it suitable for karaoke nights or live performances. You also get Bluetooth, USB-C audio, and an optical input for connecting it to a TV, along with Auracast support if you want to link it with other compatible JBL speakers for a larger setup.

Yes, Amazon has Black Friday sales, but prices aren’t always what they seem. Use a price tracker to make sure you’re getting the best deal, or refer to guides like our live blog that use price trackers for you. And if you have an Amazon Prime membership, make the most of it.

Nowadays, both large retailers and small businesses compete for Black Friday shoppers, so you can expect practically every store to run sales through Monday, November 30, 2026. The “best” sales depend on your needs, but in general, the biggest discounts tend to come from larger retailers who can afford lower prices: think places like Amazon, Walmart, Target, Best Buy, and Home Depot. You can find all the best sales from major retailers on our Black Friday coverage.

In short, yes, Black Friday still offers discounts that can be rare throughout the rest of the year. If there’s something you want to buy, or you’re shopping for gifts, it’s a good time to look for discounts on what you need, especially tech sales, home improvement supplies, and fitness tech. Of course, if you need to save money, the best way to save is not to buy anything.

Cisse discusses humble beginnings, choosing Milan, Italy call-up, Amorim and amazing form

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Alphadjo Cisse has shed some light on his journey to becoming an AC Milan player, as well as what his first few months at the club have been like.

It has been a simply remarkable rise for Cisse, who at the start of 2026 was playing in the second division with Catanzaro, on loan from Hellas Verona. Milan bought him from there but left him with his loan club, before a serious injury needed surgery and ruled him out for the rest of the season.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

Fast-forwarding to early November, and the 20-year-old is currently the top scorer for the Rossoneri with eight goals, the most recent of which came in Sunday’s 2-1 win over Genoa. There is talk of an imminent renewal already, while Roberto Mancini gave him a senior Italy call-up.

Cisse gave an exclusive interview to Cronache di Spogliatoio in which he touched upon various topics from his upbringing to the brilliant moment he is currently experiencing. MilanNews relayed his words.

“My life changed in my father’s factory. A Verona manager went to meet him at work to beg him to take me to their place. He’d been a worker forever: every day he’d leave work at 5:00pm, and by 5:15 he was already outside our house, calling me and taking me to play soccer.

“I still remember the first time. It was afternoon. He came up to the house and said, ‘Come on, I’ll take you somewhere’. And we went to the pitch. He was the one who instilled in me the passion for soccer, and from that moment on, I’ve never stopped.

“My parents made so many sacrifices to support me on this journey: my mom, when she had days off from work, would come up with me to take me on the bus to practice.”

“My mom is a very demanding mom. But she helps me stay focused and prevent fame from overwhelming me. So everything becomes easier with her. They tell me every day that they’re proud of me, but they also do everything they can to keep me grounded.

“Carlos, Milan’s assistant coach, often tells me this too: ‘ Surround yourself with people who truly love you and who help you stay focused’. I’m sure of this: I carry with me the carefree attitude of the child I was. I play with a smile. It’s hard not to have that in these first few months.”

You have just been called up by the Italian national team for the first time…

“I was on my way to do the pre-match interview against Ferencváros in the Europa League and they stopped me to say, ‘Alpha, they’ve called you up to the national team… the senior team!’ I had the match, I only realised afterwards.

“I want to prove I can compete. The call was in the air, they’d been talking to me about it for days… but this is my big chance. I haven’t had the chance to stop and think about my career. Football moves fast.”

“A few weeks ago, they asked me how much of a footballer I’d shown. I said 70%. Can I say that? Maybe I need to lower it a bit… let’s say 40%, because I have so many aspects to improve on, but at the same time, I know who I am and what I’m worth.

“Last season, Aquilani – who was my coach at Catanzaro – told me that ‘if you don’t play at least 10 years in Serie A, I think you’ll have failed’. I took it as a positive motivation, a phrase that pushed me to always do better and believe in what I can do.

“One of my friends in football is Favasuli, I met him just last year in Serie B. We often found ourselves talking… daydreaming. I still remember the day he said to me: ‘Think about when one day we’ll face two great teams’.

“I smiled. ‘It would be great to meet again in Serie A, but maybe it’s a little too late for me, I’m already 23…’ Instead, he made it too: Milan-Napoli will be in December, we’ll see. He was my driver, he drove me to camp. He’s proof that there’s no age to stop feeling young or believing in it, no matter the path.”

Your path has already taken you from Verona to Catanzaro and to Milan…

“In those very months, my life changed again. No longer in my father’s factory. In a matter of days, I had gone from wondering how far I was from Serie A to finding myself facing the most important decision of my career.

“I’ll be honest: I didn’t take that moment very well. When I arrived at Catanzaro, I had only played two five-minute games in Serie A, including against AC Milan. Aquilani and manager Polito really wanted me, they pushed me. It was my first experience away from home.

“In just a few months, I scored a lot, everyone wanted me. And in January, I was practically on a flight to Holland, bound for Rotterdam: to sign with PSV. But then AC Milan came along.”

“My friends’ WhatsApp group exploded: ‘Alpha, is it true?!’ And I was like, ‘Yes guys, yes!’ I didn’t want to make the wrong choice: PSV wanted me right away, while Milan would have let me finish the season in Serie B.

“Two great teams, but Milan… how do you explain that? I was experiencing a mix of emotions; my parents told me to stay calm and that the best was yet to come. We decided this was the right choice. And judging by how things are going, I’d say we were right.”

“The first few times I came to Milanello, I did so to recover from my injury. Milan left me at Catanzaro, yes, but after a few days I got injured. In fact, I had serious doubts: ‘I’m coming to AC Milan after an injury, let’s see how it goes’.

“This is exactly what Amorim spoke to me about as soon as we met. We focused on the injury, and he explained what he expected of me on the pitch. He’s a great guy, and he knows how to deal with us young players. And he knows how to keep me grounded. Even though I’m not someone who likes to let my mind wander.”

You kept a promise after your Europa League hat-trick…

“Even after my hat-trick in the Europa League, I have to say that everything was already clear to me: it was simply confirmation to myself that I can compete at this level. I didn’t see it as a point of arrival, but as a promise kept to the child I was.

“And above all, as a promise to keep. Even though I was coming back from a long injury, even though I’m young and have so many areas to improve, I never had any doubts about my abilities or where I could go. I’m very demanding of myself, just like my mother.”

“Modrić gave me some advice on what I need to improve: I need to read the game better and understand the moments of the match. He’s a great player, a spectacular person, willing to help you learn, and interesting to listen to.

“Even though my idol was very different as a kid: Paul Pogba, when he was at Juventus. We do different things on the pitch, and his haircut certainly didn’t go unnoticed by a kid… maybe I’ll get one in the future! What I appreciated about him was that he made people laugh: that’s what I want to do too.

“I was inspired by the player whose jersey I had as a child: Clarence Seedorf. My mother gave it to me; it was the one I wore on the pitch. So maybe it was just my destiny to come here. Seedorf became an idol at San Siro.

“The place where you feel the grandeur, the pressure, the feeling of playing in a stadium that represents a childhood dream. Being there now is like saying to the child I was: ‘See? You did it’. It’s a huge motivation for me. But I’m carefree enough not to be affected by it.

“When you start playing, you notice it less and you don’t pay attention: it’s easier. The fans are incredibly attached to the team; you notice it not just in Milan, but all over the world. And that’s how you understand where you are. I was really impressed by the organization here.”

Do you like to play with a certain freedom?

“I’m very carefree when I go on the pitch. Whether it’s playing in Serie B or going to San Siro, I think it’s normal for me. I’m the same person I always am. As a kid, I was obsessed with bicycle kicks; I scored five or six incredible goals… now I avoid them!

“In the Verona youth team, I had a coach, Paolo Sammarco, who shaped the way I play today: he gave me a lot of freedom, helping me understand how I should express myself.”

Is it important for young Italian players to have confidence?

“Italy is full of strong young players, I assure you. It all depends on the opportunities you’re given. Many have emerged in recent months, including me, and I’m sure many more will emerge in the near future.

“When you reach a certain level, you’re amazed: one above all, I was struck by Pisilli, he’s simply amazing. I knew him, but when I played with him, I understood his strength. And I understood my own strength when, in my early years in Verona, I approached the big boys.

“The level was rising, but I continued to have faith, to understand that I could achieve great things if I followed the path I had. That I had something special.”

Your life has changed a lot, are you still the same person?

“My life has changed so much in the past few months, perhaps more than I can even realize. There are moments when I stop and think about how different everything is from just a short while ago. Football is taking me to places I dreamed of, but inside I’m still the same boy.

“But I know what my ambitions are. And perhaps that’s exactly what helps me not lose myself, even when everything changes. I try to enjoy every moment, without forgetting where I started from and all the people who have accompanied me to this point.”

Finally, clear something up: how do you say your name?

“My name is Cisse, without an accent. My name can be read any way, but it’s pronounced ‘Sisse’. I even had to send a voice message to the commentators!”

Strategy buys 1,036 Bitcoin as BTC holdings climb to 850,364

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Strategy bought $81.9 million in Bitcoin last week while spending $188.3 million repurchasing STRC preferred shares, according to its latest SEC filing.

Strategy bought 1,036 Bitcoin for $81.9 million last week, bringing its holdings to 850,364 BTC.

The company paid an average of $79,012 per BTC last week, according to its Monday filing with the US Securities and Exchange Commission.

Strategy said it paid a total of $64.16 billion for its Bitcoin holdings, at an average price of $75,456 per coin. Its holdings now account for roughly 4.05% of Bitcoin’s maximum 21 million supply.

Since the start of October, Strategy has spent about $1.06 billion buying back its STRC preferred shares, nearly five times the $219.7 million it spent on Bitcoin over the same period.

Strategy raised $283.8 million in net proceeds last week by selling about $2.05 million shares of tis common stock (MSTR) through its at-the-market offering program.

Strategy used $188.3 million of those proceeds to repurchase about 1.89 million STRC shares, following $208.2 million in buybacks the previous week. It spent $81.9 million on Bitcoin and allocated another $13.6 million to its USD Cash balance.

Related: Strategy shareholders approve daily dividends for four preferred stocks

Strategy’s separate US dollar reserve fell to $4.77 billion from $4.79 billion after the company drew $21.1 million to pay dividends on its preferred stock.

Its USD Cash balance rose about 10% to $146.2 million from $132.7 million.

The latest Bitcoin purchase follows Strategy’s acquisition of 811 BTC for $65.5 million the previous week.

Apple's not the only player in the foldable business

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Foldable phones are, among other things, very expensive. Prices for the book-style foldables are so high that the cheapest models start at $1,800 — with different storage options exceeding over $3,000. Thankfully, we're now at the time of year when phone prices drop, including some of the most recent releases.

We won't be seeing any discounts on the iPhone Duo; Apple doesn't really do iPhone discounts, and the Duo is already sold out until next year. But Apple isn't the only company in the foldable market, and its rivals have already been slashing prices in the aftermath of the Duo's launch. Not all of them are recent releases, but with the slow pace of phone upgrades these days, that's not necessarily a bad thing. Especially if it means saving a bunch of cash.

So if you're in the market for a foldable phone, and aren't choosy about which brand you buy, here are the best early Black Friday foldable phone deals available right now.

If you're looking for a passport-shaped foldable, with a similar look to the iPhone Duo, look no further than the Galaxy Z Fold 8. Samsung's offering has a similar look and feel, with a pair of 50MP cameras on the back. It's also thinner and lighter than the iPhone, with a more colorful anti-glare display. However, it lacks some features like stylus support and proper dust resistance.This isn't the cheapest we've seen the Z Fold 8 go for, but it's still a generous discount from the normal price.

If you want a larger foldable, then the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra is easily one of the best foldable phones you can buy. It comes with all the same benefits as the Z Fold 8, but in a larger book-style design. It also packs a more powerful 200MP camera, a telephoto lens and a larger 5,000 mAh battery.

The Pixel 11 Pro Fold isn't the most advanced foldable, especially in terms of the display crease and battery life, but it does combine all the benefits of a folding device with the things that make a Pixel great. This means you get a large, bright display, some of the best AI features of any phone, and good cameras. Plus early access to all the latest Android updates as they happen.

If you want a cheap foldable in a more compact form, then the Motorola Razr (2025) is one of your best options. The newer Razr 2026 model is not on sale right now, but the 2025 model does offer a lot. You get a bright 6.9-inch display, a suite of AI features, a 120Hz refresh rate on both screens and a large cover display that can run just about any Android app you like.

If you want something a lot more capable, the 2025 Razr Ultra is almost half price ahead of Black Friday. This comes with the same 6.9-inch display as the standard Razr, but ups the refresh rate all the way to a crazy 165Hz. It also comes with 512GB storage as standard, 16GB of RAM, a pair of 50MP main cameras, a 50MP selfie camera and a whopping 4,500 nits of display brightness.

TRON Network Integrates with Backpack Wallet, Expanding Access to Its Multichain Ecosystem

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Geneva, Switzerland, November 9, 2026 — TRON DAO, the community-governed DAO dedicated to accelerating the decentralization of the internet through blockchain technology and decentralized applications (dApps), today announced the integration of the TRON network with Backpack Wallet. The integration brings TRON’s blockchain ecosystem directly to Backpack users, enabling them to send, receive, bridge, and manage TRON-based digital assets through Backpack’s mobile app and browser extension, all while maintaining full self-custody.

Through the integration, Backpack users will be able to send, receive, and hold TRON-based digital assets, including USDT (TRC-20), directly within their wallet while maintaining full self-custody. Users will also be able to bridge assets between TRON and other supported blockchains, explore decentralized finance applications and dApps across the TRON ecosystem, including Sun.io, and manage their TRON assets alongside holdings from other supported networks within a single multichain wallet experience.

This integration follows the recent listing of TRX for spot trading and perpetual contracts on Backpack Exchange. The listings expanded market access to TRON’s native utility token, allowing users in eligible jurisdictions to trade TRX through both spot and perpetual markets on Backpack’s trading platform. Together, these developments establish a foundation for deeper ecosystem connectivity, bringing TRON’s blockchain infrastructure and digital assets to Backpack users across both its wallet and exchange products.

The collaboration reinforces TRON’s commitment to expanding global access to blockchain infrastructure, stablecoin payments, and decentralized finance, while advancing its mission to make digital assets more accessible worldwide.

TRON DAO is a community-governed DAO dedicated to accelerating the decentralization of the internet via blockchain technology and dApps.

Founded in September 2017, the TRON blockchain has experienced significant growth since its MainNet launch in May 2018. Today, TRON hosts the largest circulating supply of USD Tether (USDT) stablecoin, which currently exceeds $94 billion. As of November 2026, the TRON blockchain has recorded over 412 million in total user accounts, more than 16 billion in total transactions, and over $27 billion in total value locked (TVL), based on TRONSCAN. Recognized as the global settlement layer for stablecoin transactions and everyday purchases with proven success, TRON is “Moving Trillions, Empowering Billions.”
