10 Tiny Gear Upgrades That Make a Huge Difference

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

When evaluating gear to bring on trips, I do tend to be fairly stingy with weight. For something to make it into my backpack, it needs to add a level of enjoyment to my hike that surpasses the penalty I’ll feel on my back from the item’s weight.

Over the years, I’ve discovered a handful of small, relatively inexpensive upgrades to my gear that have made a massive difference in the ease of a day on trail. While not strictly necessary, I would suffer the weight penalty for each of the below items on almost any trail.

For years, I complained that the worst chore at camp was blowing up my sleeping pad. Most of my backpacking was done at or above 10,000 feet, and by the time my pad was inflated, my vision would be blurring and I would feel lightheaded and terrible.

Plus, blowing a bunch of moist air into my sleeping pad would quickly lead to mold growth. Within a month, I could see little spots growing inside the pad when I held it up to the light.

The last thing I want to do when I get to camp is blow up a sleeping pad. Photo via Katie Jackson

A tiny, rechargeable pump like the Flextail or — my personal favorite — the Alpenblow Lux weighs just a few ounces and will inflate my pad for me in under a minute. It saves me from losing my breath each night and extends the life of the pad. Mostly, though, it makes each night at camp significantly less annoying.

For years, I hiked with two Smartwater bottles in my side pocket — one for clean water and one for dirty water. Eventually, I got pretty disgusted with constantly reusing a type of plastic not intended for reuse. The thin walls of a single-use bottle get permanently crinkled, trap grime in the deep thread grooves, and shed inordinate amounts of microplastic into drinking water.

The Cnoc ThruBottle is what I currently reach for when I want an exact Smartwater replacement but with different materials. Photo via Katie Jackson

However, I unfortunately love the functionality of the Smartwater bottle, so any replacement I considered needed to behave similarly without adding a ton of weight. Brands like Mazama Designs, Igneous Gear, and CNOC have changed the game with the M!GO bottle, NOBO bottle, and ThruBottle, respectively. All three are durable, squeezable, and are compatible with the most popular backcountry filters, which has let me keep my current “one dirty, one clean” water bottle system on the trail.

Hear me out, because this one’s weird. Adopting the Trailbrush has actually lowered the weight in my pack.

I really struggle with brushing my teeth while hiking. I find that the mental exertion of getting from Point A to Point B leaves nothing in the tank. By the time I get into camp, set up my shelter, and cook dinner, the absolute last thing I want to do is have a bad time brushing my teeth in the woods without running water. My dental hygiene on trail was … not great.

I hate not being able to rinse my toothbrush with abundant running water so much. Photo via Katie Jackson

The Trailbrush is a little toothbrush head that attaches to a long-handled spork, which I found really reduced the friction in my nighttime routine.

Is it a super enjoyable toothbrush replacement? No. But having it right there when I finish dinner has increased my toothbrushing by about 1,000%. I frequently find myself in the comments of posts defending this little accessory.

If you think it sounds silly, then it’s probably not for you, but if you end each trail worried about how infrequently you actually brushed your teeth, I cannot recommend this little Trailbrush enough.

I’ve spent so long searching for the perfect rain jacket, and I’ve finally been forced to accept that what I want doesn’t exist in jacket form. No jacket out there will be genuinely waterproof, allow airflow, keep me warm without causing me to overheat, and stay effective for thousands of miles. You know what does do all those things? An umbrella.

I first used an umbrella on the CDT for sun protection down in New Mexico, but quickly realized how useful it was during rain and hail storms. Instead of sending it home at the end of the desert as planned, I carried it all the way to Canada. I’ll be carrying an umbrella on any hike calling for rain gear from here on; it’s too wildly practical and easy to pass up.

I think an umbrella has dramatically changed the way I view rain on trails. Photo via Katie Jackson

For the CDT, I used a Gossamer Gear umbrella and had no complaints. However, I have my eye on Hyperlite Mountain Gear’s umbrella, as I think the vents around the top would help stabilize it during heavy rain.

The day I let go of the hold Crocs had on me was the day I started finding more value in my camp shoes.

If Crocs work for you, that’s great. They make sense on paper: waterproof, fairly lightweight, and sturdy enough for a river crossing. But I got pretty fed up with how bulky they were in my pack compared to the use I got out of them.

I’ve made the switch to Xero Shoes Z-Trail EV Sandals and have been loving them. They weigh much less than a pair of Crocs, can still be worn with socks on, and take up about a fourth of the space in my pack.

Gone are the days of shoving my Crocs into my bear can to make them fit on my pack. Photo via Katie Jackson

The bottom still has great grip for those who sometimes hike in camp shoes. They’re so minimalist that I don’t feel bad carrying them even if I only use them around my tent.

Finding your own personal hygiene balance on a thru-hike comes down to personal preferences. I don’t carry deodorant or a lot of other things I use at home to stay fresh and clean; I’ve accepted that thru-hiking is nasty and I will probably just look and smell nasty while doing it.

I like strapping it to the front of my pack, since I’m more likely to grab it if I don’t have to take my pack off to reach it. Photo via Katie Jackson

However, the Kula Cloth is one thing I always bring, if only to keep myself from smelling like pee all the time. A Kula Cloth is a reusable, antimicrobial pee cloth that is an eco-friendly replacement for traditional toilet paper in the backcountry (for pee only! Not for #2!).

Before I started using one, peeing on trail meant either packing out toilet paper or a quasi-effective drip dry. The Kula Cloth snaps to the outside of my pack, where it dries in the sun throughout the day. It carries no odor, thanks to the silver thread woven into it. For such a tiny, lightweight addition to my pack, the boost in cleanliness is astronomical.

For the low, low price of mere pennies, you can have the most waterproof bag on the market.

I’ve spent hundreds of miles hiking with a pack cover. The trouble with this setup is that in prolonged, heavy rain, water still seeps into the pack through the unprotected back panel. Sick of I’ve arriving at camp with wet gear despite faithfully using a pack cover, I finally made the switch to a trash compactor bag.

I like not having to worry about buying the right size pack cover for my pack; the same trash compactor bag has taken me through almost the entire CDT and is still going strong. Photo via Katie Jackson

Instead of covering the outside of your pack, you line the inside of your backpack with a heavy-duty trash compactor bag before packing your gear. Everything critical, especially your sleeping quilt and down jacket, goes right inside that plastic liner. When a downpour hits, you don’t need to stop and add anything to your pack to keep your gear dry.

It weighs a fraction of an ounce, costs practically nothing, and guarantees your gear stays dry even if your entire pack gets soaked.

Sometimes, when hiking, I get very fixated on the task at hand and struggle to prioritize food and water breaks. If grabbing a snack means I have to stop, take my pack off, and dig around inside, I often just ignore the hunger and keep hiking until I’m way too crabby and ravenous to continue.

Through some trial and error, I’ve found that the most practical way around this is to keep all my daily snacks and drink powders in a fanny pack so I can access what I want without stopping.

That bad boy can fit a lot of snacks. Photo by Will Standish

Not only does having everything on-hand help me keep up with important tasks (eating, drinking, reapplying sunscreen, etc.), but it also shifts some of my gear weight to the front, which helps me with balance and back pain.

I use the 2.5L Trailmix Switchpack from Chicken Tramper Ultralight Gear, but any large chest pouch, side bag, or similarly-sized fanny pack will function just as well.

A pillow doesn’t make it into my thru-hiking gear list, mostly because I think a lot of the super light and super small options are also super uncomfortable. When pack weight and space are at a premium, a puffy jacket shoved into its own pocket works just fine for me as a pillow.

However, I can’t lie and pretend that a bulky, slightly heavier pillow doesn’t dramatically improve my sleep quality when I’m willing to just carry it along with me. I prefer pillows with foam inside because they work more like a pillow at home and less like an air-filled dry bag. The NEMO Fillo is a nice hybrid foam-and-inflatable option.

If you’re bothered by the texture of your pillow against your cheek, you can still throw a nice fleece on top to act as a makeshift pillowcase. Photo via Katie Jackson

This obviously won’t work for every trip, but I’ve found a lot of value recently in intentionally infusing fun into my outdoor adventures. Sometimes I’ll bring a craft, like my current knitting project. Other times, I reach for an inflatable pool floatie or, if I’m feeling extra strong that day, my inflatable paddle board.

I’ve even packed an extra swimsuit for a trail that took me right up by the ocean. Photo via Katie Jackson

Anything that gives me a good excuse to stop hiking for a bit and really sit in my surroundings has enhanced my hikes. Other good ideas for this category: a physical book, a watercolor set, a Cornhole set, a board game, or some fairy lights for your tent.

These things force me to slow down and have fun on trips when there’s no need to grind from dusk to dawn to hit a certain mileage goal.

This website contains affiliate links, which means The Trek may receive a percentage of any product or service you purchase using the links in the articles or advertisements. The buyer pays the same price as they would otherwise, and your purchase helps to support The Trek's ongoing goal to serve you quality backpacking advice and information. Thanks for your support!

To learn more, please visit the About This Site page.

I'm Katie, a thru-hiker and ultrarunner based in Colorado. After thru-hiking the Colorado Trail, John Muir Trail, Continental Divide Trail, and Long Trail in back-to-back years, I'm looking forward to a little time of rest, relaxation, and hanging out with my cat. Or, who knows, maybe the AT. No one has ever accused me of being good at sitting still.

Notify me via e-mail if anyone answers my comment.

PlanNet Sells The Dream Of A Travel Business—But 84% Of Its Representatives Earned No Commission In 2025

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

I’d never heard of PlanNet Marketing until a reader sent me its earnings numbers. I got started down a rabbit hole. They sell travel. Or, rather, they sell people on the idea of selling travel while signing up other people to sell travel. But for all of 2025, 83.96% of PlanNet representatives earned no commission or override at all. My first reaction was: this looks awfully like a pyramid scheme. It’s a multi-level marketing business selling access to a travel agency opportunity. And the more I read, the less comfortable I became with the way that opportunity is pitched to people who need money. Two companies, two ways to make money PlanNet was launched in 2015 by founder Donald Bradley. It works with InteleTravel, a host travel agency. These are separate businesses: . InteleTravel provides the travel agency operation. Advisors get booking tools, training and supplier access. They can sell actual trips and receive a share of the commission. PlanNet sells access to that opportunity. Its representatives earn money selling InteleTravel agency subscriptions and building networks of other representatives who sell them. InteleTravel reported $1.52 billion in 2025 travel sales and 140,000 independent contractors. A conventional host agency charges fees or keeps part of an advisor’s commission in exchange for infrastructure and support. But the salient feature of PlanNet is the multilevel compensation for selling agency access. You can be an InteleTravel advisor without joining the paid PlanNet representative program. However, InteleTravel’s sign-up page routes enrollment exclusively through PlanNet. Here’s the current U.S. pricing from InteleTravel’s benefits page and PlanNet’s current policies and compensation plan. Using monthly billing, doing both costs $59.90 a month, or $718.80 over twelve months, before joining fees or other business expenses. Program    Joining fee    Recurring fee InteleTravel advisor    $179.99    $39.95 monthly PlanNet representative    $19.95    $19.95 monthly; $191.52 annual option Here’s a video describing their commission structure: InteleTravel advertises paying advisors 70%–80% of supplier commissions. A hypothetical $4,000 commissionable booking at a 10% supplier commission produces $400 so an advisor receiving 70% gets $280. You can make a living with enough customers and bookings. What PlanNet Pays For PlanNet’s compensation plan pays for something different: Sell an InteleTravel agency subscription and receive $50. Personally sponsor a PlanNet representative who makes an agency sale and receive a $25 matching commission. A qualifying Gold Builder receives a $10 bonus on agency sales in their network, down to the next Gold Builder. Qualifying representatives receive $4 monthly per active agency subscription in their compensation matrix, potentially through nine levels. At least two active personal agency sales are required to qualify. Gold Builder requires nine active personal agency sales. Higher ranks and bonuses depend on building larger qualifying organizations. The plan also includes rings and a Rolex award. What triggers those payments is selling agency subscriptions. It’s not selling travel, it’s selling the ability to sell travel. Someone can keep paying for an agency, sell no travel, and still generate a monthly payment for people above them. Recruiting another paying participant turns out to be valuable to the recruiter even when the opportunity turns out to be worthless to the person recruited. And friends and relatives become prospective customers (marks) for the business opportunity. The Federal Trade Commission says having a real product isn’t enough to make a multilevel marketing business legal. It’s how the compensation and incentives work that matters. Poor earnings on their own doesn’t mean it’s a pyramid scheme. But the economics here certainly look like one to me. The Earnings Disclosure Is Brutal Here is the full 2025 rank distribution: Rank    Share of active representatives    Average annualized earnings Representative    92.52%    $38.15 Bronze Builder    3.48%    $752.71 Silver Builder    1.34%    $2,497.37 Gold Builder    2.17%    $4,865.04 One-Star Director    0.33%    $28,517.55 Two-Star Director    0.06%    $76,733.94 Three-Star Director    0.06%    $127,496.47 Four-Star Director    0.02%    $255,404.52 Five-Star Director    0.01%    $517,001.78 Six-Star Director    0.01%    $1,088,633.37 This is before expenses. The $38.15 base rank average doesn’t cover two months of PlanNet’s monthly dues. In a February 2026 compliance post, PlanNet describes the disclosure as a “strong marketing tool.” It says “a small amount of extra effort” to reach Bronze Builder raises the average earnings, then points to the higher averages at Silver and Gold. The Target Market (‘Victims’) In a company-published recap of a 2022 Atlanta event, a representative describes helping people “move to the other side of the poverty line.” Another invokes the opportunity to “change your bloodline’s finances.” Here’s a sample, A 2024 company profile celebrates a single mother of five who says she had been living paycheck to paycheck before finding the opportunity. The Guardian reported recruitment aimed at mothers on maternity leave and women with chronic illnesses. The Sun’s August 2025 investigation described pressure to recruit and guilt about quitting. It also reported celebrity Jess Wright telling people unable to afford to “invest into your future, then this is exactly why you need this travel business”. Someone’s inability to afford a fee is being turned into an argument for paying it. But being shown the celebrity’s lifestyle tells you very little about your own likely return. It’s also not clear whether people actually receive the requisite training to sell travel, understand cancellation terms, handle client money, know the products, and fix problems. You’re Guaranteed A Profit! InteleTravel offers a first-year profit guarantee. It counts both earnings and the value of travel savings and privileges for the advisor. It covers fees paid to InteleTravel not all expenses or PlanNet fees. Eligibility includes specified training, a qualifying cruise or vacation-package sale, a completed hotel stay booking each quarter, and purchasing business cards within the first 90 days. The claim must be made within 30 days after the first anniversary. PlanNet’s policies: Prohibit selling or transferring the position you buy If you don’t keep paying, you lose your downline commissions You can’t make negative remarks about the company, its compensation plan or leadership. It’s An Old Business Model This isn’t an original business model. Evolution Travel and Archer Travel is another marketing network paired with a host agency. Evolution’s compensation plan combines travel commissions with sponsor payments and network ranks. It raises the same question about whether a participant is building a customer base for travel or a network of paying agents. YourTravelBiz, or YTB: was alleged to be a pyramid scheme built around selling travel websites. A 2009 settlement required $1 million in penalties, costs and restitution. The company’s securities filing says the settlement involved no admission or court findings of wrongdoing. One executive named in that filing was Andrew Cauthen, the Andy Cauthen whom PlanNet honored on his retirement as president in January 2026. WorldVentures: a travel club version of multilevel marketing. Its U.S. disclosure from July 2018 – June 2019 reported that 85.4% of representatives earned no commission. Norway’s regulator found it to be an illegal pyramid system. I’ve long argued that a good travel agent’s expertise has real value. Someone who knows a destination, understands a complicated itinerary or takes responsibility when things go wrong can earn their commission many times over. I don’t see how I’d ever want to work with a PlanetNet agent, and that’s a problem. Top travel advisors can still do well but this is not the broad-based business it once was because of a new thing called the internet. YourTravelBiz, or YTB: was alleged to be a pyramid scheme built around selling travel websites. A 2009 settlement required $1 million in penalties, costs and restitution. The company’s securities filing says the settlement involved no admission or court findings of wrongdoing. One executive named in that filing was Andrew Cauthen, the Andy Cauthen whom PlanNet honored on his retirement as president in January 2026. WorldVentures: a travel club version of multilevel marketing. Its U.S. disclosure from July 2018 – June 2019 reported that 85.4% of representatives earned no commission. Norway’s regulator found it to be an illegal pyramid system. I’ve long argued that a good travel agent’s expertise has real value. Someone who knows a destination, understands a complicated itinerary or takes responsibility when things go wrong can earn their commission many times over. I don’t see how I’d ever want to work with a PlanetNet agent, and that’s a problem. Top travel advisors can still do well but this is not the broad-based business it once was because of a new thing called the internet. Topics on this pageInteleTravel.comMulti-level marketingPlannet Marketing Inc.PlanNet Sells The Dream Of A Travel BusinessPyramid schemeAtlantaFederal Trade CommissionRolex+3 more More From View from the Wing

PlanNet’s compensation plan pays for something different:

Gold Builder requires nine active personal agency sales. Higher ranks and bonuses depend on building larger qualifying organizations. The plan also includes rings and a Rolex award.

What triggers those payments is selling agency subscriptions. It’s not selling travel, it’s selling the ability to sell travel. Someone can keep paying for an agency, sell no travel, and still generate a monthly payment for people above them.

Recruiting another paying participant turns out to be valuable to the recruiter even when the opportunity turns out to be worthless to the person recruited. And friends and relatives become prospective customers (marks) for the business opportunity.

The Federal Trade Commission says having a real product isn’t enough to make a multilevel marketing business legal. It’s how the compensation and incentives work that matters. Poor earnings on their own doesn’t mean it’s a pyramid scheme. But the economics here certainly look like one to me.

Here is the full 2025 rank distribution: Rank    Share of active representatives    Average annualized earnings Representative    92.52%    $38.15 Bronze Builder    3.48%    $752.71 Silver Builder    1.34%    $2,497.37 Gold Builder    2.17%    $4,865.04 One-Star Director    0.33%    $28,517.55 Two-Star Director    0.06%    $76,733.94 Three-Star Director    0.06%    $127,496.47 Four-Star Director    0.02%    $255,404.52 Five-Star Director    0.01%    $517,001.78 Six-Star Director    0.01%    $1,088,633.37 This is before expenses. The $38.15 base rank average doesn’t cover two months of PlanNet’s monthly dues. In a February 2026 compliance post, PlanNet describes the disclosure as a “strong marketing tool.” It says “a small amount of extra effort” to reach Bronze Builder raises the average earnings, then points to the higher averages at Silver and Gold. It covers fees paid to InteleTravel not all expenses or PlanNet fees. Eligibility includes specified training, a qualifying cruise or vacation-package sale, a completed hotel stay booking each quarter, and purchasing business cards within the first 90 days. The claim must be made within 30 days after the first anniversary. Chelsea news: Dermot Gallagher backs double Blues VAR snub versus Sunderland

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

By Darren Plant | 09 Nov 2026 13:02

Former Premier League referee Dermot Gallagher has backed VAR over the decisions that went against Chelsea during Sunday's game at Sunderland.

The Black Cats recorded a deserved 1-0 victory courtesy of Enzo Le Fee's first-half penalty at the Stadium of Light.

However, the decision to award Sunderland a spot kick has been questioned, with replays suggesting that Brian Brobbey fouled Levi Colwill prior to being brought down in the penalty area.

Meanwhile, Cole Palmer could have arguably won a spot kick for Chelsea at the other end having been upended by a combination of Luke O'Nien and Jules Ahoka.

Xabi Alonso's side were not given the decision because any infringement was deemed to have started or taken place outside of the penalty area.

Speaking on 'Ref Watch' on Sky Sports News, Gallagher claimed that Colwill could have no complaints over the Sunderland penalty decision.

He said: "They are both grabbing. It starts outside the area, the referee does not take sides, but once Brobbey gets in front of him and Colwill does bot let go, that is when he falls foul of the penalty.

"Once he is in front, he is in the ascendancy and Colwill drags him to the ground, so I think penalty."

When asked on the process involving Palmer, O'Nien and Ahoka, Gallagher added: "The referee gives a foul. That is what he gives.

"O'Nien completes the action when he is in the box. Once he gives the first foul, that is it. What happens afterwards, he can only take action if he deems there is serious foul play.

"That is what he fed back. What he has to do is, when he is talking to VAR, he has to say who he thinks is the player [committing the foul].

"It is O'Nien's boot in the penalty area. The argument is that if he is on the line, it is a penalty. But it is O'Nien's boot; you can't see the ball.

"O'Nien goes second. VAR said Ahoka went first and he is the first offender. They didn't stop the game. The game went on."

Alonso refused to criticise the officials in his post-match press conference, instead choosing to focus on the poor performance from his team.

However, during the match, the Spaniard appeared furious about Sunderland going unpunished over the Palmer incident.

Why Do Football Clubs Let Players Leave for Free?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Why would a club let a valuable player walk out the door for nothing?

Because sometimes nothing is the best offer on the table.

Football clubs may let a player leave for free when the cost or risk of a new contract outweighs the value of keeping him.

A club may also prefer one final season of sporting contribution to a modest transfer fee. Sometimes the player chooses not to renew, while the signing club can still face wages, bonuses and agent fees.

So the word “free” does a lot of heavy lifting in football.

It is the only price tag in the sport that tells you what the selling club gets, and nothing about what the buying club pays.

This guide explains the topic for senior professional football, with examples drawn from English football.

Where FIFA rules and domestic rules part ways, we say so without hesitation.

A free transfer is a move in which no transfer fee is paid to the previous club, commonly because the player’s contract has expired.

When we’re talking about a free agent, we’re talking about an unattached player.

“Free” describes the absence of a transfer fee here. It does not remove wages, signing costs or any compensation that rules may still require.

The word also gets stretched quite a bit.

A contract that runs out on its end date is a free transfer. A deal that both sides agree to end early, called mutual termination, is something else. So is a release, where a club lets a player go while he is still under contract.

Headlines rarely make the distinction, but the rulebook does.

You can compare free signings with fee-paying moves in our confirmed football transfers database.

No sporting director wakes up planning to lose a player for nothing.

It happens when every other option looks worse, which in football is more often than fans like to believe.

Unless a club explains its motive in public (and clubs rarely do), read them as general reasoning, not as the known story behind any specific deal.

A renewal is rarely a copy of the old deal.

It usually comes with a raise, a signing bonus and a fresh agent fee. If the club doubts the player will be worth that money in year three or four, letting the contract expire is the cheaper mistake.

A lengthy contract for an older player can increase a club’s exposure to injury or declining performance.

The club pays the full salary whether the player stays fit or not, and many clubs would rather stop early.

A player who no longer fits still collects his wages, and those wages still count against the club’s spending limits. Loyalty does not have its own line in the accounts.

Sometimes a club lets a contract run out on purpose.

If the best offer is modest, one more season of goals, a title push or a cup run can be worth more than the money.

Fans may call it a blunder in January. By May, some of them call it a trophy.

A club cannot force a renewal and it cannot force a sale either.

If the player turns down every new contract and every move his club agrees, the deal simply runs out. He leaves for free, usually with a better offer already waiting somewhere else.

A short contract gives a club less long-term wage exposure and gives a player an earlier chance to renegotiate or move. It also offers less security to the player and less protection of resale value to the club. The right length depends on performance risk, bargaining power and each side’s plans.

For a club, a two-year deal limits the damage if a signing flops. The price is that the club may be shopping for a replacement sooner than planned.

It protects resale value, because any buyer must pay to take over the years still left. But it turns into an anchor if the player’s form drops, since the wages keep coming whether he plays or not.

Short contracts and free transfers are related, but they are not the same decision.

A short deal moves the end date closer. What happens on that date is a separate argument, usually held in a room full of agents.

Every contract has a clock on it, and the clock usually works for the player.

As a contract approaches expiry, a club will generally have less leverage in transfer negotiations, because the player may become available without a fee.

Is there a formula for how fast that value falls?

Form, demand, injuries and competition among interested clubs can all affect the fee available.

For clubs hoping to recover a transfer fee, an earlier sale or a contract extension may improve their position.

Keeping the player until expiry can still be the better sporting decision.

A player’s value and the cost of signing him are not necessarily the same thing. Transfer fees attract most of the headlines, while wages, signing bonuses and agent payments can be just as important to a club’s finances. Transfer speculation also extends beyond traditional football reporting, with discussions appearing on social media and platforms such as crypto betting site Sportbet.one, although these conversations should not be confused with verified transfer fees or the actual financial terms of a deal.

Before 1995, a player at the end of his contract was not really free.

His old club could still demand a fee before letting him go, and Jean-Marc Bosman, a Belgian midfielder, found that out in 1990.

He was nearing the end of his contract with RFC Liège and rejected a new offer that would have cut his salary by about 75%. He wanted to join French club Dunkerque instead.

The two clubs reached a conditional transfer agreement, but Liège doubted Dunkerque’s ability to pay and never requested the transfer certificate the move required. The deal fell through, and Liège suspended him.

He won five years later, and thousands of players have earned more from his case than he ever did.

The Bosman ruling of December 15, 1995 stopped clubs from demanding a fee for an out-of-contract player moving to a club in another EU member state.

It did not end every form of post-contract compensation worldwide, whatever the legend says.

When it comes to signings, FIFA’s rules set the timing.

Under article 18(3) of the FIFA Regulations on the Status and Transfer of Players (July 2025 edition), a player may sign with a new club once his contract has expired or is due to expire within six months.

The new club must first tell the current club in writing.

The six months count back from the real end date, so the magic month is not always January.

And signing a future contract is not the same as being registered to play.

Registration still depends on the transfer window and domestic rules.

The signing club may pay wages, a signing-on bonus and agent fees, even when no transfer fee goes to the previous club.

Compare the full contract period and separate one-off payments from annual pay before judging which deal costs less.

Illustrative five-year signing costs. Original SoccerNews editorial example using hypothetical figures, not a real transfer or market average.

Calculation: fee-paying = £20m + (5 × £3m) + £1m + £2m = £38m. Out-of-contract = £0 + (5 × £5m) + £5m + £3m = £33m. Annual wages are counted once, through the five-year total. Assumptions: both players complete five full years; wages are fixed gross amounts; bonuses and agent fees are paid by the signing club and counted once. Excludes employer payroll charges, taxes, performance bonuses, financing, add-ons, training compensation, resale income and early termination. These are nominal commitments, not cash schedules or accounting expenses.

In this hypothetical example, the free signing costs £33m over five years, compared with £38m for the fee-paying signing.

Higher wages and signing costs absorb much of the saved fee. Different contract terms could reverse the result.

In other words, the costs shift between transfer fees, wages and other signing expenses.

Which brings us to the most confusing pair of words in football. A free agent is the player. A football agent is the representative who negotiates for him, and sometimes for a club. Only one of those job titles contains the word “free.” Neither of them works for free.

Premier League clubs reported £460.3 million in payments to football agents for the period from February 4, 2025 to February 2, 2026, according to The FA.

The figure covers reported agent payments across different types of transactions, including some agreed before the reporting period. It should not be read as the cost of free transfers alone.

Premier League Squad Cost Ratio (SCR) rules in force for 2026/27 assess defined squad costs against relevant revenue and player-sale results.

The 85% Green Threshold operates alongside additional allowance and a Red Threshold, so crossing 85% does not automatically trigger a points deduction. Free signings still create relevant costs through pay and other deal expenses. (Premier League)

In plain terms, every pound spent on the squad now fights for the same space.

Squad costs include wages, agent fees and transfer fees spread over time, a process called amortization. Since the Premier League’s five-year rule for new or extended contracts, a fee can be spread over five years at most.

So a £20m fee on a five-year deal adds £4m a year to the accounts (£20m ÷ 5). This assumes straight-line accounting and no early sale or write-down. It is an accounting charge, not the cash payment, which can follow a different schedule.

In this example, an out-of-contract signing avoids the £4m annual amortization charge linked to the hypothetical £20m transfer fee. The club must still account for the player’s wages and any other signing or registration costs. That is the part of “free” the unveiling photo never shows.

You can follow how this plays out window by window in our Premier League transfers coverage.

A club that lets a player walk is not always being careless.

It weighs sporting value against the price of a renewal, and the player gets a vote too. For the signing club, “free” generally means no transfer fee to the previous club, not a cost-free deal.

Under FIFA’s July 2025 regulations, a professional may conclude a contract with another club when his current contract has expired or is due to expire within six months. The approaching club must first inform the current club in writing. Domestic rules and the regulatory edition in force must also be checked.

Some out-of-contract players can be registered outside a registration period under specified exceptions. Being unattached does not create an unlimited right to register at any time. The date the contract ended, association rules and competition eligibility all matter.

No transfer fee does not always mean no compensation. Training-compensation rules can apply to qualifying young players, and domestic schemes may also apply. Contract expiry is different from early termination, which can raise other compensation questions.

It is a one-off payment a club makes to a player for agreeing to join. A signing-on bonus may be substantial in a free transfer, but the amount depends on the negotiation.

No. A club can accept an offer, but the player must agree personal terms with the buyer. If he says no, he stays until his contract ends.

Rule summaries are based on the Bosman judgment (C-415/93), the FIFA Regulations on the Status and Transfer of Players (July 2025 edition), Premier League statements on squad-cost rules and amortization, and The FA’s 2025-26 agent-payment report. The cost table is an original SoccerNews example with hypothetical figures. Rules change, so check the edition in force on the date you read this. Browse more football explainers.

Kim Kardashian's shocking advent calendar is the perfect metaphor for 2026

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Christmas is coming, which means annoying music in shops, an inevitable lack of snow, and Kim Kardashian charging $148 for the contents of her bathroom drawer.

Kim's 'SKIMS Accessories Advent Calendar' has achieved that rare festive miracle: making a chocolate-based advent calendar look like a sound financial investment. Because what we have here, for a cool $148/£158, is twelve days of tat. Hair ties. Scrunchies. Bobby pins. Keychains. A squishy toy. Something called a 'bra elastic headband'. It's essentially the contents of a lost property box.

Unsurprisingly, the internet is having a field day. A viral TikTok unboxing has attracted tens of millions of views, with its creator becoming visibly more exasperated as each compartment reveals another object you could reasonably expect to find down the back of a sofa, culminating in the grand finale: a furry compact mirror keychain.

It's worth saying that from a branding packaging design perspective, it's almost impressive. It's a nice-looking object with pretty illustrations, and the SKIMS wordmark is striking in a bold, festive red. But there's something beautifully 2026 about taking a handful of mundane objects, putting them in expensive-looking packaging and asking people to cough up the cost of an actual spa day. Like so many of this year's main characters (AI, subscription-based everything, $10 matcha lattes), it's overpriced, overhyped and underwhelming. But unlike AI, that $148 leaving your bank account definitely isn't artificial.

In response to the backlash, SKIMS has emailed customers who purchased the advent calendar: “We wanted to reach out personally about your recent purchase of the Holiday 2026 advent calendar. We understand the product did not meet customers’ expectations, and we are truly sorry. We want every customer to be happy with what they receive, and we fell short this time.”

Believe it or not, this isn't the first time we've seen a celebrity's brand take a hit as a result of an underwhelming advent calendar. Back in 2017, popular vlogger Zoella faced backlash for her own calendar. Like Kim's it contained only twelve doors, all of which were filled with items that might be found down the back of a sofa. Unlike Kim's, it was a mere $60. Inflation, eh?

If you need help restoring your faith in the festive season, check out the magical Disney Holiday Short, which is moving people to tears.
